<meta>This page begins the dedication of a scholarly edition of the works of Emperor Julian, addressed by the Basque scholar Pierre Martini to Cardinal Odet de Coligny.</meta>

<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<sig>†</sig>

### TO ODET
# DE COLIGNY
## CARDINAL OF CHÂTIL-
## LON, PIERRE MARTINI
### of Morentin in Navarre,

->Greetings.<-

<image-desc>A decorative floriated woodcut drop cap 'S' featuring scrolling vines and floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
I CERTAINLY hope (most distinguished Cardinal) that there will be no one who is rightly angry with me if I publish the works <note>original: "monumenta," referring here to literary remains or written works</note> of the Emperor Julian <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 CE), the last pagan Roman emperor, who attempted to suppress Christianity in favor of Neoplatonic Hellenism.</note> for the general public. For although I grant that the crimes of impiety and tyranny, in which that man miserably entangled himself, are wicked and worthy of hatred: the brightest lights of eloquence and learning certainly deserve nothing, I think, that would justify them being extinguished along with his vices. Otherwise, let Demosthenes, Cicero, Homer, Virgil, Plato, Aristotle, and in a word all authors of elegant and refined literature—poets, orators, philosophers—indeed, let all the arts of culture <note>original: "humanitatis," the "studia humanitatis" or liberal arts</note> and wisdom be removed from our midst, simply because they were handed down almost entirely by superstitious or impious men. If that were to happen, what kind of society, I ask, would we maintain today—

<summary>In this dedication, Pierre Martini defends his decision to publish the writings of the controversial "Apostate" Emperor Julian, arguing that Julian's literary and intellectual merits should be preserved despite his religious failings.</summary>
<keywords>Odet de Coligny, Pierre Martini, Julian the Apostate, classical education, eloquence, humanism, censorship, liberal arts</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Pierre Martini to Odet de Coligny, justifying the publication of works by Julian the Apostate. It follows the previous page’s argument that even if an author is impious, their intellectual contributions and the historical lessons of their life are valuable.</meta>

->**E P I S T O L A .**<-
<meta>catchword: A-</meta>

maintain? But among the Latin authors, Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BCE), whose poem "On the Nature of Things" promoted Epicureanism and was often viewed as atheistic by later Christians.</note> is read, and among the Greeks, Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher who famously wrote "Against the Christians."</note> and Lucian <note>A Greek satirist known for mocking both traditional religion and early Christianity.</note> are published and read in both Greek and Latin; and yet I believe there is no one who complains that the works <note>original: "monumenta"</note> of these men still exist. For it pertains primarily to the benefit of history and to the knowledge of antiquity not only to know that such men once lived, but also to learn who they were and what they were like. But, you might say, they were impious. So be it, since even their examples are useful for deterring men and calling them back from impiety; hence that unhappy Theseus <note>In Greek mythology, Theseus was a legendary king of Athens; Martini refers here to his eternal punishment in the underworld as described by Virgil.</note> cries out in the underworld as an example of his own misery, just as it is written in Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book 6:

> Be warned, learn justice, and do not despise the gods. <note>original: "Discite justitiam moniti, & non temnere divos."</note>

Furthermore, the history of Julian is worthy of a certain special attention and consideration. In him were extraordinary gifts of nature and character, great and almost singular virtue in administering the affairs of war and peace; added to these many good qualities was a knowledge of true piety <note>Referring to Julian's Christian upbringing before his later rejection of the faith.</note>, adorned even by ample testimonies of his life. But the man, burning with a desire for power and captured by the deceits and arts of magic, renounced Christ; and having attained the supreme power of the empire, he most cruelly harassed the Christians: he banned them from schools, removed them from magistracies, despoiled them of their goods, a- <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "A-" suggests the next word is "Atque" (And) or another word beginning with A.</note>

<summary>Martini defends the study of Julian the Apostate by comparing him to other "impious" classical authors whose works are still valued for their historical and moral lessons. He acknowledges Julian's early Christian education but highlights how his lust for power led to his apostasy and persecution of the Church.</summary>

<keywords>Lucretius, Porphyry, Lucian, Virgil, Julian the Apostate, Christian persecution, historical education, religious apostasy, classical literature</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Julian the Apostate’s works by Pierre Martini to Odet de Coligny. The previous page concluded by noting Julian's natural talents despite his impiety. This page draws a parallel between the Church’s survival of Julian’s reign and the Trojans’ relief after the Greek siege.</meta>

->**E P I S T O L A .**<-

...expelled Athanasius <note>St. Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a chief defender of Trinitarianism against Arianism, whom Julian the Apostate exiled for his influence.</note>, and finally in every way disturbed and afflicted the Church. It will therefore be useful to recall from these records both what the state of those times was and what the feelings of good men were. For indeed, how great a pleasure will it finally be for the Church—having endured and been liberated from such great evils—not only to remember the labors performed, but also to look upon the enemies by whom she was so often wounded now driven away, the camps deserted, and their leader prostrate and lying low? Just so, those Trojans, exhausted by the weariness of the long siege, rejoiced when they saw the enemies removed, as the same poet <note>The poet Virgil.</note> says in the 2nd book of the *Aeneid*:

> *Therefore all Troy <note>original: "Teucria"</note> released itself from its long grief:*
> *The gates are opened; it is a joy to go out and see the Greek <note>original: "Dorica"</note> camps,*
> *The deserted places, and the abandoned shore:*
> *Here the band of Dolopians, here fierce Achilles used to pitch his tent.*

Therefore, this pleasure will be added to the utility of history by reading these things. Wherefore, even if no grace of eloquence or genius appeared in these writings, nevertheless no one, I believe, should take it poorly that these benefits of utility and pleasure have been shared by us with them. As for the reliability of the edition, since I had obtained a single copy from the library of Petrus Ramus <note>Pierre de la Ramée (1515–1572), a highly influential French humanist, logician, and educational reformer.</note>, and that copy was faulty in several places, the old reading...

† ij

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Pierre Martini to a distinguished Cardinal (likely Odet de Coligny). Martini concludes his remarks on the editorial process and transitions into praising the Cardinal’s patronage of the University of Paris and his teacher, the famous scholar Petrus Ramus.</meta>

# E P I S T L E.

Where [the text] seemed to be erroneous, we have changed it according to our own judgment, but we have noted in the margin how it was read previously. You will therefore receive, most distinguished Cardinal, both this small gift of an edition and myself, its author, with that kindness with which you are accustomed to receive all those in whom some sign of virtue and goodwill appears. 

Your praises are many and illustrious in every kind, but one in particular, as long as literature shall exist, will be proclaimed and carried to the heavens: that you protect, adorn, and enlarge the University of Paris <note>original: "Academiã Pariſiẽſem"</note>; that by your generosity you sustain the studies of many; and finally, that you embrace good and learned men with singular zeal and love. 

Because you truly hold Pierre de la Ramée <note>Petrus Ramus (1515–1572), a famous humanist, logician, and educational reformer who was the author's teacher.</note>, my teacher, in such high regard, I say that you bind to yourself in perpetuity not only me, but all those throughout the world who love true education—those awakened and assisted by his sleepless labors—and you entice them to love you and honor you with every duty. 

I have also some other works, such as, among the rest, a Hebrew grammar worked out by us according to logical principles <note>Martini is referring to his *Grammatica Hebraica* (1567), which applied Ramist logic to the study of Hebrew.</note>, which will be published in your name very soon, if I learn that these present works have not been unwelcome to you. 

Farewell. Paris <note>original: "Lutetia"</note>, May 1st, 1566.

->P. MARTINI<-

<summary>In this concluding section of the dedication, Pierre Martini praises the Cardinal’s patronage of the University of Paris and his support for the controversial scholar Petrus Ramus, while promising a future work on Hebrew grammar.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal preface to Julian’s *Misopogon* by the editor Pierre Martini. It follows the dedicatory epistle to the Cardinal dated May 1566.</meta>

# P. MARTINI
## MORENTINUS OF NAVARRE
### PREFACE TO THE *MISOPOGON*
### of the Emperor Julian,
### wherein the life of the author is discussed
### from various sources, especially the
### passages of [Ammianus] Marcellinus.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring ornate scrollwork and floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

That the Emperor Julian possessed singular talent and eloquence is declared by his own surviving works. Furthermore, no small praise for other virtues worthy of a prince is attributed to him in history, so much so that if the things recorded of him are true, I would not hesitate to compare him in those respects with the greatest of rulers. But a single stain of impiety and apostasy <note>original: "ἀποστασίας" (*apostasias*). Julian (r. 361–363 AD) was the last polytheistic emperor of Rome and was dubbed "the Apostate" by the Church for his rejection of Christianity.</note> has forever erased and extinguished all those praises. 

This is a great example and one especially to be considered, so that both princes and private citizens might dread the danger of such a great and wretched ruin. They should consider that the best and greatest God is to be worshiped sincerely, and they should embrace true piety truly and seriously, rather than thinking it to be some childish game or joke. Above all, they should reflect upon that most certain oracle of Christ: *Many are called, but few are chosen* <note>original: "πολλοί εἰσιν κλητοὶ, ὀλίγοι δὲ ἐκλεκτοὶ" (*polloi eisin klētoi, oligoi de eklektoi*). A quotation from the Gospel of Matthew 22:14.</note>. 

Therefore, so that this whole matter might be more easily understood, and the argument of the proposed speech be explained from its causes, the life of Julian must first be examined from his nature and educa—

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>In this preface, Pierre Martini introduces the Emperor Julian, acknowledging his great intellectual talents while condemning his "apostasy" from Christianity. He presents Julian's life as a cautionary tale for both rulers and commoners on the importance of sincere religious piety.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Julian the Apostate, Misopogon, Ammianus Marcellinus, Pierre Martini, piety, apostasy, Matthew 22:14, Christian history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Pierre Martini, completing the sentence from page 3 regarding the study of Julian’s education and character. It details his noble lineage, his precarious childhood during the dynastic purges, and his physical and mental traits as recorded by the historian Ammianus Marcellinus.</meta>

# PREFACE

—tion, and next it must be understood from his character. Regarding his nature, it involves his homeland and parents as well as his natural disposition of mind and body. His homeland, then, was Byzantium <note>Byzantium, the ancient Greek city, was rebuilt and renamed Constantinople by Constantine the Great in 330 AD.</note>, a noble and wealthy city of Thrace, then the seat and residence of the empire, which was later called Constantinople and New Rome. His parents were no less noble and famous than his homeland. For his father was Constantius <note>Julius Constantius (d. 337 AD), the half-brother of Constantine the Great.</note>, the brother of that great Constantine; his mother was named Basilina, who was noble by descent from her ancestors, as Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–400 AD), a Roman soldier and historian who is the primary source for Julian's life and reign.</note> says. However, he was not able to enjoy their presence for long; for his father was suppressed <note>Martini refers here to the massacre of the imperial family members that followed the death of Constantine the Great in 337 AD, intended to secure the throne for Constantine’s sons.</note> by the successors of the empire after the death of his brother Constantine, and his mother died a few months after bringing Julian into the light of the world.

Thus, while still a young boy, he was left destitute of all hope of comfort or protection along with his brother Gallus <note>Constantius Gallus (c. 325–354 AD), Julian's older half-brother, who served as Caesar under Constantius II before being executed.</note>, who was his only brother on his father’s side, and who was of uncertain and nearly broken health. The same ruin that had previously struck their father would have surely overwhelmed both sons, had not the one’s youth and the other’s ill health freed them from the danger. And these were the "seeds of nature" found in the splendor of his homeland and parents.

But in both mind and body, there was a varied natural disposition. His mind was indeed very restless <note>original: *levissimus*. In this context, it suggests a quick, perhaps fickle or highly active temperament that required grounding.</note>; thus (says Marcellinus in Book 22), he permitted his prefects and those closest to him to confidently restrain his impulses with timely warnings whenever they strayed, turning them back toward what was proper. He showed from time to time that he grieved over his faults and rejoiced in being corrected. His physical constitution, of a moderate sort, is described by the same author in Book 25: "The form of his limbs," he says, "was such: he was of moderate height, with soft hair as if it had been combed, and a shaggy beard tapering to a point. He glowed with the beauty of flashing eyes, which revealed the sharpness <note>original: *angustias*. Marcellinus uses this term to describe the intense, piercing quality of Julian's gaze or the "quickness" of his mental processing.</note> of his mind, and eyebrows..."

<meta>catchword: handsome [decoris]</meta>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical and psychological profile of Julian the Apostate, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's description of his anatomy, likely sourced from Ammianus Marcellinus. It then transitions into a much harsher critique by Gregory of Nazianzus.</meta>

### ON JULIAN'S MISOPOGON. 5

...handsome and with a very straight nose, a mouth somewhat large, his lower lip divided <note>original: "labro inferiore diviso." This likely refers to a prominent vertical groove or a slight cleft in the lip.</note>, a thick and arched neck, and vast, broad shoulders. From his head down to the tips of his nails, his features were joined in a straight alignment, giving him great strength and speed in running. Here, through the narrowness of his mind, the same fickleness <note>original: "levitas." In this context, it refers to a lack of moral or intellectual stability.</note> noted above is observed; yet a physical firmness is added, from which a great portion of his later military glory was achieved. 

But on this entire matter, the oration of Gregory of Nazianzus <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–389 AD) was a Christian theologian and classmate of Julian in Athens; he became one of Julian’s most vocal critics.</note> is more detailed and more candid. He saw Julian as a young man in Athens and discerned his future character from the habits of his soul and body. He speaks as follows in his second book *Against Julian*:

> Then (he says) I felt that I was no poor judge of character, although I am not one of those born with a natural talent for such arts <term>Physiognomy: the pseudo-scientific practice of judging a person's character from their physical appearance</term>. Rather, the inconstancy of his nature and his strange excitability made me capable of divining his future, for he is the best prophet who knows how to guess correctly. Indeed, it seemed to me a sign of no good thing to see a neck bent forward, shoulders that twitched and jumped, a wandering and rolling eye that looked about with a furious gaze, and feet that were unstable and limping. He was a mocker, breathing out insult and contempt; his facial expressions were ridiculous, bearing the same immoderate and unrestrained laughter. He would nod and shake his head without reason, his speech was hesitant and gasping for breath, his questions were inept and foolish, and his answers were no better—clashing with one another, never constant, and never proceeding in an orderly or learned fashion. Why should I pursue every detail? I saw him to be the same man before his deeds as I later knew him to be through his deeds.

These are his words, from which we understand the inherent and inborn perpetual fickleness and inconstancy of this man, so that the primary cause of the errors that followed may already be grasped. Such, then, was his nature. What of his education? By which masters, and by what—

<sig>A iij</sig>

<summary>This page contrasts Julian's physical strength and "straight" features with the scathing psychological and physical critique by Gregory of Nazianzus, who viewed Julian's nervous mannerisms as omens of his future instability.</summary>

<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Gregory of Nazianzus, physiognomy, Athens, character analysis, physical description, imperial biography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Emperor Julian, moving from the physical descriptions provided by his contemporaries to his early upbringing and education under the eunuch Mardonius and later sophists.</meta>

# PREFACE
## 6

...was treated and cultivated in various types of studies and arts. 

After his seventh year of age, he was handed over to the eunuch Mardonius. Regarding this matter, Julian himself addresses the people of Antioch in his work *The Beard-Hater* <note>original: "Misopogon." A satirical essay written by Julian in 363 AD to mock the people of Antioch and defend his own lifestyle.</note> in this manner: "Do you wish," he says, "for me to produce the name and race of my tutor? He was a barbarian and a Scythian by birth, and bore the same name as that man who led Xerxes into the war against Greece and noble Argos. This man—whose name was once a title of honor but is now used as an insult <note>Mardonius was the name of the Persian general defeated at Plataea; Julian implies the name is now associated with the empire's ancient enemies.</note>—was a eunuch, educated under my grandfather so that he might explain the poems of Homer and Hesiod to my mother. But when she died a few months later, having left me as her first and only son, I—like a deserted and orphaned maiden snatched from many calamities, still a tender girl—was handed over to him after my seventh year." <note>Julian uses these feminine metaphors to emphasize his helplessness and the vulnerability of his youth.</note> 

From this teacher, he was then led to the public schools in Constantinople, where he spent his boyhood practicing grammatical and rhetorical studies. He had Nicocles the Lacedaemonian <note>Lacedaemonian refers to someone from Sparta.</note> as his master of the grammatical art, and Ecebolius the sophist for rhetoric. Furthermore, this teacher [Ecebolius] later followed the changing fortunes of his pupil and defected from the Christians to the pagans <note>original: "ethnicos." Literally 'the nations,' a common term used by early Christian writers to refer to non-Christians or polytheists.</note>. Finally, once Julian had died, he returned to the Christians; prostrating himself before the doors of the temple <note>In this context, the 'temple' refers to a Christian church building.</note>, he cried out repeatedly: **"Tread upon me, the salt that has lost its flavor."** <note>A reference to Matthew 5:13. Ecebolius was performing a public act of penance for his apostasy.</note> 

Yet for Julian, this schooling was a matter of no less piety and religion than of intellect and literature. Therefore, he performed the public office of a reader <term>Anagnostes: A lower-ranking member of the clergy in the early Church responsible for the public reading of the Holy Scriptures during the liturgy.</term> in the church and recited the sacred books in the hearing of the people. He believed that nothing greater or more enriching could happen to him for his own glory than this piety. Thus, being devoted to these teachers and arts, he shortly achieved such great fame and authority among all men that [he was prepared] to administer the empire...

<column-break/>

<meta>catchword: administrando (administering)</meta>

It highlights Julian's early, seemingly sincere participation in the Christian church as a reader (anagnostes) before his later fame and rise to power.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Emperor Julian, detailing his education in Nicomedia. It follows the previous discussion of his early tutors, Mardonius and Ecebolius, moving into his exposure to the rhetorician Libanius and his eventual embrace of Neoplatonic philosophy.</meta>

# ON JULIAN’S *THE BEARD-HATER*
### 7

...was frequently said to be well-suited for demonstrating his talents. Therefore, so that no opportunity for a political revolution might arise from it, he departed from this first school in Nicomedia. Libanius the Rhetorician <note>Libanius (314–392 AD) was one of the most influential Greek teachers of the 4th century; though a pagan, his students included both Julian and prominent Christian saints like John Chrysostom.</note> was flourishing in that city at that time, having achieved great praise for his eloquence, but he was particularly devoted to pagan superstition. For this reason, Julian at first kept away from his school and only read his books at home, because he was wonderfully delighted by them. 

But afterward, having been gradually corrupted by his familiarity with Libanius’s style, Julian not only began to frequent the school, but even dared to declaim against the Christians in the Greek manner—as if, for the sake of exercising his talent, he wished to make some incredible thesis appear probable through his speaking. This was Julian's second school: a school, I say, that was a mistress of error and impiety. For Paul rightly says, quoting the poet Menander:

> Bad company corrupts good character.
> <note>original Greek: Φθείρουσιν ἤθη χρηστὰ ὁμιλίαι κακαί. original Latin: Corrumpunt mores bonos congressus mali. This is a famous maxim from the Greek playwright Menander, later quoted by the Apostle Paul in 1 Corinthians 15:33.</note>

Nevertheless, in this school, such were his exercises in eloquence. There he also learned philosophy. Philosophy at that time was not entirely Platonic or Aristotelian, but a mixture of both. Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) known for his work *Isagoge*, an introduction to logic.</note> a little before, and then Iamblichus <note>A Syrian Neoplatonist (c. 245–325 AD) who integrated religious ritual and magic into philosophy.</note> were considered the leaders of this system of education. Iamblichus's authority was the highest among all; therefore, Julian especially honored and loved this philosopher, and bestowed great honors and rewards upon him. 

He held to this kind of philosophy: hence, in his letters, he calls himself a **devotee of the doctrines of Plato and Aristotle** <note>original: "τῶν Πλάτωνος καὶ Ἀριστοτέλους ζηλωτὴν δογμάτων."</note>. In this matter, he also made great use of the work of that eunuch teacher whom I mentioned before [Mardonius], just as Julian himself declares to the people of Antioch in *The Beard-Hater* <note>original: "Misopogon."</note>, when he shifts the blame for his own lifestyle onto him with a certain irony: "Often," he says, "you have heard these names, as I believe..."

<sig>A iiij</sig>

<summary>This page describes Julian's education under the pagan rhetorician Libanius and his transition from Christian studies to Neoplatonic philosophy. It highlights how his rhetorical exercises evolved into critiques of Christianity and identifies Iamblichus as a major philosophical influence.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Libanius, Nicomedia, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Misopogon, Mardonius, Rhetoric, Greek Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Emperor Julian, focusing on the influence of his early tutors and his transition toward the mystical philosophy of Maximus of Ephesus. It follows the discussion of Julian's education and his exposure to ancient philosophers like Porphyry.</meta>

->PREFACE<-

...are well known to you, though clearly so greatly harassed and derided: Plato, Socrates, Aristotle, and Theophrastus. When that old man <note>Julian refers here to his childhood tutor, Mardonius, who raised him with a strict, traditional education based on the Greek classics.</note> had followed them perhaps too foolishly and simply, and afterward encountered me as a boy burning with the desire to learn, he persuaded me that if I wished to follow in their footsteps, I would become better—not perhaps better than other men (for I would have no contest with them), but certainly better than my own self. I indeed (for what else should I have done?) having obeyed the old man, can now change nothing at all, however much I might desire to; and I blame myself for not opening a free path to all vices. 

But there come to my mind those things which the Athenian guest discusses in Plato's works: 

> He is worthy of honor who commits no sin; but he who even deters others from injury is worthy of more than double honor. For the former is equal to one person, while the latter is equal to many, as he reports the crimes of others to the magistrates. But he who, moreover, joins himself as an ally in punishing injury, this man is a great and perfect man in the state; let him be pronounced the victor in the contest of virtue. 
> <note>This quote is taken from Plato’s *Laws*, Book 5. Julian is using it to justify his own philosophical rigor and his later actions as Emperor.</note>

These things that old man taught me. And such, up to that point, was the philosophy of Julian. 

Meanwhile, Maximus of Ephesus <note>Maximus was a Neoplatonist philosopher and theurgist (a practitioner of "divine magic") who became Julian's most influential spiritual advisor.</note> came to Nicomedia. He was a philosopher who was later punished with the death penalty by the Emperor Valentinian for his magical arts and impostures. This man was Julian’s second corrupter, and taught him, as Nazianzen <note>Saint Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), a contemporary of Julian who wrote scathing "Invectives" against him after his death.</note> says, an impiety 

> <note>original Greek: ὅση τε περὶ ἀστρονομίαν καὶ τὰς γενέσεις καὶ φαντασίαν προγνώσεως τερατεύεται, καὶ τὴν ἑπομένην τούτοις γοητείαν</note> 
> "that is concerned with astronomy, horoscopes, and the illusions of monstrous predictions, and the sorcery that follows these things." 

This school plays tricks with astronomy, birthdays, and the appearance of divination, with magic as their companion. The letters written to Maximus in such honorable terms declare how much Julian always valued this philosopher. From this school, he then moved to Athens 

> <note>original Greek: καθ' ἱστορίαν τῆς Ἑλλάδος</note> 
> "to seek out the history of Greece," and...

<summary>This page describes Julian's shift from traditional classical education under Mardonius to the "corrupting" influence of Maximus of Ephesus, who introduced him to magic and divination. It highlights the conflict between Julian's early philosophical training and the mystical practices that would later define his reign.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Julian the Apostate’s upbringing and character. While the previous page discussed his early philosophical training under Mardonius and his first encounters with theurgy through Maximus of Ephesus, this page examines his time in the schools of Greece and compares his early temperament to that of his brother, Gallus, and other historical figures like Marcus Aurelius.</meta>

# ON JULIAN'S *MISOPOGON*. 9

<margin>the schools located there</margin> Gregory <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a prominent Christian theologian who studied with Julian in Athens and later became one of his harshest critics.</note> says that he traveled to visit Greece and to learn about the schools of that region, where we showed above that he was personally known. Therefore, Julian's education was established by such teachers and arts, so that the second cause of his error <note>The author is identifying three "causes" for Julian's eventual rejection of Christianity: his nature, his education, and his ambition.</note> now easily becomes apparent. Thus far, we see only the seeds of both his nature and his education; let us now look at the fruits in his character. Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian and soldier who served under Julian and wrote a detailed history of his reign.</note> touches upon these generally in his fourteenth book when he compares the character of Julian's brother, Gallus. He says, *"He [Gallus] differed as much from the temperate character of his brother Julian as Domitian did from Titus among the sons of Vespasian."* <note>original: "Tantúm... á temperatis moribus Juliani differens fratris, quantum inter Vespasiani filios fuit Domitianum & Titum." Titus was remembered as a "darling of the human race," while his brother Domitian was remembered as a suspicious tyrant.</note> Here Gallus is compared to Domitian, and Julian to Titus. And indeed, he [Julian] seems not to have been averse to moderation and modesty of spirit, since of all the emperors, he especially loved the philosopher Marcus [Aurelius] and set him as a model for himself to imitate. This was certainly the fruit not so much of nature as of education, which, as **Ovid** says:

> It softens the spirit and does not allow it to be wild.
> <note>original: "Emollitque animos, nec sinit esse feros." A famous line from Ovid’s *Letters from the Black Sea* regarding the civilizing influence of the liberal arts.</note>

But nevertheless, a certain heat of ambition disturbed this moderation and tranquility of character. Thus, this third cause, joined to the two former ones—nature and education—easily led the man away from the true and the right, plunging him into fraud and crime. However, let us now see his character in each individual virtue. And first, what was his courage like? As a young man not yet having passed his twenty-third year, he was created Caesar <note>In this period, "Caesar" was the title for a junior emperor or heir-apparent, while "Augustus" was the senior emperor.</note> on the eighth day before the Ides of November [November 6th] during the consulship of Arbetio and Lollianus. This was at a time when barbarians were laying waste to Gaul, and the Emperor [Constantius II] was prevented by fear from waging war against them. Marcellinus says in his fifteenth book, *“Frequent messengers were harassing Constantius...”*

<summary>This page analyzes the three factors leading to Julian's "error"—his nature, education, and ambition—while comparing his early moderate temperament to his brother Gallus and the Emperor Marcus Aurelius. It marks the transition to Julian's political and military career, beginning with his appointment as Caesar to defend Gaul.</summary>

<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Ammianus Marcellinus, Education, Marcus Aurelius, Gallus, Titus and Domitian, Roman Empire, Caesar, Gaul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of Julian’s education in Greece and his temperate nature compared to his brother Gallus. # PREFACE

*...indicating that Gaul was already in a desperate state, with no one resisting the barbarians who were laying waste to everything. For a long time, the Emperor <note>Constantius II, the cousin of Julian who had executed much of Julian's family but now needed a legal heir to help manage the crumbling empire.</note>, residing in Italy, deliberated on how he might repel these calamities, as he himself desired (for he considered it dangerous to thrust himself into a region so far removed). At last, he found a proper course of action: he thought to bring his cousin Julian—who had not long ago been brought back from Greece at his summons—into the partnership of the empire, even though Julian was still wearing his philosopher’s cloak <note>original: "palliatum". The pallium was the traditional cloak of a Greek philosopher, signaling that Julian was still a student of the Academy, not a soldier or a statesman.</note>. He later carried this out, and furthermore gave him his sister Helena in marriage.*

*Julian, meanwhile, was not unaware of the great dangers to which he was being exposed; he believed he was being called not so much to honor as to his death. Thus, on the day when he was dressed in the imperial purple and brought into the court in the royal manner, he is said to have quoted this verse from Homer:*

->Purple death and strong fate seized him.<-
<note>original Greek: Ἔλλαβε πορφύρεος θάνατος καὶ μοῖρα κραταιή; Latin: Cæpit purpurea mors, & parca violenta. This is a line from the Iliad (5.83). Julian is making a dark pun: the "purple" of the imperial robes he just put on would lead to his "purple" (bloody) death in battle.</note>

*He was even frequently heard murmuring that he would gain nothing more than to die under a heavier burden of business. Hence that silent complaint in his work, the Misopogon <note>The "Beard-Hater," a satirical work Julian wrote later in life to mock the people of Antioch who ridiculed his philosophical austerity.</note>, that he had been banished to the Gauls, the Germans, and the Hercynian Forest <note>A vast, ancient forest in central Europe that represented the wild, "barbarous" frontier of the Roman world.</note> to wage war with wild beasts like a hunter. And in another place he says: "It seemed that neither of us wanted it: neither he who gave this honor (or favor, or whatever other name you wish to call it) gave it willingly, and he who received it (the gods are my witnesses) truly and from his soul refused it." Thus he speaks of himself.*

*And certainly, as Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, the primary historian of this era and a soldier who served under Julian.</note> says, it was tossed about everywhere as a rumor that Julian had been chosen not to relieve the troubles of Gaul, but so that he might be destroyed in the most savage wars—since he was judged to be inexperienced even then, and unlikely to endure even the sound of arms. Nevertheless, that inexperienced youth, raised in the shade of the Academy <note>The school founded by Plato in Athens; the "shade" implies a life of quiet study away from the "sun and dust" of the battlefield.</note> and not in the sun and dust...*

<meta>catchword: truly</meta>

<summary>This page describes Julian the Apostate’s reluctant transition from a student of philosophy in Greece to the Caesar of Gaul, highlighting his fear that the Emperor Constantius II intended for him to die in battle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Julian’s transition from a student in Athens to a military commander in Gaul. # ON JULIAN’S *MISOPOGON* <note>The "Misopogon" (Beard-Hater) was a satirical work written by Julian himself later in his reign.</note>

Having been educated in a truly military fashion <note>original: "vere militari educatus". This suggests Julian adapted quickly to soldiering despite his academic background.</note> and cast into such great waves of danger, what did he do? If he had wished only to protect the province committed to him and merely repel injury without provoking the enemy, he would still be rightly judged to have performed a great feat. But he did provoke them; and he who had been accustomed to being a terror to others was himself forced to fear Julian. 

I say nothing of the speed with which he expelled the barbarians from Gaul, nor the courage with which he defended the noble and ancient cities of **Autun**, **Auxerre**, **Troyes**, and **Reims**. Finally, I will not commemorate the other things he achieved in Gaul and Germany; I say only what all the writers of that age proclaim with one voice: that seven of the most powerful kings of all Germany were defeated by him in one massive and most difficult battle at **Strasbourg** <note>original: "Argentoratum". The Battle of Strasbourg in 357 AD was Julian’s most famous victory, where his 13,000 men defeated a much larger Alamanni force.</note>, and their leader, **Chonodomarius**, was captured and sent to Rome to Constantius. In every respect, the shattered and afflicted affairs of Gaul were restored to their original state by him. 

But this praise was contaminated by the most wretched stain of ambition when he wished to be hailed as **Augustus** <note>The title of the senior Emperor. Up to this point, Julian had been "Caesar," or junior emperor. His troops proclaimed him Augustus in Paris in 360 AD, which was seen as an act of rebellion against his cousin Constantius II.</note>. From this, a most atrocious civil war would have followed had not an almost sudden death carried off Constantius beforehand. Upon receiving this news, Julian set out first for Constantinople to establish his empire, and then for Antioch for the **Persian War**. 

And here it should be noted what Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian historian whose *Ecclesiastical History* is a major source for this period.</note> relates in his history: that Julian, persuaded by a certain Pythagorean **metempsychosis** <note>original Greek: μετεμψυχώσει; the belief in the transmigration of souls from one body to another.</note>, believed himself to be Alexander the Great. Therefore, he perhaps hoped for a similar glory from the defeat of the Persians; however, the matter turned out entirely differently, as will be understood later. Thus we see that the praise for this virtue shone forth greatly in this emperor. Let no one think that the study of literature and wisdom hinders military glory, or that physical strength is more...

<summary>This page recounts Julian the Apostate's military successes in Gaul, specifically his victory at Strasbourg, and his subsequent rise to power as Augustus, fueled by his belief that he was the reincarnation of Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Gaul, Battle of Strasbourg, Chonodomarius, Constantius II, Alexander the Great, metempsychosis, military vs. scholarship</keywords>

->### PREFACE<-

...than that great things are managed by the prudence of the mind. But though he possessed such courage, what of his **temperance**? It was altogether admirable, both in his private and public life. And indeed, his private life regarding food, sleep, and sexual matters was exemplary. Regarding food and sleep, **Marcellinus** <note>original: "Marcellinus". This refers to the Roman historian Ammianus Marcellinus, a primary source for Julian's life.</note> says in his sixteenth book: "He forbade pheasant, sow’s womb, and udders <note>original: "Phasianum & vulvam, & sumen". These were considered luxury delicacies in Roman high society.</note> to be demanded or brought to him, being content with the cheap and random food of a common soldier." From this it resulted that he divided his nights into three duties: rest, public affairs, and the **Muses** <note>The "Muses" here refers to his literary and philosophical studies.</note>. We read that **Alexander the Great** used to do this, but Julian did so much more vigorously. For Alexander, with a bronze basin placed beneath him, held a silver ball in his arm stretched out beyond the bed; so that when sleep relaxed the tension of his muscles, the ringing caused by the falling of the object into the basin would break his sleep. Julian, however, woke whenever he wished without any device. Rising always at midnight, he did not get up from down feathers or silk blankets shining with shifting light, but from a rug and a **sisura** <note>original: "σισύρᾳ". A rough, shaggy blanket or cloak made of goat hair.</note>, which common folk call a *sifurna*. <margin>In the common editions of Marcellinus, this is incorrectly read as *xysira* and *Susurna*.</margin> Julian declares this same thing about himself in his *Misopogon* <note>The "Misopogon" or "Beard-Hater" is a satirical essay Julian wrote to mock the people of Antioch who ridiculed his ascetic lifestyle.</note>, where he complains that he was hated by the people of Antioch for this very reason.

> "For my private habits," he says, 
> "sleepless nights on a rough blanket, and a diet entirely lacking 
> in satiety, produce harsh manners that are repulsive 
> to a delicate city. But do not think this is done by me 
> for your sake: a certain grave and foolish error has 
> led me ever since I was a boy to wage war against my belly. 
> For I do not allow it to be filled with banquets."

Therefore, it has very rarely happened that I vomited, and I remember it happening to me only once since I was made Caesar—and not from an excess of food, but by chance. Thus, in this manner, he almost boasts of his temperance in food and sleep. From this, furthermore, followed his temperance in sexual matters; concerning which Marcellinus in his [twenty-fifth] book...

# ON JULIAN’S *BEARD-HATER* <note>original: "Misopogon". This is a famous satirical work written by Julian to mock the citizens of Antioch who ridiculed his simple lifestyle and beard.</note>

...in the twenty-fifth book, he [Ammianus Marcellinus] first says that Julian shone with such inviolate chastity that after the loss of his wife, he never again engaged in any sexual activity. He lost his wife shortly after receiving the honor of the rank of **Augustus**, <margin>Julian's young son</margin> having previously lost an infant son by her while in Gaul. The same author continues shortly after: "This stain [of lust]," he says, "he so cautiously avoided in the full strength of his adult years that he was never accused even of the slightest suspicion of any desire, not even by the servants of his private life, as often happens." And in the twenty-fourth book, when he records the plunder captured during the Persian war, he says: "Of the beautiful young women who were captured—as is common in Persia, where the beauty of women is exceptional—he wished neither to touch nor even to see any of them. In this, he imitated **Alexander the Great** and **Scipio Africanus**, <note>Two of the most famous generals of antiquity, both known in historical anecdotes for their self-restraint toward female captives.</note> who avoided such things so as not to be broken by desire, proving themselves unconquered by hardships everywhere." 

Furthermore, he expelled the barbers and cooks as if they were agents of luxury and excess. Marcellinus says in the twenty-second book: "It happened that a barber, having been ordered to come and trim the Emperor's hair, entered dressed in an ostentatious manner. Upon seeing him, Julian was stunned. 'I did not,' he said, 'order a financial officer, <note>original: "rationale". An imperial official in charge of the treasury or accounts.</note> but a barber.'" When the man was asked what profit he made from his trade, he replied that he received twenty daily rations of food, and as many portions of fodder for pack animals (which are commonly called **units** <note>original: "capita", literally 'heads', used here as a technical term for animal rations.</note>), along with a heavy annual salary, not counting many lucrative bonuses. Moved by this, Julian dismissed all such servants, along with cooks and others like them who were accustomed to receiving nearly the same, viewing them as unnecessary to him. Giving them the power to go wherever they wished, he cast them out. 

And this was his temperance at home; there follows his external temperance regarding theaters and games. Of this, there is a certain boast, presented under the guise of self-criticism, in his work *The Beard-Hater*. "I keep myself away," he says...

<summary>This page details Emperor Julian's strict personal morality, including his lifelong chastity following his wife's death and his dismissal of the overpaid, extravagant palace staff, such as barbers and cooks, in favor of a simpler life.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Ammianus Marcellinus, chastity, temperance, Misopogon, Alexander the Great, Scipio Africanus, Roman imperial court, austerity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Emperor Julian’s personal discipline, moving from his dismissal of palace luxuries (like barbers and cooks mentioned on the previous page) to his famous disdain for public spectacles like the theater and the chariot races. # PREFACE

> ...[I stay away] from theaters through the height of foolishness, nor do I admit the stage into the palace court, except at the beginning of the year—and even then, I do so out of a daze of the mind, like a poor farmer paying some forced tribute to an unjust master. But even on those occasions when I have entered, I am more like a man who detests those games than one who is actually watching them. 

The same author says a little later: "Pay attention, for what I shall say is even more novel and admirable. I have always hated the **Circus games** <term>Circenses: The Roman chariot races held in the Circus Maximus, the most popular and rowdy entertainment of the era.</term>, just as those who are burdened by debt hate the **forum** <note>The forum was the center of legal and financial business; Julian suggests he avoids the races with the same anxiety a debtor feels when passing the place where his creditors wait.</note>. Therefore, I rarely attend them—only on the feast days of the gods—and I do not spend the whole day there, as my paternal cousin, my maternal uncle, and my own brother used to do. Instead, after watching about the sixth race, and even that not with great pleasure—rather, by Hercules, with annoyance and disgust—I gladly depart." 

And he says that this was indeed the fruit of his learning alone. "My tutor taught me," Julian writes, "when I was going to my teachers, to look at the ground rather than the theater, and to value the chin of my head more highly <note>A reference to his philosopher’s beard, which he prized as a symbol of maturity and wisdom over the smooth-shaven face of a frivolous youth.</note>. Frequently that tutor said to me when I was a boy: 'Do not allow yourself to be led into the theaters by the crowd of your peers, nor be held by any desire for such a spectacle. Do you seek the **Circus games**? You will find them most elegantly described in **Homer**; take up the book and read. Do you hear of actors and dancers? Let them be; the young men among the **Phaeacians** <note>In Homer's Odyssey, the Phaeacians were a seafaring people known for their graceful dancing.</note> dance more vigorously. You have there the lyre-player **Phemius** and the singer **Demodocus**. There are also in that same poet many descriptions <note>original: "stirpes" (literally "stems" or "roots"), here referring to the poetic passages or lineages of stories</note> which are heard with greater pleasure than our shows are watched.'"

->Thus once in **Delos**, by the sacred altars of **Apollo**,<-
->I beheld a tall palm tree rising from the green grass.<-
<note>Julian is quoting the *Odyssey* (Book 6, lines 162–163), where Odysseus compares the beauty of a young woman to a palm tree he once saw. Julian uses this to show that literature provides more beautiful "sights" than the theater.</note>

> What of **Calypso’s** island shaded by trees? What of **Circe’s** caves? What of the garden of **Alcinous**? Believe me, you will see nothing more pleasant than these in your mind. 

Therefore, there was a great **self-restraint** <term>temperantia: The virtue of moderation and self-control, highly prized by Stoic and Neoplatonist philosophers like Julian.</term> in this prince, which must be credited to his diligence in learning. Meanwhile...

# ON JULIAN’S *MISOPOGON*. 15

...nevertheless, a certain empty talkativeness and unseemly behavior were noted in him. When one day (says **Marcellinus** <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–400 AD), a Roman soldier and historian who is the primary source for Julian's reign. Though he admired Julian, he was often critical of his lack of imperial "gravitas."</note>) it was announced to him while he was hearing legal cases that the philosopher **Maximus** <note>Maximus of Ephesus, a Neoplatonist philosopher and theurgist who became Julian's most influential spiritual mentor.</note> had arrived from Asia, he leaped up in an unseemly manner. Forgetting who he was, he ran headlong far out from the entrance hall, and having kissed and received the man with reverence, he led him back inside with him. Through this ill-timed display, he appeared to be an excessive seeker of empty glory. This lightness of character was natural and innate to him, as was shown previously, nor could any amount of education remove it.

But since we have examined his self-restraint, let us now look at his **wisdom** in every branch of learning. That his mind was most eager for learning
<margin>Ecdicius, Prefect of Egypt</margin>
is proven by a letter to a certain **Ecdicius**, the Prefect of Egypt, in which he demands the books of Bishop **George of Alexandria** <note>Julian ordered the seizure of this library after George was murdered by a mob; Julian wanted the collection for himself, despite his hatred for the Christian bishop.</note>. *“Some,”* Julian says, *“delight in horses, others in birds, some in wild beasts. But I, indeed, have burned with a desire for books since I was a child.”* Isocrates says, *“If you are a lover of learning, you will be very learned”* <note>original Greek: ἐὰν ᾖς φιλομαθὴς, ἔσῃ πολυμαθὴς</note>. Therefore, this **love of learning** produced **great learning** in Julian. 

What, then, of the liberal arts—Grammar, Rhetoric, and Logic—how did they fare in him? We have already seen his masters of the arts of Grammar and Rhetoric, within which Logic was also contained; but let us seek a clearer and more certain proof of his learning from his own writings. It is likely that during that period of leisure in schools and studies, before he attained the honor of the title of Caesar, many things were written by him that have perished through the ravages of time. Of these, only a few orations sent to **Iamblichus** <note>A famous Neoplatonist philosopher whom Julian deeply admired, though they were not contemporaries.</note> are mentioned, as is clear from a certain letter to him. But after he entered public life, he handled and cultivated these studies even more diligently. From these sleepless nights, **Suidas** <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> commemorates the work titled *The Caesars* (namely, the lives of the Caesars from Augustus onward) and the work *On the Three Figures* <note>original: περὶ τριῶν σχημάτων, likely a grammatical or rhetorical treatise on the forms of logic or speech.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Emperor Julian’s character and his preoccupation with omens. ### PREFACE

...crying out, "Alas! What a great tree, and yet there is danger that the sapling <note>original: *surculus*. Julian is the sapling, while the great tree represents the reigning Constantinian dynasty.</note> born from it might also perish." At this, someone entirely unknown to me said: "Contemplate it diligently, and be of good heart; for while the root remains in the ground, that small tree will remain and take firmer hold." This clearly indicates that he would succeed **Constantius** <note>Constantius II, Julian's cousin and the senior Emperor, whose relationship with Julian was fraught with suspicion.</note>. Those things narrated by **Marcellinus** <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, the historian mentioned on the previous page.</note> in his twenty-first book pertain to nearly the same theme. He says that when **Julian Caesar** was exercising on the field at Paris, shaking his shield with various movements, the small pins that held the circular frame together were shaken out and lost; only the handle remained, which he gripped and squeezed with a strong hand. While everyone present was terrified as if by a dire omen, he said, "Let no one fear. I hold firmly what I was already holding." 

Likewise, when he was later resting soberly at **Vienne** <note>A city in south-eastern Gaul (modern France) where Julian stayed during his campaigns.</note>, at the hour of midnight, a certain vision appeared, shining brightly. It spoke these heroic verses to him while he was just barely awake, repeating the same things many times. Relying on these words, he judged that no hardship remained for him:

> When **Jupiter** shall reach the wide boundary of glorious **Aquarius**,
> and when **Saturn** shall walk upon the twenty-fifth degree
> of the **Virgin**, King **Constantius** shall have a hateful 
> and painful end to his dear life in the land of Asia.
<note>original Greek: Ζεὺς ὅταν ἐκ πλατὺ τέρμα μόλῃ κλυτοῦ ὑδροχόοιο, παρθενικῆς δὲ κρόνος μοίρῃ βαίνῃ ἐπὶ πέμπτῃ εἰκοστῇ, βασιλεὺς κωνστάντιος ἀσίδος αἴης τέρμα φίλου βιοτοῦ στυγερὸν καὶ ἐπώδυνον ἕξει.</note>

*Jupiter, when he has crossed the wide boundary of the famous Aquarius, and Saturn has come to the twenty-fifth part of the Virgin, King Constantius in the land of Asia shall have a sad and lamentable end to his beloved life.* <note>The author provides a Latin translation of the Greek verse: *Jupiter ubi trãfierit latũ terminũ inclyti aquarii...*</note>

From this magical vanity, he collapsed into a false, impious, and superstitious theology. He used to supplicate **Mercury** in secret (says **Marcellinus**); and so that he might entice everyone to his favor without hindrance, he feigned adherence to the Christian cult, from which he had already secretly defected long ago...

It also describes his secret abandonment of Christianity in favor of pagan worship and magic.</summary>

### ON JULIAN’S MISOPOGON <note>The *Misopogon* or "Beard-Hater" was a satirical work written by Julian himself against the people of Antioch who mocked his lifestyle and appearance.</note>

...shared by only a few companions, he was intent upon **the art of divination and augury**, and all the other things that worshippers of the gods have always performed. And this pretense lasted only as long as **Constantius** lived. But upon his death, Julian plainly changed his religion; meanwhile, he cleverly fostered discord among the people so that he might reign more securely. Indeed, as the same author <note>Referring to the historian Ammianus Marcellinus.</note> states in his twenty-second book, when he noticed that the things he feared were abolished and he had free time to do as he wished, he laid bare the secrets of his heart. By explicit decrees, he ordered that the temples be opened, that sacrificial victims be brought to the altars, and that the worship of the gods be restored.

<margin>«</margin>
In order to strengthen the effect of his arrangements, he summoned the disagreeing **Christian bishops**, along with the divided commoners, into his palace. He warned them that, once their civil discords were laid to rest, each person should serve his own religion without fear or hindrance.
<margin>«</margin>
He did this persistently for this reason: so that as license increased their dissensions, he would not have to fear a unified populace later. 
<margin>«</margin>
Therefore, this religious policy sufficiently declares the man's theology. Yet in the performance of sacred rites, he was foolishly superstitious, and ridiculous even to the pagans themselves. As **Marcellinus** says in his twenty-fifth book, he was "superstitious rather than a lawful observer of sacred rites," slaughtering countless cattle without restraint. It was estimated that if he had returned from his campaign against the **Parthians**, oxen would have soon been lacking. In this, he was like that **Emperor Marcus** <note>Marcus Aurelius, who was also noted for his extensive sacrifices.</note>, regarding whom we have received this saying:

<margin>The word *synikēsē* is poorly read in the text of Marcellinus.</margin>
*The white oxen to Marcus Caesar: If you win, we are finished.* <note>original Greek: οἱ λευκοὶ βόες μάρκῳ τῷ καίσαρι· ἂν σὺ νικήσῃς, ἡμεῖς ἀπωλόμεθα. This was a famous joke suggesting the emperor's sacrifices were so numerous they would exhaust the supply of white cattle.</note>

How greatly he burned with hatred for the **Christian religion** is declared by the books he wrote and published against it. Such, then, was his theology. It is said, however, that he held an excellent view regarding the excellence of the soul. 
<margin>«</margin>
Hence that noble saying of his: *It is shameful for a wise man, since he possesses a soul, to seek praise from...*
<margin>«</margin>

<meta>This page continues the character analysis of the Emperor Julian. # PREFACE.

*...body. And so much for his prudence. But what of his justice? What was it like? He snatched the supreme power from **Constantius**; this was the height of iniquity. Indeed, although he did not harass the **Christians** with the sword and open force like other tyrants before him, he nevertheless miserably vexed them with the most unjust and bitter laws. Two edicts against them survive in his letters: one by which he forbids them from schools; the other by which he strips them of their goods and fortunes. He even mocked them most wickedly, claiming he was doing this "so that they might travel more easily into the kingdom of heaven" <note>original Greek: "ἵνα εις τὴν βασιλείαν τῶν οὐρανῶν εὐοδώτερον πορευθῶσι"</note>. **Marcellinus** <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian who was generally a supporter of Julian but critical of this specific law.</note> mentions the former edict in his twenty-second book. "That merciless law," he says, "should be buried in eternal silence, which forbade masters of rhetoric and grammar who were practitioners of the Christian rite from teaching."*

*Furthermore, he barred these same people from military service and from holding magistrate offices, because he claimed the **Gospel** <term>Gospel: The Christian scriptures, specifically the New Testament.</term> took the power of the sword away from them, as **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Byzantine church historian.</note> relates in his *Ecclesiastical History*. In this, he perversely and maliciously misappropriated the authority of the Gospel, which does not prohibit public justice <note>Literally "public vengeance" (vindictam), referring to the state's right to use force.</note>, but only private vengeance. He also rejected Christians complaining of injuries, mocking them by saying: "It is your lot to endure injuries" <note>original Greek: "ὑμέτερον τὸ κακοπαθεῖν"</note>. Out of hatred for the Christians, he lavished benefits upon the **Jews** and urged them to restore their **Temple**; an edict on this matter survives in his letters. But the Jews attempted this in vain against the decree of God, and to their great misfortune. **Marcellinus** is a witness to this in his twenty-third book. "He thought," he says, "to restore at immoderate expense the once-ambitious Temple at **Jerusalem**, which after many lethal struggles had been taken with difficulty, first under the siege of **Vespasian** and later by **Titus**. He gave the task of speeding the work to **Alypius of Antioch**, who had once governed the **Britains**...* <note>The Roman provinces in Britain.</note>

<meta>The catchword "rat" at the bottom of the page completes the Latin word "curaverat" (had governed/taken care of) for the start of the next page.</meta>

### ON JULIAN'S *MISOPOGON*. <note>The *Misopogon* (Beard-Hater) is a satirical work written by Julian himself to mock the people of Antioch who had insulted him.</note>

->B iij<-

*...acted in place of the prefects. And so, while **Alypius** <note>A friend of Julian and former governor of Britain commissioned to rebuild the Temple.</note> was vigorously pushing the matter forward, and the governor of the province was assisting him, fearful balls of fire repeatedly burst out near the foundations, making the place inaccessible to the workers, who were burned several times. In this way, with the element of fire resolutely repelling them, the undertaking ceased. And he did not set aside this great hatred for **Christians** even in the settling of legal cases; for he would ask which religion each of the litigants practiced.* <note>Julian’s biographer Ammianus Marcellinus records that Julian often favored non-Christians in court, though he tried to maintain a facade of legality.</note> *Yet, even within this injustice of the prince, a certain zeal for maintaining equity appeared, in that he reformed certain parts of civil law. "**Marcellinus**," he says (in his twenty-second book), "corrected the laws for the better, cutting away ambiguities and clearly indicating what they commanded to be done or forbidden."*

*He also hated informers. Therefore, when someone accused a wealthy citizen of high treason <term>high treason: In Latin, "laesa majestas," literally "injured majesty," a crime against the state or the Emperor.</term> before **Julian**, the Emperor turned to his Count of the Sacred Largesses <note>original: "comitem largitionum," the high-ranking official in charge of the imperial treasury.</note> and said, "Order that purple footwear be given to this man to be carried to his adversary."* <note>This was a sarcastic dismissal; purple shoes were a symbol of the Emperor. Julian was implying the accuser should try being the Emperor himself if he wanted to pursue such claims.</note> *Also, when the orator **Delphidio** was vehemently accusing a certain man and, out of a lack of evidence, finally cried out: "O most illustrious **Caesar**, can anyone ever be found guilty if it is enough to deny the charge?" **Julian** replied: "Can anyone be found innocent if it is enough to have accused him?"*

*I praise this equity of mind in the prince; the events that follow have the appearance of clemency. When he was preparing for war against **Constantius**, and all the soldiers except **Nebridio** were in agreement—and for that reason **Nebridio** was put in great danger—the Emperor covered him with his own general's cloak <term>general's cloak: "Paludamentum," a ceremonial cloak worn by Roman commanders.</term>. And when the man, cast down and pleading, asked to touch the Emperor’s right hand, **Julian** said: "What special thing will be reserved for my friends if you touch my hand?" When **Lucillianus**, the prefect, was in a nearly identical situation and the opportunity was given to adore the purple...*

# PREFACE.

*...now somewhat recovered from his fear, he said: "Incautiously, O Emperor, and rashly with only a few men, you have entrusted yourself to the enemy's side." Julian, smiling, replied: "Save these words of wisdom for **Constantius**; <note>Constantius II, Julian's predecessor and cousin, known for being more cautious and suspicious.</note> for I displayed the insignia of majesty not so you could act as my advisor, but so that you would cease to tremble." Therefore, let this moderation of mind and his equity be praised thus far. But what follows should by no means seem worthy of a wise and statesman-like prince. For when he had departed from **Constantinople** and arrived at **Antioch** to wage war against the **Persians**, and decided to winter in that city, he was led by no probable reason—but only by a vain desire for glory so that he might appear a prince of the people—to lower the official price of the grain supply <term>grain supply: In Latin "annona," the state-regulated system for providing grain to the city's population.</term>. This act produced famine and scarcity for the city, and for Julian, it bred the utmost hatred. And as the people of Antioch were naturally witty and sharp-tongued, possessing a certain innate freedom of spirit, they mocked Julian in various ways.*

***Marcellinus*** <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a historian and soldier who served under Julian and provided a balanced account of his reign.</note> *says in his twenty-second book: "He was laughed at as a 'Cercops' <note>original: "cercops." In Greek myth, the Cercopes were mischievous, monkey-like forest creatures; here it is a slur on Julian's small stature.</note>, a short man, stretching out narrow shoulders, and sporting a goat-like beard, walking with huge strides as if he were the brother of **Otus** and **Ephialtes**," <note>Giant brothers from Greek mythology who famously attempted to storm Mount Olympus.</note> whose height **Homer** extols as being immense. He was also called a 'Slaughterer' <note>original: "Victimarius," a low-level temple assistant who physically killed the animals.</note> instead of a 'Sacrificer' <note>original: "Sacricola," a more dignified term for a priest.</note>, with many people alluding to the excessive frequency of his animal sacrifices. He was appropriately blamed for this, since for the sake of ostentation he would immoderately carry the sacred objects himself in place of the priests, and he delighted in being surrounded by a crowd of common women. In these words of Marcellinus, two things in particular are noted about Julian: his physical person and his religion. A third may be added from his own work, the ***Misopogon*** <note>Greek for "Beard-Hater," a satirical essay Julian wrote to mock himself and the people of Antioch.</note>, regarding the image on his coins. This was a bull lying upon an altar, by which Julian meant to signify that the ancient custom of sacrifices had been restored by him; but the people of Antioch interpreted it to mean that the world had been subverted <note>The people of Antioch joked that the bull on the coin represented Julian himself "overturning" the empire.</note> by Julian. Therefore, to such insults—*

<meta>This page transitions from the physical insults Julian endured in Antioch to his literary response. It analyzes the structure and intent of his famous satire, the Misopogon, explaining how Julian used irony to defend his character and criticize the city's shift toward Christianity and luxury.</meta>

# ON JULIAN’S "BEARD-HATER."

[These insults] were avenged by the publication of the *Misopogon* <note>The "Beard-Hater," a satirical work written by Julian in 363 AD in response to the citizens of Antioch who mocked his philosopher’s beard and his austere lifestyle.</note>, the main points of which must now be briefly set forth. In it, roughly three charges are brought against the people of Antioch: the first is intemperance, because they are devoted to games and theaters; the second is impiety, because, having neglected the religion of their ancestors, they worship Christ instead of Jupiter and Apollo; the third and last is the injustice of the magistrates, who tolerate and ignore the greed of the wealthy, which caused the famine in the city. And although in this speech the Antiocheans are actually being rebuked, a different appearance is feigned; for a constant irony is employed <term>in a Socratic manner <note>original: "σωκρατικῶς." Referring to Socratic irony, where a speaker pretends to be ignorant or humble to expose the flaws of their opponent.</note></term>, through which Julian seems to accuse not the Antiocheans, but himself, because he is unable to imitate their habits. 

Indeed, through a dialogue-like device, the people of Antioch are introduced as mocking Julian’s beard (whence he is called the <term>Beard-hater <note>original: "μισοπώγων" (misopogon).</note></term>) and indeed his entire person and way of life, because it is far removed from all pleasure, because it is too religious, and finally, because it is too severe in its legal judgments. Julian ironically confesses all these things to be his "crimes" and begs their pardon, shifting the blame onto his teacher <note>Julian refers to Mardonius, a eunuch who raised him and instilled in him a love for Greek literature and strict discipline.</note>, by whom he was taught from childhood to live temperately, religiously, and justly. He further explains that he spent his youth among the Gauls <note>The inhabitants of Roman France and Germany, whom Julian viewed as rugged, disciplined, and uncorrupted by Mediterranean luxury.</note>, men who were harsh, warlike, and ignorant of luxuries. Therefore, the essence of this rebuke is contained in this syllogism:

> *He who is at odds with the customs of others is deservedly accused by them:*
> *Julian is at odds with the customs of the people of Antioch, because he despises pleasures, and because he restrains the impiety and injustice which they strictly hold and defend:*
> *Therefore, he is deservedly accused by them.*

<sig>B iiij</sig>
<vocab>Beard-hater, Antiocheans, Julian, irony, syllogism, in a Socratic manner, Beard-hater</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes Julian the Apostate’s "Misopogon," a satirical defense in which he uses irony to mock the citizens of Antioch for their Christian faith and love of luxury. Julian frames his own disciplined, pagan lifestyle as a "fault" to highlight the city's perceived moral decay.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Misopogon, Antioch, Socratic irony, Asceticism, Paganism vs Christianity, Famine, Mardonius</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page concludes the analysis of Julian's ironic self-deprecation and transitions into a serious critique of the emperor’s arguments. It highlights the historical reality behind the famine in Antioch, citing contemporary witnesses like Ammianus Marcellinus to argue that Julian’s "satire" was actually a tool of political and religious propaganda.</meta>

### PREFACE

Finally, this ironic rebuke concludes with a serious criticism of this sort:

> *He who gives benefits to the ungrateful is a fool;*
> *Julian gave benefits to the ungrateful when he praised, enriched, and supported the people of Antioch before he even knew them;*
> *Therefore, he is a fool.*

This is the summary of the entire speech, in which most points are false and irrelevant. For the charge of "impiety" brought against the people of Antioch is based on the fact that they had rejected the superstition of their ancestors and embraced the Christian religion—which Julian, in his wickedness, was accustomed to calling <term>atheism <note>original: "ἀθεότητα" (atheotēta). Julian used this term to describe Christians because they rejected the traditional Roman gods.</note></term>. This, therefore, is a slander. Regarding what he says about their "delights" and luxury, although there was some truth to it, the Christian religion had already corrected many such behaviors. Furthermore, the magistrates are blamed in vain for the city's hunger and poverty, since Julian himself was the author of it. 

Even Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–397 AD), a Roman soldier and historian who generally admired Julian but remained critical of his flaws.</note>, though he is lavish in his praise of Julian, confirms this in his twenty-second book: *For no probable reason,* he says, *and out of a desire for popularity, he insisted on lowering the prices of all goods; when these are regulated improperly, it often gives rise to scarcity and famine. Even when the council of Antioch clearly showed that this could not be done at that time under his orders, he never wavered from his purpose—resembling his brother Gallus <note>Constantius Gallus, Julian's half-brother, known for his violent and erratic rule as Caesar in the East.</note>, though without the bloodshed. Therefore, raging against them thereafter as detractors and rebels, he composed an invective volume which he called the "Antiochian" or the "Beard-Hater," counting up the city's shameful faults with a hostile mind and adding many things to the truth.* 

These are the words of Marcellinus, from which we understand that <term>rhetorical colors <note>original: "rhetoricos colores." A term for stylistic flourishes or "spin" used to make an argument more persuasive, even if it distorts the facts.</note></term> were employed in that speech to obscure the truth. Gregory of Nazianzus <note>One of the "Cappadocian Fathers" of the early Church, who wrote scathing orations against Julian after the emperor's death.</note> mentions this speech at the end of his second book against Julian.

<p align="center">iii</p>
<meta>catchword: his</meta>

<summary>The author critiques Julian’s Misopogon, arguing that the emperor's claims of "impiety" were merely attacks on Christianity and that Julian himself caused the famine he blamed on the city's leaders. The text cites the historian Ammianus Marcellinus to prove that Julian's irony masked a stubborn and vengeful political failure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Emperor Julian’s "Misopogon," specifically addressing the emperor's hypocrisy regarding his treatment of Christians versus his self-congratulatory asceticism.</meta>

## ON JULIAN’S MISOPOGON.

*In these words:* This discourse of yours is drawn from the lies and trifles of Porphyry <note>original: "Πορφυρίου" (Porphyriou). Porphyry was a Neoplatonist philosopher and a famous critic of Christianity whose works Julian admired.</note>, in which you exult as if they were divine voices; or perhaps it is from your *Misopogon*—also called the *Antiochene* (for you give both titles to that speech)—than which nothing is more dishonorable to Christians. At that time, the imperial purple <note>original: "πορφύρις" (porphyris). Refers to the royal status and robes of the Emperor.</note> and the flatterers who admired everything of yours made that speech great; but now it is a mangled and plucked beard, mocked even by those who suffered alongside you. In this speech, you puff yourself up greatly as if saying something wonderful about the frugality of your body and the fact that you never suffered indigestion from overeating. But that you so bitterly drove out the Christians, and held such a great and sacred nation as nothing, you willingly pass over. Yet, if one man suffers indigestion or lets out natural belches <note>original: "ἐρυγὰς φυσικὰς" (erygas physikas).</note>, what harm does the public interest take from that? But when such a great persecution is stirred up, and such great confusion and innovation are brought upon the state, is it not entirely necessary for Roman affairs to be in a wretched state?

That is: *This speech of yours comes from the lies and trifles of Porphyry, in which you exult as if they were divine voices, or from your **Misopogon** or **Antiochene** (for you entitle that speech with both names), than which what could be more shameful to Christians? At that time, the purple made it great, along with the sycophants who admired all your deeds; but now it is a beard corrupted and plucked out on all sides, derided even by its own authors. In this, you swell with pride over the frugality of your body and the fact that you never fell into indigestion from over-fullness, as if you were saying something miraculous. But that you so bitterly expelled the Christians, and accounted such a great and sacred people as nothing, you gladly pass over. Although, even if some single person fell into indigestion or emitted natural belches, what damage does the republic take from that? But with such a great persecution excited, and such great confusion introduced into the republic, is it not altogether necessary for Roman affairs to be in a miserable state?*

So much, then, for the *Misopogon*. But Julian, having finished the winter and pursuing the expedition he had begun, departed from Antioch.

->P R E F A C E<-

...where, when they had come to **Ctesiphon**, the principal city of the **Persians**, he perished from an uncertain wound in the thirty-second year of his age. He was **Caesar** for six years, and **Augustus** for one year and one month. Furthermore, two oracles regarding his death are read in **Suidas** <note>Also known as the Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note>, which I nevertheless think are one, but placed in reverse order <note>original: "ὕστερον πρότερον" (hysteron proteron), a rhetorical device where the logical or chronological order of events is flipped.</note>. Thus I read in his work: *There is also the oracle given to him when he was staying near Ctesiphon.*

# ON THE DEATH
## Oracle of Julian.

The Father of Counsel, **Zeus**, once destroyed the race of the Earth-born <note>The Giants of Greek mythology who attempted to overthrow the Olympian gods.</note>,
Most hateful to the blessed ones who inhabit the Olympian halls;
**Julian**, the godlike king of the Romans,
Fighting against the Persian cities and their long walls,
Destroyed them with fierce fire and strong iron,
And relentlessly he subdued many cities and many nations.
But even the **Alamannic** soil of the western men
He seized in thick battles and laid waste to their fields.
But whenever with your scepter you subdue the Persian blood,
Driving them with swords as far as **Seleucia**,
Surely then a fire-bright chariot shall lead you to **Olympus**,
Swirling in a whirlwind of storm-clouds,
Having cast off the much-enduring pain of mortal ways;
You shall come to the ancestral court of heavenly light,
From whence you wandered and came into a human body.

*That is, There is truly also an oracle delivered to him, when he was at Ctesiphon.*

## An oracle concerning the death of Julian.

*Prudent **Jupiter** once destroyed the race of the Earth-born,*
*Most hateful to the blessed ones inhabiting the heavenly homes:*
*The divine **Julian**, King of the **Romans**,*
*Fighting, the cities of the **Persians** and their long walls*
*He laid waste with fire and hard iron,*
