<meta>This page completes the prophetic oracle regarding the death of Emperor Julian and provides a concluding moral analysis of his character. It follows the previous page's discussion of the Emperor’s Persian campaign and his transition from a mortal ruler to what the oracle claims is a divine being.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>ON JULIAN’S "MISOPOGON" <note>original: "Misopogonem," Julian’s satirical work titled "The Beard-Hater," though here it serves as a general heading for the commentary on his life.</note></header>

*And bravely he subdued many cities and nations:*
*Indeed, wisely engaging in frequent battles, he laid waste*
*To the fields of the **Alamannic** <note>Germanic tribes from the Rhine region.</note> soil of the men of the West.*
*But after you have subdued the Persian blood with your scepter,*
*Shaking them with swords as far as **Seleucia** <note>A major Mesopotamian city on the Tigris River.</note>,*
*Then a shining chariot, mixed with storms and whirlwinds on every side,*
*Will carry you to heaven,*
*The laborious care of mortal habits having been cast aside:*
*You shall come to the ancestral home of ethereal light,*
*From whence you migrated and entered a human body.*

For this reason, we have thus far described and set forth the life of **Emperor Julian**. We have seen altogether great praises of this prince in every kind of virtue; yet that supreme crime of <term>godlessness</term> <note>original: "ἀθεσίας" (athesias), referring to Julian’s rejection of Christianity in favor of paganism.</note> arising from three causes—the fickleness of his nature, the perversity of his training especially in the **magical arts**, and finally the desire for power—brought upon him a most shameful stain and a most wretched end to his life. Therefore, if anyone considers this example carefully and accurately, he will indeed love and cherish all virtues, but above all he will seek out **piety**, the <term>foundation</term> <note>original: "κρηπῖδα" (krēpida), meaning a base, pedestal, or foundation.</note> and basis of all virtues, and once known, will hold it fast, nor will he ever allow himself to be torn away from it.

# JULIAN
## THE EMPEROR

->The Beard-Hater, or the Antiochian.<-
<note>original: "Misopōgōn, ē Antiochikos." This is a famous satirical work written by Emperor Julian in 363 AD. It was his response to the people of Antioch, who had mocked his austere lifestyle, his pagan religious devotion, and specifically his philosopher's beard.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap initial 'A' featuring a central letter 'A' surrounded by an ornate pattern of blooming flowers and scrolling foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

Many songs, both stately and charming, were composed by the poet **Anacreon** <note>A Greek lyric poet (c. 582 – 485 BC) famous for his drinking songs and hymns to love; Julian suggests he lived an easy life.</note>; for it was his lot to receive from the Muses a life of luxury. But to **Alcaeus** <note>An aristocratic poet from Lesbos who wrote about political turmoil and war.</note> and to **Archilochus of Paros** <note>A poet celebrated for his biting, aggressive satire; he is often credited with inventing the iambic meter for the purpose of mockery.</note>, the god did not grant a Muse that turned toward merriment and pleasure. For because they were often forced to struggle against enemies, they used music for this specific purpose: making the burdens that divine fate <note>original: "ὁ δαίμων" (ho daimōn), referring to one's allotted fortune or guiding spirit.</note> gave them lighter by directing insults at those who wronged them. 

But for me, the law—and, I suppose, common decency for all public figures—forbids me from accusing by name people who have done no actual wrong, <gloss>Note</gloss> <note>original: "ση" (sēmeiōsai), a common marginal abbreviation in manuscripts meaning "mark" or "take note."</note> even if they happen to act with hostility toward me. Moreover, the style of education currently prevailing among free-born men has stripped away the musical quality of song; for today it is considered shameful to practice music, a pursuit which in the past was dedicated to those who were [unjustly] wealthy...
<note>The text ends mid-word "unjustly" (original: "ἀδίκως"), which continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>Emperor Julian begins his satirical essay by comparing his literary task to the ancient poets Anacreon, Alcaeus, and Archilochus, distinguishing between songs of pleasure and songs of mockery. He reflects on how the "law" and modern education restrict how a ruler can respond to those who insult him.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Misopogon, Anacreon, Alcaeus, Archilochus, satire, Greek lyric poetry, Antioch, education, Muses</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the opening of Julian’s "Misopogon" (The Beard-Hater). The previous page established the context of the work as a satirical response to the citizens of Antioch. Here, Julian begins his literary defense by comparing himself to ancient poets, setting up a rhetorical structure where he justifies his use of self-mockery instead of attacking his subjects directly.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR'S BEARD-HATER,
->or, The Man of Antioch.<-

<margin>
The introduction <term>Prooemium; the opening part of a speech or work</term> is an established form of criticism through the contrast of certain poets with Julian: Anacreon played with excellent song; Alcaeus and Archilochus even took vengeance on their enemies. Yet to Julian, neither is permitted, and yet he will do both in a certain way, while he uses the Muses to criticize himself. The opening statement <term>Protasis; the first part of a conditional or rhetorical argument</term> precedes this.
</margin>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' featuring ornate floral and scrolling vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
The poet **Anacreon** <note>A Greek lyric poet known for his lighthearted songs about love and wine.</note> composed many excellent and delightful songs, because the Muses granted him the ability to be playful. But to **Alcaeus** <note>An aristocratic poet from Lesbos who wrote about political turmoil.</note> and to **Archilochus of Paros** <note>A poet famous for his sharp, biting satire, often used against his enemies.</note>, the God did not grant Muses that turned toward pleasantness and pleasure. For when they were forced to endure various injuries and troubles, they used their Muses for this purpose: they made whatever burdens the God had given them lighter through their own insults directed at their adversaries.

<margin>
The counter-proposition <term>Antapodosis; the second part of a comparison or a response to the initial statement</term>.
</margin>
But for me, the law forbids (as I believe it does for all others) to accuse by name those who, though they have suffered no wrong, choose to be enemies of their own will. Furthermore, the habit of my education and training takes away from me that musicality of verse which is valued among noble minds today. Indeed, it seems shameful now to take up music and poetry, which for a long time now has been surrendered to the wicked and the wealthy <note>Julian is lamenting a perceived decline in culture, where the arts have become the domain of the corrupt rather than the philosophically minded.</note>.

<summary>Emperor Julian contrasts his situation with ancient poets, explaining that while they used their craft to mock enemies, he is constrained by law and decorum, leading him to choose self-satire instead.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Misopogon, Anacreon, Alcaeus, Archilochus, satire, rhetoric, Muses, Antioch</keywords>

is: Nevertheless, for that reason I will not refrain from the protection of the Muses, as far as I am able. Indeed, I have seen those barbarians who dwell across the Rhine <note>Julian refers to the Germanic tribes he encountered during his military campaigns as Caesar in Gaul.</note> eagerly embrace rustic songs composed of words similar to the harsh-crying shrieks that birds let out, and take delight in those songs. Therefore, when I considered this myself, I said thus silently to myself, and indeed with great confidence: "I will certainly sing for myself and for the Muses." <note>original: "Certe mihi canam, & musis"</note> 

<margin>
³ Thus far was the introduction <term>Prooemium; a preliminary statement</term>; there follows the ironic vituperation <term>Vituperation; sustained and bitter railing or condemnation</term>. The first chapter of the argument concerns the contempt for luxury and pleasures, first in his person, then in his life. Regarding his person, there is the length of his beard, the filth of his head, his nails, his fingers, and his whole body.

For no law forbids one to write praises or vituperations against oneself. If I very much wanted to praise myself, I could not; but I will be able to vituperate myself in infinite ways.

# MISOPOGON.

...anyway. Nevertheless, I will not refrain for this reason from what help I can get from the Muses. Indeed, I have seen those barbarians who dwell across the Rhine <note>Julian refers to the Germanic tribes he encountered during his military campaigns as Caesar in Gaul.</note> delighting and rejoicing in wild songs composed of words—similar <margin>Written as: croaks</margin> to the cries of harshly-shouting birds—and taking pleasure in those melodies. For I think, in my opinion, it happens that bad music is annoying to audiences, but by nature most delightful to the musicians themselves. 

Considering this myself, I am accustomed to say to myself with great confidence: "I will certainly sing for the Muses and for myself." This song is composed in prose, and it contains many great insults—not, by Zeus <note>original: "mà Día"</note>, against others (for how could I, when the law prohibits it?), but against the poet himself and the author. **For there is no law that prevents one from writing praises or censures against oneself.** 

Though I might very much wish to praise myself, I have no reason to; but I have ten thousand reasons to blame myself. And I will begin first with my face. For although it was not, I think, made by nature particularly beautiful, nor well-favored, nor youthful, I myself have added to it, out of perversity and ill-temper...

<page-num>36</page-num>

# EMPEROR JULIAN

### THE BEARD-HATER

->Punishing myself through my own foolishness,<- I do not even allow the theatrical chorus <note>original: "thymelēn" — the term refers to the altar in the center of a theater, and by extension, the musical and dramatic performances held there.</note> into the palace. I only admit them on the New Year, out of a sheer lack of sensibility, like some poor rustic farmer with few possessions paying a tax or tribute to a harsh master. And indeed, when I do enter the theater, I look like a man merely discharging a religious obligation.

I possess nothing; and yet, even though I hear myself called the Great King <note>The title "Great King" (basileus megas) was traditionally used for the Kings of Persia, but Julian uses it here to mock the grand expectations placed upon the Roman Emperor.</note>—as if I were a governor or a general over the whole inhabited world—I am expected to rule over mimes and chariot-drivers! 

When you witnessed this behavior a short while ago, you were reminded of that famous "youth" of yours, and of that former spirit and mind <note>Julian is likely sarcastically referencing his predecessor and cousin, Constantius II, whose reign the people of Antioch remembered fondly for its lavish public games and imperial splendor.</note>. To some, perhaps, even this behavior of mine is burdensome, and a clear sign of a miserable character. 

But I will add something even more unusual: I have always hated the race-courses, just as men who are deep in debt hate the marketplaces. Consequently, I rarely visit them, and then only during the festivals of the gods; even then, I do not spend the entire day there, as was the habit of my cousin, my uncle, and my half-brother <note>Julian refers to his family members from the Constantinian dynasty: his cousin Constantius II, his uncle Julian (Count of the East), and his half-brother Constantius Gallus, all of whom indulged the public’s love for the games.</note>. 

After watching six races in total—and not even watching those with the passion of a man who loves the sport, nor, by Zeus, with the intense hatred of one who turns away in disgust—I gladly... <note>original catchword: "apal-" likely for "apallattomai," meaning "I depart" or "I take my leave."</note>

...I depart. But enough about these external matters. Although, how small a portion of the "injuries" I have inflicted upon you have I mentioned so far? Consider my private life: sleepless nights spent on a simple pallet <note>original: "stragulo" — a rough rug or blanket used as a bed, contrasting with the luxurious couches of the wealthy citizens of Antioch</note>, and a diet that stops short of satiety. These habits produce a bitter temperament, one that is quite repulsive to a city of refinement. 

However, do not think that I do this specifically to spite you; a certain serious and foolish mistake has influenced me since childhood, leading me to wage a constant war against my own stomach. I simply do not permit it to be filled with banquets. Consequently, it has happened to me less often than almost anyone else that I have vomited; I remember this occurring only once since I was appointed Caesar <note>Julian served as Caesar—a junior emperor—in Gaul from 355 to 360 AD before becoming sole emperor</note>, and even then, it was not due to an excess of food, but by mere chance.

<margin>7 A digression regarding Julian's vomiting and the city of Lutetia.</margin>

It might be useful to recount the history of that event—not because I believe the story is particularly pleasant, but because it suits my present situation wonderfully. Once, I was in winter quarters at my dear Lutetia (for this is what the Gauls call the town of the Parisians). It is a small island, situated in the river <note>The Seine</note>, which surrounds it on all sides. Wooden bridges <note>original: "pontes sublicii" — bridges built on piles</note> lead to the island from both banks. The river's flow is rarely diminished or increased by the seasons,

I depart. But enough about these external matters.

And yet, how small a portion of my "offenses" against you have I mentioned? Consider my private life: sleepless nights spent on a simple pallet <note>original: "stibadi" — a mattress of straw or leaves, contrasting with the soft, luxurious beds of the wealthy citizens of Antioch</note>, and a diet that falls short of all satiety; this produces a bitter temperament, and one hostile to a city of vanity <note>original: "typhosei" — meaning a city puffed up with pride or conceit</note>. Rather, a certain strange and foolish delusion <note>Julian uses self-deprecating irony here to describe his philosophical training</note> took hold of me from childhood and persuaded me to wage war against my own stomach. For I do not allow it to be filled with an abundance of food. Consequently, it has happened to me less often than almost anyone else to vomit; I remember this occurring only once since I was appointed Caesar <note>Julian served as Caesar—a junior emperor—in Gaul from 355 to 360 AD</note>, and even then, it was due to an accident, not from gluttony.

It is worth recounting the story—not that it is particularly charming in itself, but because it is especially fitting to my character.

He is transitioning from a general description of his austere lifestyle to a specific anecdote about the city’s climate and an unusually harsh winter he experienced there.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

<header>EMPEROR JULIAN.</header>

It is wonderful in many ways, both in the summer season and in winter. It provides water that is most pleasant and clear to look at, and for those who wish to drink. For since they inhabit an island <note>Julian refers to the Île de la Cité, the historical heart of Paris, which was surrounded by the Seine</note>, they must draw their water primarily from the river. The winter there becomes very mild, due, as they say, to the warmth of the ocean; for it is distant by no more than nine hundred stades <term>stade: an ancient Greek unit of length; nine hundred stades is roughly 103 miles, a reasonably accurate estimate for the distance from Paris to the Atlantic coast</term>. Perhaps a certain subtle breeze from the water is distributed inland. If indeed sea-water is perceived to be warmer than fresh water, whether it be for this reason or some other cause unknown to me, the fact remains: those who inhabit the place have a warmer winter. 

Indeed, good vines grow among them, and some people have even devised a way to grow fig trees by covering them in winter—as if with garments—using wheat-straw and similar materials which are accustomed to ward off the damage caused to trees by the freezing air. On that occasion, however, the winter proved to be more severe than usual, and the river carried along what looked like slabs of marble. You surely know the Phrygian stone <note>original: "Phrygion lithon" — likely referring to Docimian marble or "Pavonazzetto," a white marble with purple-red veining; here Julian compares the color and appearance of river ice to white marble slabs</note>, which these great blocks of ice most resembled. These white [ice blocks] were large and pushed against—

but just as it is in summer, so it is accustomed to be in winter. The water is most pleasant and purest in appearance, and from it the people drink. For since they inhabit an island <note>Julian refers to the Île de la Cité, the historical heart of Paris, which sits in the middle of the Seine river</note>, they must draw their water from there especially. The winter there is most mild because of the warmth (as they say) of the Ocean.

<margin>⁸ A stadium (says Pliny in Book 2, Chapter 23) makes 125 of our paces, that is 625 feet. Therefore nine hundred stadia will make 112,500 paces—that is, 45 of our leagues—calculating a league as containing two and a half Italian miles. This is, moreover, a fair three-day journey.</margin>

For the sea is no more than nine hundred stadia <term>stadia: an ancient Greek unit of length; nine hundred stadia is approximately 103 miles, a fairly accurate distance from Paris to the coast</term> away, and perhaps some thin breeze is diffused from there; for sea water seems to be warmer than fresh water. Whether for this reason, or another unknown to me, the fact remains: the winter for the inhabitants of that land is milder. Furthermore, excellent vines grow there; many have now even successfully produced figs by a certain skill, covering them through the winter with wheat-straw as if with garments, and with other things of that kind which are accustomed to protect trees from the injuries of the weather. Thus, while living among the Celts <note>The "Celts" here refers to the inhabitants of Gaul (modern-day France), where Julian served as Caesar.</note>, like the character in Menander’s *Old Cantankerous* <term>Old Cantankerous: original "Dyskolos," a famous comedy by the Greek playwright Menander featuring a misanthropic, self-reliant protagonist. For in this city there are many dancers, many flute-players, and more mimes than there are citizens, and there is no respect for the rulers. 

For you believe that blushing is for the unmanly; while for the "manly," like yourselves, it is fitting to spend the dawn in revelry and the night in indulgence, showing by your actions rather than teaching by words that you despise the laws. For indeed, the laws are only fearsome because of the rulers who enforce them; therefore, whoever insults a ruler has, as a natural consequence, trampled upon the laws. ...[on their] advising me to cast up the food I had previously taken, I did not eject much, nor indeed was there much of it; thus I felt so relieved that I enjoyed a lighter night and was ready for any task the next day. However, the rusticity of the Gauls bore this easily.¹⁰ But a city <note>Antioch.</note> so wealthy and flourishing, and abounding in a great crowd of men, is rightly incensed: a city in which, of course, there are many dancers, many flute-players, and more stage-performers than citizens, and no respect toward the rulers.

### MISOPOGON

...laws, to deal with your equals on even terms, and to bear your superiority over them more gently; to take care and foresee that the poor should be wronged as little as possible by the rich, and because of this, to endure the troubles that are likely to happen to you often: hatred, fits of temper, and insults. Then, you bear even these things with self-restraint, without growing harsh or looking upon others with anger, but rather training your spirit as much as possible and bringing it to its senses. 

And if one were to define it as a work of temperance <term>Sophrosyne: A central Greek virtue of moderation and self-control. Julian describes it here as a rigorous discipline of both public and private life.</term> to abstain from every pleasure that is not entirely becoming—not even those that seem shameful—in public, because you are convinced that it is impossible for a man to be temperate in private at home and in secret if he wishes to be licentious publicly and openly, finding delight in the theaters. 

If, therefore, temperance is such a thing, you are ruined yourself, and you are ruining us—we who cannot endure to hear so much as the word "slavery" <note>original: δουλείας (*douleias*). The Antiochians are portrayed as so addicted to "freedom" (interpreted here as license) that they resent any moral or legal restraint.</note>, whether toward the gods or toward the laws! For freedom is sweet in all things. And what great irony <note>original: εἰρωνεία (*eirōneia*). Julian uses this to describe his own refusal to adopt the trappings of absolute power, which his critics view as a false or annoying modesty.</note> is this? You claim you are not a "Master" <note>original: δεσπότης (*despotēs*). This was the Greek equivalent of the Latin *Dominus*. Julian famously attempted to revert the role of the Emperor from an absolute "Lord" back to the *Princeps*, or "First Citizen."</note>, nor do you tolerate hearing the title, but even...

<summary>In this passage, Julian ironically mocks his own virtues of temperance and fairness, claiming they are "ruining" the pleasure-loving citizens of Antioch who find his rejection of imperial titles and theatrical displays to be a burden rather than a blessing.</summary>

...to serve the laws, to live on equal terms with your equals; if you excel in any matter, to bear it with moderation; to take care and provide that the poor are not oppressed by the rich, and for that reason to swallow <note>original: *exorbere*, literally "to drink up" or "drain," suggesting Julian must fully internalize and endure these hardships.</note> all the annoyances that are likely to have happened to you very often, such as enmities, angers, and insults.

Julian argues that a leader must exhibit this in public and private to be credible.</term>—you abstain publicly from every pleasure that seems neither particularly honorable nor shameful, because you believe that no one can be temperate at home if he is dissolute abroad and takes delight in the theaters. 

If, I say, such is your "temperance," you ruin yourself and you ruin us—we who cannot bear to hear even the name of "slavery" <note>The citizens of Antioch are portrayed as seeing any moral or legal restraint—even towards the gods—as a form of slavery (*servitutis*), preferring an unchecked "liberty" (*libertas*).</note>, neither toward the Gods nor toward the laws; for among all people, liberty is the most delightful thing.

Julian's critics accuse him of "playing humble" by refusing the trappings of an absolute monarch.</note>? ### THE EMPEROR JULIAN

...you are angry (and so many, following your lead, have now removed that name from the empire as if it were offensive <note>original: *invidiosum*. The citizens are referring to the title *Dominus* (Lord or Master). Julian famously rejected this title, which had been standard since Diocletian, preferring to be seen as a fellow citizen or *Princeps*.</note>, even those who once were accustomed to it) and yet you force us to serve both magistrates and laws. But how much better would it have been for you to be called our master, but in reality to allow us to be free? O you, most mild indeed in your eyes, but most harsh in your deeds!

Then you torture us, when you force the wealthy to be moderate in court judgments, and you strip the poor of the freedom to bring false accusations <term>Calumniandi: From *calumnia*, the Roman legal term for bringing false or malicious charges. The citizens ironically "complain" that Julian's legal reforms took away their "liberty" to harass others in court.</term>. Truly, by dismissing the stage, the actors, and the dancers, you have ruined our city, so much so that we receive no good from you, except that, having endured that hateful severity of yours for seven months now, we have sent our vows and prayers—that we might be rescued from such great evils—to the old hags who constantly hang around the tombs of the dead <note>A satirical and derogatory reference to the Christian practice of praying at the shrines of martyrs and the veneration of relics. Julian, a staunch Neoplatonist pagan, viewed these Christian customs as a cult of corpses.</note>. But we have achieved what we wanted through our wit, since we pierced you with insults as if with arrows.

...you are angry; so you have already persuaded most people, who for a long time have lived in hope, to remove this name from your rule as if it were offensive <note>original: *epiphthonon*. $^{15}$ “Rejoice in your heart, old woman, and restrain yourself; do not pour out your cries of lamentation.” <note>original: *Gaude animo vetula, & cohibe, nec funde querelas.* This is a quote from the *Odyssey* (22.411), where Ulysses commands the nurse Eurycleia to be silent in her triumph.</note>
To be sure, the poet certainly did not depict the Trojan women or men as supplicants—not for Priam, nor for any of his children, nor even for Hector himself (although he says the Trojans prayed to Hector as if to a God). But to Minerva <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena.</note>, he says, they all stretched out their hands with a loud wailing. Even so, this behavior is characteristic of barbarians and suited to weak women; while it is not impious toward the gods, it is like what you do, who praise us men as if we were gods and flatter us. It would be far better not to flatter even the gods themselves, but to worship them soberly and modestly.

<margin>The personification is set aside.</margin>
$^{16}$ Look, I am once again fashioning those little phrases I am accustomed to using among you, and yet I do not allow myself the freedom of speech I ought to have. Instead, because of my usual <term>hardness and harshness</term> <note>Julian often describes his own character as "boorish" or "harsh" to mock the way the refined citizens of Antioch perceive his strict Stoic lifestyle.</note>, I cheat myself. Therefore, someone might argue these and other such things among men who wish to appear free-spirited toward princes and even toward the Gods themselves—so that he may be considered a devoted and, as it were, kindly father—when in fact his nature is wicked, just like my own.

### MISOPOGON

“Rejoice in your heart, old woman, and restrain yourself; do not cry out in triumph.” <note>original: Ἐν θυμῷ γραῦ χαῖρε... Julian continues his citation of Homer’s *Odyssey* (22.411), where Odysseus commands the nurse Eurycleia to suppress her loud cries over the death of the suitors.</note> To be sure, the poet did not represent people raising such ritual cries to Priam, nor to any of his daughters or sons, nor even to Hector himself—even though he says that the Trojans prayed to Hector as if he were a god. Yet, Homer never showed women or men making these loud cries to him in his poetry. 

<margin>Iliad, Book 6</margin>
Instead, he says that all the women “raised their hands to Athena with a ritual cry.” <note>original: ὀλολυγῇ (*ololygē*). This refers to a high-pitched, piercing cry used by women in ancient Greek religious rituals, often at the moment of sacrifice or when approaching a cult statue.</note> This behavior is indeed barbaric and suited only to women; however, it is not impious toward the gods, unlike the behavior you practice. For you praise us men as if we were gods instead of the true gods, and you flatter us. But it is best, I believe, not to flatter even the gods, but rather to <term>serve them</term> <note>original: θεραπεύειν (*therapeuein*). While it can mean "to heal," in this context it refers to the attentive care and service one gives to the gods through ritual and lifestyle.</note> 
<margin>written as: of the sober ones.</margin>
with sobriety. 

Look, here I am again, artfully crafting my usual little phrases, and I do not even allow myself to speak freely and without fear as the occasion demands. Instead, because of my usual <term>harshness</term> <note>original: σκαιότητος (*skaiotētos*). Julian uses this word to describe his own "clumsiness" or "boorishness," adopting the perspective of the sophisticated citizens of Antioch who found his austere, traditional manners to be unrefined.</note>, I even slander myself. Someone might say these things and others like them to men who wish to be free not only toward their rulers, but even toward the gods—so that a person might act as a lawgiver for them, being well-disposed toward them like a gentle father, even if he were "wicked by nature," as I am.

### JULIAN THE EMPEROR

You endure those who hate you and revile you, both secretly and openly, since you imagined that those seen in the temples praising you were actually flattering you. For I do not think you have considered how to harmonize yourself with these men, neither in your pursuits, nor in your lifestyle, nor in your character. Well then, but who will tolerate this about you? You generally sleep alone at night, and there is nothing at all to soften your wild and savage spirit. Every path to <term>sweetness of soul</term> <note>original: γλυκυθυμίᾳ (*glukuthymia*). This refers to a state of being cheerful or having a pleasant disposition, which Julian’s critics claim he lacks due to his strict lifestyle.</note> is closed off everywhere; and the greatest of evils is that you find joy in living such a life, and you have turned common curses into a source of pleasure.

Then are you indignant if you hear such things from someone, when you ought to be grateful to those who, out of goodwill, admonish you more melodiously in <term>anapestic verse</term>? <note>original: ἐν τοῖς ἀναπαίστοις (*en tois anapaistois*). Anapests were a meter often used in Greek drama and satirical chants. Julian refers to the mocking songs the people of Antioch sang about him in the theaters.</note> [They tell you] to shave your cheeks, and—beginning first with yourself—to display all these beautiful spectacles to the laughter-loving people: mimes, dancers, women who have lost all sense of shame, boys competing in beauty with women, and men who have plucked themselves smooth, not only on their jaws but on their entire bodies, being smoother <meta>catchword: than</meta>

<summary>Julian satirizes his own asceticism and solitary habits, contrasting them with the Antiochans' demands for public entertainments and their preference for a clean-shaven, groomed appearance over his philosopher's beard.</summary>

19 The second chapter concerning religion is repeated.
20 These are Julian’s own words in this parenthesis. Daphne was a lovely and grand suburb of Antioch, where there was a magnificent temple of Apollo, which that fierce and savage King Antiochus Epiphanes built. In this temple was a statue of Apollo resembling the Olympian Zeus, which was destroyed by a sudden outbreak of fire on the eleventh day before the Kalends of November <note>October 22, 362 AD.</note>, while Julian was in Antioch. He was so moved by this event that he ordered investigations to be conducted with more than usual severity, and commanded the great church of Antioch to be closed: for he suspected that the Christians had done this, driven by envy because they were unwilling to see the temple being surrounded by a <term>peristyle</term> <note>original: *peristylio*. Days on which it was religiously forbidden to conduct public business.</note>, he once more takes up vows in the shrine of Jupiter Philios according to the customs of our ancestors. And who could tolerate a Caesar coming to the temples so often, when it is permitted to trouble the gods only once or twice? One ought instead to celebrate those holidays which are common to the whole people, and in which not only those who "know the gods" can participate, but also those <note>Meaning the Christian population of the city.</note> with whom the city is full. It would truly be a great pleasure and delight for everyone to experience, when...

### MISOPOGON
### (The Beard-Hater)

<meta>catchword: danc-</meta>

...of the women, so that they might appear attractive to those they encounter; you frequent festivals and public assemblies—but by Zeus, certainly not the sacred ones in which one must behave with sobriety! For we have had enough of those things, just like the <term>ancient oak</term> <note>original: δρυὸς. An allusion to the proverb "enough of the oak," referring to the time before agriculture when humans supposedly lived on acorns. The Antiochenes are saying Julian’s old-fashioned religious fervor is as primitive and unappetizing as a diet of raw acorns.</note>, and there is already a great surfeit of them. Caesar sacrificed in the temple of Zeus once, then in the temple of Fortune; then he went to the temple of Demeter three times in a row— <margin>It is written: *he walked*</margin> —for I have forgotten how many times he entered the precinct of Daphne, which was betrayed by the negligence of its guards and destroyed by the daring of godless men <note>Julian refers here to the Christians, whom he frequently called "atheists" for their rejection of the Olympian gods. The Great Temple of Apollo at Daphne had recently been destroyed by a fire which Julian blamed on Christian arsonists.</note>. 

The Syrian New Moon arrives, and Caesar goes once again to the temple of Zeus the Friend; then comes the public festival, and Caesar goes to the precinct of Fortune. Even on an <term>inauspicious day</term> <note>original: ἀποφράδα (apophrada). A "forbidden" or "black" day on the calendar when religious or public business was traditionally avoided as a bad omen.</note>, he returns to the temple of Zeus the Friend to offer prayers according to ancestral custom. 

And who can tolerate a Caesar who haunts the temples so often, when it is possible to bother the gods only once or twice? He should instead be celebrating those festivals which are common to all the people—the ones in which everyone can participate, not just those who "know the gods," but the sort of people with whom this city is filled! In those festivals, there is much pleasure and many charms, the kind that one might enjoy perpetually, such as seeing...

...dancing men, and children, and many women. <margin>It is written: *we call happy.*</margin> When I consider these things, I congratulate you on your happiness, but I am not displeased with my own lot; for perhaps these habits of mine are dear to me through the will of some god. Therefore, know well, I do not even feel resentment toward those who are annoyed by my life and my choice of lifestyle. Instead, I add to their insults as many jokes against myself as I can, pouring out these reproaches upon myself even more heavily—I, who through folly did not understand from the beginning the character of this city. And this I failed to see despite having read, as I persuade myself, no fewer books than any of my contemporaries.

<margin>It is written: *to you*</margin> It is said that there was once a king from whom this city takes its name—or rather, the person after whom this city was founded and named. The royal physician Erasistratus discovered the cause of the prince's wasting illness by observing his pulse whenever Stratonice entered the room.</note>. <margin>Some manuscripts read: "he restrained himself"</margin>
Such were the things he suffered while *she* <note>The prince's stepmother, Stratonice.</note> was present. But when she departed and others entered, he remained calm and appeared just like those who suffer no ailment at all.

When Erasistratus fully understood the nature of this passion, he explained it to the King. The King, because he was **devoted to his son** <note>original: "φιλόπαις" (philopais) — a term for a father whose love for his children guides his actions.</note>, declared that he would yield his own wife to the lad. At first, the prince refused; however, a little while after his father had passed away, he no longer nobly declined the favor previously offered to him, but pursued it with great intensity.

Such were the deeds done for Antiochus. As for those descended from him, it is no cause for reproach if they seek to emulate their founder or their namesake. For just as with plants, it is natural for qualities to be passed down through many generations—and perhaps, in general, later growths are similar to the stock from which they sprouted—so too among men it is likely that the **character of descendants** remains similar to that of their ancestors. Indeed, I myself have known the Athenians to be the most **honor-loving** <note>original: "φιλοτιμοτάτους" (philotimotatous). Such was the case with Antiochus. Therefore, it should be no cause for resentment if descendants imitate the grandfather who was the founder of their lineage, or at least the source of their name. For just as it is likely in plants that qualities are spread as widely as possible—and perhaps that those things which are born are entirely similar to the stock from which they sprang—so too among men it is believable that the **character of descendants** <note>original: "nepotum mores"</note> is akin to that of their ancestors. # THE WORKS OF EMPEROR JULIAN

I have observed them to be of such character and most civilized. Although I certainly saw uprightness in all Greeks, and I can affirm regarding them that they are the most religious of all and most kind toward guests; however, among the Greeks themselves, I can certainly give a more abundant and greater testimony in this matter to the **Athenians**. For if they retain the ancient image of virtue and character, why is it that the Syrians, Arabs, Gauls, Thracians, Illyrians, and among these the **Mysians** <note>A people located in the Balkans near the Danube.</note> situated along the banks of the **Ister** <note>The ancient name for the lower Danube River.</note> cannot do the same? But my own lineage comes from those very people—thoroughly wild, harsh, rustic, unrefined, and stubbornly clinging to whatever it has once decided; all of which show a remarkable hardness of spirit.

<margin>An excuse is concluded in place of an accusation.</margin>
Therefore, first I beg pardon for myself, and then in turn I grant the same to you, who follow your ancestral customs and way of life. Nor do I now bring this forward as a reproach:

...and most civilized, although I have seen a sense of fairness in all Greeks. I can say on their behalf that they are the most pious toward the gods of all people and the most welcoming to guests. ### EMPEROR JULIAN

<margin>
²⁸ A personification of the city of Antioch, where the third point of the argument is expanded upon: the perceived "injustice" by which Julian prohibits the unregulated sale of grain.

²⁹ The saying of the poet Theognis is: "Adopt the mind of the many-tangled octopus, which appears to the eye to be like the rock it clings to." <note>original: "πουλυπόδος νόον ἴσχε πολυπλόκου, ὃς ποτὶ πέτρῃ τῇ προσομιλήσῃ, τοῖος ἰδεῖν ἐφάνη." ...[by using a barber] a boy is expected to appear handsome, and to become a youth who is refined—if not in age, then certainly in his manners and delicate features. ²⁸ You, however, do not know how to live among men. ²⁹ You neither approve of Theognis nor do you imitate the octopus that takes on the color of the rocks; instead, you embrace that "Meconian rusticity," stupidity, and madness 

<margin>
³⁰ Regarding this proverb, see the Suda. <note>The Suda is a vast 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia. The "Meconian" proverb refers to a legendary figure noted for extreme, old-fashioned simplicity or dullness.</note>

Do you not know that there is a great distance between us and the Gauls, the Thracians, and the Illyrians? <note>Julian spent much of his youth and military career in the rugged northern provinces; the Antiochians are mocking him as a "barbarian" who lacks their eastern sophistication.</note> Do you not see that there are many taverns in this city? Julian attempted to fix prices to prevent famine, which angered the merchants who wanted to profit from high prices.</note></term> to the people and to foreigners for whatever price they wish. 

Indeed, the tavern-keepers complain about the landowners, and the landowners are hostile toward you, precisely because you compel them to be just. As for the officials, because they used to make a profit from both types of city misfortune (as I believe), they previously rejoiced in taking a double fruit—acting both as the landowners who grew the crop and the tavern-keepers who sold it. But now, stripped of both advantages, they are rightly enraged. Even the common people of Syria are angry, since they are unable to drink to excess, nor to dance the <term>cordax<note>The *cordax* was a lewd, suggestive dance from ancient Greek comedy, often performed by those who were drunk. Julian’s strict moral code suppressed such public displays.</note></term>,

<margin>Written in the Royal manuscript</margin>

you laughed like a **beggar**, saying that a temperate city needs bread, wine, and oil, but meat is for a city that is already luxurious. <note>Julian had attempted to regulate the price of grain during a shortage in Antioch, which led to a conflict with the local elite who controlled the food supply.</note> For you claim that to take any account of fish and birds is beyond luxury, an insolence that was not even present among the suitors in Ithaca. <note>original: "Ἰθάκῃ μνηστῆρσι." In Homer’s Odyssey, the suitors are the archetype of greedy, wasteful guests; Julian suggests the people of Antioch are even more demanding than they were.</note> Thus, because you take no pleasure in eating pork or mutton and never touch fish, you thought it was unnecessary to provide these things for others. 

You think you are legislating for Thracians—your own fellow citizens—or for those unfeeling Gauls who raised you to be as "hard as holm-oak or maple." <note>Julian spent much of his youth and military career in Gaul and the northern frontiers. The citizens of Antioch mock him as a "barbarian" who lacks the soft, refined manners of the Hellenized East. But truly, my habits do not permit me to cast my eyes about lasciviously in every direction, so that I might seem beautiful to you in body, if not in mind. But my tutor <note>Julian refers here to Mardonius, a Gothic eunuch who raised him with a strict, classical, and Hellenic education, steering him away from the courtly excesses of the time.</note> taught me, when I used to frequent the masters, to look at the ground rather than at the theater, and to value the beard on my chin <note>original: "mentum capitis." Wherefore you ought to forgive me, especially since I show you the one at whom you might much more justly be angry: namely, that most persistent tutor, who was already troublesome to me then, when he proposed that single path of austerity for living well. He is now the author of such great offense toward you, since he generated and, as it were, imprinted in my soul those values which I then tried to reject. And yet he, as if he were doing me a favor, eagerly applied himself to it, calling it "gravity"—as I believe—which was...

...rusticity <note>original: "ἀγροικίαν." # THE BEARD-HATER

### BY THE EMPEROR JULIAN

<page-num>79</page-num>

...and who he was by race that said these things? By the gods and goddesses, he was a barbarian, a **Scythian** <note>original: "Σκύθης" (Scythes). In the 4th century, the term "Scythian" was often used by Greeks to refer generally to Northern peoples, including Goths.</note> by birth, and a namesake of the man who persuaded Xerxes to march against Greece and the far-famed **Argos**. This name—which twenty months ago <note>A reference to the time since Julian’s accession or his arrival in the region; his austere habits were initially respected before the social friction with Antioch began.</note> was a name to be revered, but is now brought forward as a crime and a reproach—belonged to a **eunuch**. He had been raised by my grandfather so that he might lead my mother through the poems of **Homer** and **Hesiod**. 

Since she died only a few months after giving birth to me, her first and only child—just like some motherless maiden, a young girl snatched away from many misfortunes—I was handed over to him after my seventh year. From that time on, when leading me to my teachers, he convinced me that there was only one path; he neither wished to know any other himself, nor would he permit me to walk any other. It was he who caused me to be hated by all of you. 

But if it seems right to you, let us—both you and I—make a truce with him, ending <gloss>written as: ceasing</gloss> our hostility. For he did not know that I would ever come to you, nor even if I were to visit you frequently, that...

<page-num>80</page-num>

...[that I] would ever be a ruler, nor that I would come into so great an office as the gods have given—having forced many circumstances together to persuade both the giver and the receiver. 

For it seemed that neither of us wished it—neither the one giving the honor (or "favor," or whatever you prefer to call it) was willing to give it, nor was the one receiving it willing to take it; as all the gods know, he <note>Julian is referring to himself in the third person here, recounting his reluctant acclamation as Emperor by his soldiers in Gaul.</note> truly refused it. And so this matter has gone, and will continue to go, as is pleasing to the gods. 

But perhaps if my **tutor** <term>pedagogue: a household guardian and teacher responsible for a boy's moral conduct</term> had foreseen this, he would have taken great care to ensure that I appeared as pleasing as possible to you. But then, is it not impossible now to set aside and unlearn these traits, whether my character was dyed with something too gentle or too rustic? **Habit**, they say, **is second nature**; <note>original: "ἔθος, φασί, δευτέρη φύσις" (ethos, phasi, deutere physis). A famous Greek proverb suggesting that long-term behavior becomes as fixed as biological traits.</note> and to fight against it is a struggle. To cast off the practice of thirty years is exceedingly difficult—especially when that practice has been ingrained with such severity. 

For me, it has already been more than thirty years. Very well! But what possessed you <note>Julian turns his satire toward his own choice to involve himself in the tedious administration of the law.</note> to take it upon yourself to hear legal disputes and to act as a judge? Surely your tutor did not teach you this, for he did not even know that you would ever rule. A terrible **color** <note>original: "χρών" (chrōn). A likely reference to the "Imperial Purple," the dye of the Emperor's robes, which Julian suggests has forced him into a life of duty and legal drudgery.</note> persuaded me—the very thing you also blame as being most responsible for my...

<meta>This page continues the Emperor Julian’s self-deprecating and satirical defense of his character. On the previous page, Julian discussed how his rustic upbringing by his tutor Mardonius made him ill-suited for the refined, luxury-loving culture of Antioch. Here, he continues to explain that his rise to power was unwanted by both himself and his predecessor, Constantius II, and that his character is now too firmly set to change.</meta>

-># MISOPOGON<-

<page-num>81</page-num>

...hoped I would ever rule, especially in so vast an empire as the gods have bestowed—much against the will of both the one who gave it <note>Referring to his cousin, the Emperor Constantius II, who reluctantly named Julian as Caesar.</note> and myself who received it. For it seemed that neither of us desired it: neither he who gave this honor (or "favor," or whatever other name you wish to call it) gave it willingly, nor did he who received it (as the gods are my witnesses) accept it without a true and heartfelt refusal. But these matters were guided by the will of the gods then, and so they shall be guided in the future. 

If my teacher <term>pedagogue: Mardonius, a eunuch who raised Julian in a strict, traditional, and somewhat ascetic manner</term> could have foreseen this in his mind, perhaps he would have striven with every care and diligence to make me as agreeable and beloved to you as possible. But now, it is no longer possible to suddenly uproot whatever has been deeply planted in my character, whether it be gentle or harsh. **Habit**, as the old proverb says, **is a second nature**; <note>original: "consuetudo... altera natura est." A Latin rendering of the Greek "ἔθος ἐστὶ δευτέρα φύσις," suggesting that long-term behavior becomes an inseparable part of one's identity.</note> and to fight against it is a laborious struggle. To cast off a habit practiced for the space of thirty years is exceedingly difficult—especially one that was instilled with such great effort. 

Indeed, I have even more years than that.

<margin>34 *The objection concerning the prohibition of injustice is repeated and resolved through other reasons.*</margin>

But let it be so. Come now, what possessed you to want to involve yourself in matters of commerce and regulation? For I do not suppose that even that teacher of yours taught you this, since he did not even know you would rule. It was that wicked old man <note>An ironic reference to Mardonius. ### EMPEROR JULIAN

<page-num>82</page-num>

...you rightly accuse him <note>Referring back to his tutor, Mardonius.</note> along with me as the leader of my life and the author of my character—although he himself was surely led into the same deception by others. I believe you have often heard these names, which are so greatly mocked and derided by you: **Plato**, **Socrates**, **Aristotle**, and **Theophrastus**. Since that old man had obeyed them all too foolishly and simply, and afterwards found me as a boy already inflamed with a desire for learning, he persuaded me that if I wished to follow in their footsteps, I would become a better man—perhaps not better than other men (for I have no contest with them), but certainly better than my former self. 

But I (for what else could I have done?), having obeyed the old man, can no longer change anything, however much I might desire it. I even blame myself for not opening a free path to every kind of vice. But those words come to mind which the **Athenian Stranger** <note>A character in Plato’s *Laws* who acts as the primary philosophical voice.</note> discusses in the works of **Plato**: *“He is worthy of honor who commits no wrong; but he who also prevents others from doing injury is worthy of more than double honor.”* For the former is equal in worth to only one person, but the latter is equal to many, as he reports the crimes of others to the magistrates. But he who, moreover, offers himself as an ally to the magistrates in punishing and restraining...

Yet, out of "freedom," the asses insist on using the porticoes, and no one bars them from anything, so that their liberty might not be taken away. 

Such is the freedom of this city. O most modest citizens! I congratulate both those of you who play such pranks and those who applaud the pranksters and take pleasure in them. For it is certain that some are delighted by speaking insults of this kind, while others are delighted by hearing them. I marvelously congratulate you on such great **harmony** <note>original: *concordiam*. Julian is being deeply sarcastic, suggesting the only thing the city agrees on is being rude to him.</note>, and you do very well that you are all of this sort within a single city.

For that would be to snatch away and diminish the very fountainhead of **liberty**—to deprive men of the power to say and do whatever they please. Therefore, since you knew excellently well that liberty must be defended and preserved in all matters, first...

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR #

...to their **wives**, so that they might be for you exceedingly **free** and **wanton**. Then you permitted those women to bring up the children, for fear that we might ever attempt to impose a heavier rule upon you, and they would then turn out to be **slaves** <note>Julian is being sarcastic; he implies that the citizens of Antioch view civic discipline and obedience to law as "slavery."</note>. You fear that if they were first taught as young men to respect their elders, they might, through that habit, become more reverent toward their rulers; and finally, instead of growing into **men**, they would end up as **man-slaves** <note>original: *andrapoda*. A derogatory term for those who lack the spirited independence of a free Greek citizen.</note>. You worry that by becoming **temperate**, **fair**, and **orderly**, they might be utterly corrupted without even knowing it!

What then do the women do? They lead the children to their own **objects of worship** <note>Julian is criticizing the Christian faith of the Antiochene women. As a devotee of the old gods (Hellenism), he viewed Christianity as a soft, irrational religion of "pleasure."</note> by means of **pleasure**, which indeed seems to be the most blessed and highly-valued thing not only to humans but even to wild beasts. 

From this, I believe, it happens that you are so very "happy," since you deny every form of slavery: first, that toward the **gods**; second, toward the **laws**; and third, toward us, the **guardians of the laws** <note>By "guardians of the law," Julian refers to his own role as Roman Emperor.</note>. It would certainly be strange of us if, while the gods overlook such a "free" city and do not punish it, we ourselves were to become annoyed and harsh. For you know well that for us...

...to your wives you have permitted, so that they might be for you **free** and **unrestrained**. <meta>This page continues the Emperor Julian’s satirical address to the citizens of Antioch, the "Misopogon." Having previously discussed how the women of the city lead the youth toward their own religious rites and away from the old gods, Julian now addresses the specific insults the citizens have leveled against him—namely, their preference for his predecessor, Constantius II, and for Christ, represented by the Greek letters Chi and Kappa.</meta>

### EMPEROR JULIAN ###

...twice already a wrong has been done to the gods by the city.

<margin>
Chi.
Kappa.
</margin>

But indeed, someone might say, "Chi" and "Kappa" have done the city no harm. <note>The letters Chi (Χ) and Kappa (Κ) are the first letters of Christ (Christos) and Constantius. The people of Antioch were shouting these letters as a way to mock Julian, showing their preference for Christianity and the previous emperor over Julian's pagan revival.</note> What sort of riddle this may be of your "wisdom," it is difficult to understand; however, having found certain of you to act as interpreters, we have learned that by these letters the beginnings of certain names are signified: the one declares **Christ**, and the other **Constantius**. 

Permit me, therefore, to say freely what I feel: In one thing only did Constantius do you a wrong—in that, when he had made me **Caesar**, he did not deprive me of life <note>Julian refers to the fact that his cousin Constantius II had executed much of Julian's family, yet spared Julian and eventually named him his junior emperor (Caesar).</note>. For as to the rest, I wish that to you alone out of all the **Romans**, the gods would grant many "Constantiuses," or rather, that you might experience the plunderings of his friends. For to me indeed that man was a cousin and a friend; but after he preferred to be an enemy, and the gods most kindly settled our contest, I was a more faithful friend to him than he had hoped I would be before our hostilities began. 

Therefore, why do you think I take it poorly when he is praised? I, who am angry even with those who disparage him! But you love **Christ**, and you hold him as if he were a guardian deity...

Julian sarcastically suggests that the people deserve the corruption of the previous administration if they so truly miss it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian’s satirical work, the "Misopogon" (Beard-Hater). ### MISOPOGON ###

The gods have shared in the dishonor shown by the city. The **Chi**, you say, has done the city no harm, nor has the **Kappa**. 

<margin>
Chi.
Kappa.
</margin>

What this riddle of your "wisdom" might be, it is difficult to grasp. However, having found certain interpreters from your own city, we were taught that these letters represent the initials of names, and that they are meant to signify: the one **Christ**, and the other **Constantius**. 

I responded, therefore, by speaking with frankness: "In one thing only did **Constantius** wrong us—that after making me **Caesar**, he did not put me to death." <note>Julian is being deeply sarcastic here. Constantius II, his cousin, had executed many of Julian's family members but spared Julian, eventually appointing him as Caesar (junior emperor). Julian implies that the "harm" was leaving him alive to become the ruler the people of Antioch now dislike.</note> 

As for everything else, may the gods grant that you alone, out of all the **Romans**, might experience many such "Constantiuses"—or rather, that you might experience the greed of his friends! For to me, that man was both a cousin and a friend. But since he chose enmity over friendship, and the gods thereafter presided over our contest against one another with great kindness <note>Julian refers to the civil war that nearly broke out between him and Constantius. The "kindness" of the gods refers to Constantius dying of natural causes before the battle, which allowed Julian to take the throne without further bloodshed.</note>, I became a more faithful friend to him than he ever expected to have me be before we became enemies.

Why then do you think you grieve me by singing his praises, when I am actually annoyed by those who revile him? And since you love **Christ**, you have as your city-guardian... <meta>catchword: guardian of the city</meta>

### JULIAN THE EMPEROR ###

...that greedy one <note>original: "λίχνον" (lichnon). Julian is continuing the thought from the previous page, contrasting the "greedy" Christ—likely a jab at the church's wealth—with the traditional gods.</note> instead of Zeus, and the god of Daphne <note>Apollo, whose great temple was at the nearby suburb of Daphne.</note>, and Calliope, as your cleverness has laid bare. Julian is using them as a sarcastic comparison because they were famously devoted to the sun god Elagabalus.</note> "loved" Christ—they who set fire to the tombs of the **Galilaeans** <note>Galilaeans: Julian’s standard derogatory name for Christians.</note>! But did I ever offend any of the people of **Emesa**? Yet I have offended many of you—I might almost say all of you: the council, the wealthy, and the common people.

For the common people are mostly annoyed with me—or rather, the whole lot of them, having chosen **atheism** <note>Julian calls Christianity "atheism" because it rejected the traditional Greek and Roman gods.</note>—because they see me clinging to the laws of our ancestral worship. The powerful, on the other hand, hate me because they are prevented from selling everything for huge sums of silver <note>Julian had attempted to regulate grain prices in Antioch to help the poor, which infuriated the wealthy landowners and merchants who were profiting from a shortage.</note>. And all of you alike hate me because of the dancers and the theaters—not because I deprive the public of these things, but because I care for such things less than I care for the frogs in the marshes. 

So, do I not rightly accuse myself for providing so many handles for such hatred? 

But consider the **Roman Cato** <note>Cato the Younger, a famous Roman statesman known for his rigid morality and stubbornness. Julian compares his own unpopularity in Antioch to an incident where Cato was mocked by the citizens of the same city.</note>. I do not know how he felt about his beard, but he was worthy of praise above almost anyone else who takes pride in self-control, high-mindedness, and—most importantly—courage. When he approached this populous and...

And am I not right to accuse myself, since I have stirred up so much hatred and envy against me?

### EMPEROR JULIAN ###

[when Cato] came to that most luxurious and abundant city <note>Antioch.</note>, and saw the young men in the suburbs, sent along with the magistrates for the sake of an escort and procession, he believed that all this preparation had been made by your ancestors on his behalf. Thus, quickly dismounting from his horse, he began to walk forward; at the same time, he scolded his friends who had run ahead into the city, because it seemed they had announced Cato’s arrival to them and urged the citizens to come out to meet him.

While Cato was in this situation—somewhat confused and blushing <note>Cato the Younger was known for his extreme modesty and Stoic virtue; he was embarrassed by what he assumed was a display of vanity on his behalf.</note>—the **gymnasiarch** <term>gymnasiarch: a high-ranking city official who managed the gymnasium and overseen the education of the youth</term> stepped forward and said, "O stranger, where is Demetrius?" For old age is accustomed to bringing even the more somber men back to a youthful eagerness for listening, for which reason...

<meta>This page continues the anecdote from the previous page where Cato the Younger arrives at the gates of Antioch. Julian is using this historical example to contrast his own rugged, "boorish" nature with the perceived vanity and luxury of the citizens of Antioch.</meta>

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR #

it happens that young and old are equally fond of stories. Well then, I shall tell you how **Cato** <note>Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman famous for his rigid Stoic morality and refusal to compromise his principles.</note> replied to the **gymnasiarch** <term>gymnasiarch: a high-ranking civic official responsible for the education of the youth and the oversight of the city's gymnasiums.</term>. Do not think that I am slandering the city; the words are not my own. Perhaps some rumor has reached even your ears, spread by that **man of Chaeronea** <note>original: "χαιρωνέως ἀνδρὸς" — referring to the famous Greek biographer Plutarch (c. 46–120 CE), who wrote a Life of Cato.</note> who belongs to that "wretched race"—as people say of those "boastful philosophers"—a group which I myself have not yet managed to join, though in my ignorance I have prayed to share and participate in their company. 

This man, then, recounted that Cato made no answer, but only cried out like one stunned or foolish, "O, what an ill-fated city!" and then he went his way. Do not wonder at this, if I now feel the same way toward you; for I am a man even more boorish than he, and just as much bolder and more stubborn as the **Celts** <note>The tribes of Gaul (modern France/Belgium), where Julian had served as Caesar and spent much of his time in military campaigns.</note> are compared to the **Romans**. For he was born in Rome and reached old age being bred among his fellow citizens. But I, just as I was entering manhood, was occupied with the Celts and **Germans** and the **Hercynian Forest** <note>A vast, ancient forest in central Europe (modern Germany), which in antiquity was considered a wild and dangerous frontier.</note>, and I have already spent much time there like a hunter among wild en-

->MISOPOGON. 101<-

I think it happens that young and old are equally delighted by the hearing of stories. That man, I say, recorded these things: that Cato then answered nothing, but only cried out in the manner of a madman or a fool, "O, wretched city!" and so he departed. 

<margin>4. A summary of his excuse, expanded by the fact that he lived among the Gauls—a harsh people who know nothing of luxury.</margin>

4. For he was born in Rome and lived among his fellow citizens until almost extreme old age. ### THE EMPEROR JULIAN

->**THE BEARD-HATER**<-

...by warring, because I had fallen into those customs which know nothing of flattery, but rather how to live freely and simply with everyone under equal laws. Therefore, my first instruction after my childhood education was in the books of **Plato** and **Aristotle**; and at that time I was not suited for this fashionable life, which is so very blessed with pleasures. Later, as a grown and robust man, having gained the power to live according to my own habits and judgment, I lived among the most fierce and warlike peoples. 

These people know **Venus** <note>The Roman goddess of love and beauty.</note> as the goddess of weddings and **Bacchus** <note>The Roman god of wine and festivity.</note> as the giver of joy only for the sake of marriage and children, and for drinking wine only as far as is permitted to each. But as for the dancers, when they were allowed to perform their art in the theater, the people let them be, thinking they resembled people **possessed by nymphs** <note>original: νυμφολήπτοις; a Greek term for those seized by a religious frenzy or a specific type of madness attributed to nature spirits.</note>. 

And there, the theater was a more ridiculous sight, much like myself. But there, the few mocked the many; here, I and a few others appear ridiculous to all of you in every respect. And I am not upset by this fact; for I would be unjust if I did not put up with the present circumstances, having so particularly embraced those others. For the Celts loved me so much because of the similarity of our characters that they dared not only to take up arms on my behalf, but also gave me much money; and when I frequently refused it, they forced me to take it, and in all things...

# EMPEROR JULIAN

Perhaps it is not out of place to recount it even now. "It is a terrible thing," I said, "that so great a city should be so neglectful of the gods. Even a tiny village dwelling on the furthest reaches of the **Black Sea** <note>original: "πόντου" (pontou); referring to the Pontus region, which Julian uses as an example of a remote, rustic area.</note>, possessed of ten thousand plots of its own land, would have offered at least a bird on its own behalf. Now, for the first time since the gods dispersed the **cloud of atheism** <note>Julian's polemical way of referring to the reign of his Christian predecessors, particularly Constantius II, during which traditional sacrifices were suppressed.</note>, the annual festival of our ancestral God has arrived. You ought to have sacrificed oxen by the dozens, or at the very least, if one person could not easily afford it, the whole city should have joined together to offer a bull to the God.

Instead, every one of you wastes money privately on lavish dinners and the festivals of **Maioumas** <note>A popular and controversial water festival in Antioch known for its rowdy atmosphere and performances; Julian contrasts this "frivolous" spending with religious duty.</note>. Yet, for your own sake and for the **salvation of the city** <note>original: "σωτηρίας" (sōtērias); the well-being or preservation of the state through divine favor.</note>, no citizen sacrifices privately, nor does the city as a community. Only the priest stood there, having provided a goose from his own home. I think it would have been much more just if that priest had gone home carrying **portions of meat** <note>original: "μερίδας" (meridas); it was traditional for priests to receive a share of the animals sacrificed by the public.</note> given by you, rather than him providing the only offering. 

The gods have indeed commanded their priests to honor them with nobility and the practice of virtue, and to perform the appropriate rites. But it is fitting, I think, for the city to sacrifice both privately and publicly. But now...

<meta>catchword: you</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian expresses his outrage that the wealthy citizens of Antioch failed to provide a single animal for the festival of Apollo, despite spending freely on secular parties.</summary>

### THE BEARD-HATER

Perhaps it is not out of place to recount it even now. "It is a grave matter indeed," I said, "that so great a city should so despise the gods that not even a single village on the furthest borders of the **Black Sea** <note>original: "Ponti"; referring to the Pontus region, which Julian uses as an example of a remote, rustic area that would still show more piety than the sophisticated Antioch.</note> would act so. And though this city possesses infinite lands, when the solemn festivals of the ancestral God arrived—and the **cloud of impiety** <note>Julian's polemical way of referring to the reign of his Christian predecessors, whom he accused of obscuring traditional Roman religion.</note> had been dispersed by the gods—the city brought not even a single bird on its own behalf. It would have been fitting, above all, to sacrifice oxen tribe by tribe; but if that was too difficult for an individual, then surely the entire city ought to and could have offered a bull to the God.

<margin>
46 These festivals were held in the month of May, and a leave of absence was granted to the magistrates to go to Ostia <note>The port of Rome; the annotator is drawing a parallel between Roman and Antiochene customs.</note>, according to Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine Greek historical encyclopedia.</note>. Hence the dockyard of a larger city was called Maiouma. See Sozomen <note>A 5th-century Christian historian.</note>, Book 5, Chapter 3.
</margin>

Every one of you, in your private lives, pours out money on banquets and the festivals <gloss>46</gloss> of **Maiouma** <note>A popular water festival in Antioch that Julian viewed as a symbol of luxury and moral decay compared to the "serious" religious sacrifices he preferred.</note>. Yet for your own sake and the common safety, you sacrifice neither privately nor publicly. Only the priest sacrificed—the very man who ought to have returned home enriched by some portion of meat from the multitude of your victims. But the gods have commanded that priests only lead a life of honor and virtue as their service, and that they perform the sacred duties as is fitting. It was your duty, I believe, to sacrifice both publicly and privately. Now, however, every—

### EMPEROR JULIAN

Every one of you instructs his wife to bestow everything upon the **Galileans** <note>original: "Galilæis"; Julian’s derogatory term for Christians, intended to frame them as a provincial, backward sect rather than a legitimate religion.</note>. Once these people have fed the poor with your wealth, they display an extraordinary "miracle" of impiety to those in need (of whom the world is full). 

But you, first of all, while you despise the worship and honor due to the God, you think you commit no offense. No one brings the items necessary for the sacred rites—because, I suppose, he has nothing with which to feed himself. Yet, whenever any one of you celebrates a birthday, he prepares a magnificent feast and welcomes his friends to the most sumptuously laden tables; and yet, at the solemn festivals of the gods, no one has brought oil for the God's lamp <note>Referring to the eternal flame or lamps in the Temple of Apollo at Daphne.</note>, no libation, no incense, and no sacrificial victim. 

For my part, I do not know how such things are regarded by the "good men" <note>Julian uses this sarcastically to refer to the wealthy elite and magistrates of Antioch who maintained a facade of civic virtue while neglecting traditional piety.</note> in your city, but I believe this: they do not please the gods in the least. I remember saying such things at that time, and the God confirmed my speech with his own testimony—would that he had never done so, and had not abandoned the protection he had preserved for so many years, but rather in that storm and tempest...

### MISOPOGON.
H ij
<meta>catchword: casting</meta>

Each one of you allows his wife to carry everything out from your home to the **Galileans** <note>original: "Galilaioi"; Julian’s derogatory term for Christians, intended to frame them as a provincial, backward sect rather than a legitimate religion.</note>. Those people then use your resources to feed the poor, and thus they perform a great "miracle" of godlessness <note>Julian uses the word "atheotes" (atheism/impiety) to describe Christian charity, viewing it as a deceptive tactic to win converts away from the traditional gods.</note> toward those in need of such help. Most of the human race, I suppose, is of this sort. 

But you yourselves, first of all, being negligent toward your own gods, assume you are doing nothing out of the ordinary. Not one of those in need approaches the priests; for there is, I imagine, nothing available to feed them. And yet, when someone hosts a birthday party, he prepares a meal and a feast quite sufficiently, welcoming his friends to a sumptuously laden table. But when it is an annual festival, no one brings oil for the god’s lamp, nor a libation, nor a sacrificial victim, nor even frankincense. 

For my part, I do not know how any "good man" <note>Julian uses this term sarcastically to refer to the wealthy elite of Antioch who maintained a facade of civic virtue.</note> among you, seeing these things, could approve of them. I, at least, believe they do not please the gods either. I remember saying such things at that time, and the god <note>The god Apollo, patron of the great temple at Daphne.</note> bore witness to my words—would that he had never done so by abandoning his protection, which he had maintained for a long time, in that * storm <gloss>alt. reading: weasel</gloss> <note>The Greek text has "zale" (storm/distress), but a marginal note suggests "gale" (weasel). This refers to the fire that destroyed the Temple of Apollo at Daphne shortly after Julian's visit; the "storm" is the disaster, while "weasel" might be a scribal error or a cryptic reference to an omen.</note>

# EMPEROR JULIAN
## ii H
<meta>The catchword "στερον" at the bottom of the page is the suffix of the final word "ἀπραγμονέστερον" (more carefree/less meddlesome), which carries over to the next page.</meta>

...casting my mind elsewhere <note>original: "βλέψας ἀλλαχοῦ τῶν κρατούντων τὴν διάνοιαν"; Julian likely refers to shifting his focus or the governing part of his soul.</note>, and forcing my own hands. I made myself hated by you, acting foolishly. For I ought to have kept silent, just as, I suppose, many of those did who entered the temple with me, and I should not have been meddlesome <note>original: "πολυπραγμονεῖν" (polypragmoein); a common Greek term for being a busybody or interfering in matters that do not concern one.</note> nor rebuked you. But instead, I acted out of rashness and that most ridiculous flattery. For you must not think that those words I spoke to you then were said out of genuine goodwill. Rather, I suppose, I was hunting for a reputation of piety toward the gods and of sincere goodwill toward you; and that, I imagine, is a most laughable form of flattery. I poured out many rebukes over you in vain.

You are doing what is only just, then, by repaying me for those rebukes and marking me out for ridicule in your public spaces. For while I, in the presence of the god, by the altar and at the very feet of his statue, attacked only a few of you, you in turn—in the marketplace and among the common people—employ those citizens skilled in such "wit" to mock me. For you know well that all who speak share their words with their listeners, and he who listens to blasphemies <note>original: "βλασφημιῶν" (blasphemion); in this context, Julian refers to the slanderous jokes and insults directed at him by the Antiochenes.</note> with pleasure, sharing in that same delight, is more carefree—

...had turned away the minds of the oppressed and restrained his hands. Neither did we levy a tax in gold, nor did we demand silver, nor did we increase the tribute; rather, in addition to all arrears being canceled, a fifth of the customary taxes was remitted for everyone.

For it is said that the kite, though it once had a voice similar to other birds, took it into its head to neigh like noble horses. Consequently, it forgot its own voice and, unable to master the other sufficiently, lost both; thus its cry became more wretched than that of all other birds. I myself fear that I may suffer this same fate—to fail at both rustic simplicity and cleverness <term>dexiotes: Refers to the "cleverness," urbanity, or social grace prized by the sophisticated citizens of Antioch.</term> at the same time. For already—as you can see for yourselves—we are nearing the end, if the gods will it,

->"Since now my white hairs are mingled with the black,"<-

as the **Teian poet** <note>original: "ὁ Τήιος... ποιητής"; referring to Anacreon of Teos, a famous lyric poet known for his themes of love, wine, and the trials of old age.</note> said. So be it. But regarding my "rusticity," I ask you—by the gods and by **Zeus of the Market** and **Zeus the Protector of the City** <term>Zeus Agoraios and Polieuchos: Epithets for Zeus as the divine guardian of civic order, commerce, and the safety of the city-state.</term>—give me an account: have I ever wronged you, either as a community or as individuals? Is it because you are unable to take revenge for such wrongs openly that you instead abuse me in the marketplaces with your insults and anapests <term>anapests: A rhythmic meter frequently used in the satirical "parabasis" of ancient Greek comedy, where the chorus addressed the audience directly to mock public figures.</term>? Do you drag me about and parade me just as comic actors do to **Heracles** and **Dionysus**? Or did I abstain from doing you any harm, so that I might not...

## 126

...place you in such a position that you might drive me to the same kind of vengeance? What then is the cause of your offense and envy toward us?
