But it cannot be—believe me—that both those who pay the taxes are forgiven everything, and those who are accustomed to receive them are given everything. Since, therefore, I seem to have diminished nothing of the public distributions <note>Refers to the "annona" or grain doles and other state-sponsored spectacles.</note> which the royal expense is accustomed to provide, <margin>remitted not a few taxes</margin> and yet I have remitted not a few taxes to you, does this not seem an admirable thing, and like a riddle?

## 127

...were you forced to defend yourselves by following that same path of retaliation? What then is the cause of your offense and your hatred toward us? You must know well that it is impossible to both remit everything for those who pay the taxes <term>eisphora: a direct tax or contribution to the state</term> and also to give everything to those who are accustomed to receiving distributions. When, therefore, I am seen to have diminished nothing of the public grants <note>original: "δημοσίων συντάξεων" (demosion syntaxeon); these refers to the state-subsidized grain, oil, and entertainment the citizens expected.</note> which the imperial treasury is accustomed to provide, while I have also remitted not a small amount of your taxes, does this matter not resemble a riddle?

Indeed, previously I praised you as enthusiastically as I could, without waiting for the test of experience, nor considering how we might feel toward one another. I simply assumed that you were children of the Greeks <note>original: "ἑλλήνων παῖδας" (Hellenon paidas); Julian uses this to denote civilized, cultured people who share his love for classical tradition.</note>, and that I myself—though my ancestry is Thracian—am a Greek by my habits and way of life. I supposed, therefore, that we would love one another most dearly. 

Let this, then, be one reproach against me for my rashness. Furthermore, when you sent embassies to me—and you arrived later than not only the others, but even after the Alexandrians from Egypt—I remitted for you a great deal of gold and a great deal of silver, as well as vast amounts of taxes <term>phoroi: various imperial taxes and tributes paid by provinces</term> specifically for your city, beyond what I did for others. # BEARD-HATER

...we perceive. And these matters are quite small, and not yet enough to drive the city into open conflict with me. But then comes the greatest matter of all, the very source from which that great hatred arose: when I arrived among you, the common people <term>demos: the general body of free citizens, often distinct from the ruling aristocracy</term>—who were being squeezed <note>original: "πνιγόμενος" (pnigomenos), literally "choked" or "suffocated" by the economic pressure of the elites.</note> by the wealthy—first raised this cry in the theater: "Everything is in abundance, but everything is expensive!" 

On the following day, I spoke with the powerful men among you, attempting to persuade them that it is better to overlook unjust gain and instead do good for both your fellow citizens and for visitors. They promised to take care of the matter, but although I overlooked their conduct and waited for three consecutive months, they treated the affair with such neglect as no one would have expected. 

When I saw that the cry of the common people was true, and that the marketplace was being constricted <term>stenochoroumenēn: a term describing a market under pressure or "narrowed" by artificial scarcity rather than a lack of supply</term>—not because of any real shortage, but because of the greed of the property owners—I established a fixed price for every item and made it clearly known to all. 

At that time, there was a great abundance of other goods among the people; for there was wine and oil and everything else in plenty. However, they were lacking in grain <note>original: "σίτου" (sitou), referring to wheat, the essential staple of the ancient Mediterranean diet.</note>, as a terrible crop failure had occurred due to the previous droughts—

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR

When that supply was exhausted, I provided from my own household and gave to the city first five thousand, then seven thousand, and finally, just now, ten thousand <term>modius: a Roman unit of dry volume, roughly two gallons or nine liters</term>—as you have now become accustomed to calling them. All of this wheat had been brought to me from Egypt; I took it from my own stores and donated it to the city. Furthermore, I did not demand the price of a gold coin for only ten measures, but for fifteen—the same amount of silver that was previously accepted for only ten. 

Indeed, if that many measures were already costing a gold coin among you back in the summer, what was there to hope for then, when—as the Boeotian poet <note>The "Boeotian poet" is Hesiod; Julian is likely referencing a passage from "Works and Days" regarding seasonal hunger</note> says—"cruel hunger is in the houses"? Would you not have gladly accepted barely five measures, especially during such a harsh winter as the one that followed? Why then did your wealthy men secretly sell the grain they held in the fields, while burdening the State with their own expenses? Not only the city folk, but even the common people <term>plebs: the rural laborers and lower classes</term> flocked from the countryside in every direction to buy bread, which was the only thing available in both great abundance and at a low price. ### JULIAN THE EMPEROR

...remembers fifteen measures of grain being sold for a gold coin <note>The *solidus*, the standard gold coin of the later Roman Empire.</note> when the city was in such a state of abundance? It was for this very action that I became an object of hatred to you: because I did not allow wine, vegetables, and fruit to be sold for gold, nor did I allow the grain which the rich had locked away in their warehouses to suddenly be converted into silver and gold for them at your expense. For those men were disposing of their grain outside the city to their own advantage, bringing upon the people "hunger, the scourge of mortals" <note>original: "λιμὸν ἀλυκτῆρα βροτείων." This is a poetic or oracular phrase, likely referencing an epic source or an oracle, personifying hunger as a predatory force.</note>, as the god said when exposing those who practice such schemes.

The result is that the city has come to have an abundance of bread alone, and of nothing else. I understood this even at the time I was taking these actions—that I would not please everyone—but it mattered not at all to me. For I believed it was my duty to bring aid to the common people who were being wronged, as well as to the foreigners who had arrived here on account of myself and the officials who attend us. Since, I suppose, it happens that while some are now departing, the rest of the city is of a single mind regarding me: some indeed hate me, while others, though they were fed by me, 
<margin>written elsewhere as "they are useless."</margin>
are **ungrateful**.

<summary>Emperor Julian argues that his price controls on grain were necessary to protect the poor and foreign visitors from the greed of wealthy hoarders. He acknowledges that these policies made him unpopular with the elite and laments the ingratitude of the citizens he fed.</summary>

### MISOPOGON.

...remembers fifteen measures of grain being sold for a gold coin? <note>Julian refers to the *solidus*, the standard gold coin. By forcing a low price for grain, he angered the local elites who wanted to profit from the shortage.</note> It was for this reason that I became an object of hatred to you: because I did not allow wine, vegetables, and autumn fruits to be sold for gold, nor did I allow the grain which the rich had locked away in their storehouses to be suddenly converted into silver and gold for their own profit. For those men were selling their grain profitably outside the city, and they brought upon the people "hunger, the scourge of mortals" <note>original: "famem vexatricem mortalium." This is Julian's Latin translator rendering a Greek poetic phrase, likely an oracular or Homeric reference.</note>, as the God sang when rebuking men of this sort. 

The result was that the city indeed had an abundance of bread, but had nothing else besides. And I indeed foresaw even then, when I was taking those actions, that I would be unpopular with some; however, that matter was of no concern to me, because it was my intention to bring aid to the oppressed common people and to the foreigners who had come with me—both on my own account and for the sake of the **magistrates** <term>magistratus: high-ranking Roman officials and administrators attending the Emperor's court</term> who were with me. 

But since it has so happened by chance that those visitors have departed, and the entire city has united against me (some indeed out of hatred, while others, though they were fed by me, pursue me with **ungrateful** hearts)...

# EMPEROR JULIAN

...committing all things to the fates, I turn myself to another nation and people, mentioning nothing to you of those things which ten years ago you justly brought upon yourselves,

when the common people, with a great outcry, set fire to the houses of the wealthy and killed the **magistrate** <note>This refers to the riot of 354 AD, where the starving populace of Antioch murdered Theophilus, the governor of Syria, because of a grain shortage.</note>; and in turn, for these very actions which the populace had carried out in a justified but immoderate anger, they suffered punishments. For what reason then, in the end, are you so hostile to us? Is it because we feed you from our own household—something which has happened to no city until now—and we feed you so liberally and magnificently? Is it because we have increased your numbers <note>Julian likely refers to the expansion of the city's senate or the influx of his own imperial retinue</note>? Or because we did not punish the thieves whom we had caught? Would you like me to mention one or two examples, so that no one might think this is merely rhetorical flourish or fiction? 

You said that three thousand **acres** <term>jugerum: a Roman unit of area, roughly 0.6 acres, used here to describe the size of the disputed territory</term> of land were unproductive, and you asked for them to be granted to you; but when you obtained this, you rich men alone usurped those lands, defrauding the rest. Having investigated this, it was clearly proven. I, however, took those fields away from the unjust possessors, and nothing of...

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian’s satirical "Misopogon" (Beard-Hater), a work where he mocks the citizens of Antioch for their luxury and ingratitude while defending his own austere lifestyle and administrative decisions. This section follows his complaints about the city's reaction to his economic interventions.</meta>

### MISOPOGON

Having entrusted all things to <note>original: ἀδραστείᾳ (Adrasteia), an epithet of the goddess Nemesis, signifying inescapable fate or divine retribution.</note> **Adrasteia**, I shall depart for another nation and another people. I will mention nothing more to you of what you did to one another nine years ago—though you acted with just cause—when the common people, with a great shout, brought fire to the houses of the powerful and killed the **magistrate** <note>This refers to the riot of 354 AD, where the starving populace of Antioch murdered Theophilus, the governor of Syria, because of a grain shortage.</note>. Yet, in turn, you suffered punishments from those same powerful men for the actions you took in a justified, yet immoderate, anger. 

By the gods, for what reason then are we met with such ingratitude? Is it because we feed you from our own household—a thing that has happened to no other city until today—and we feed you so magnificently? Is it because we have expanded your **registry** <note>Julian likely refers to increasing the number of men eligible for the local senate (curia) to ease the burden of civic duties.</note>? Is it because, having caught those who were stealing, we did not prosecute them? Would you like me to remind you of one or two examples, lest anyone assume this matter is merely a rhetorical flourish or a pretense? 

You claimed that three thousand **allotments** <term>kleroi: portions of land. In this context, Julian is referring to public or imperial land that was supposed to be used to support the city's needs.</term> of land were unproductive, and you asked to receive them; but once you obtained them, all of you who were not in need divided them among yourselves. This was clearly revealed upon investigation. I took these lands away from those who held them unjustly, and without prying too much into what had happened previously...

<meta>catchword: ...ly (from the Greek -θεν, likely beginning 'previously' or 'from there')</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian recounts the 354 AD riot in Antioch and accuses the city's wealthy elite of embezzling three thousand allotments of land intended for public relief.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Julian, Antioch, Misopogon, Theophilus, riots, corruption, land reform, Adrasteia, ancient economics</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Misopogon," where Emperor Julian finishes his defense of his fiscal and land reforms in Antioch. He specifically addresses the mismanagement of public lands and the ingratitude of the city’s elite before sarcastically "thanking" them and departing.</meta>

### EMPEROR JULIAN

...tained them, though they were tax-exempt, those who most of all should have been subject to taxes, I assigned them to the heaviest **public services** <term>leitourgiai: compulsory public services performed by wealthy citizens for the state, such as organizing festivals or maintaining the city's infrastructure.</term> of the city. And now, those who breed horses for you every year hold nearly three thousand allotments of land free of tax. This was due to the foresight and administration of my uncle and namesake <note>Julian refers to his maternal uncle, also named Julian, who served as Count of the East (Comes Orientis) and was known for his administrative rigor.</note>, and by my own favor. 

Since I punish the villains and thieves in this way, I naturally seem to you to be turning the world upside down. For you know well that leniency toward such people increases and feeds the wickedness among men. 

So, my argument here comes around again to the point I wish to make: I am the cause of all these evils for myself, because I have bestowed favors upon ungrateful characters. This is a result of my own folly, then, and not a result of your "freedom" <note>Julian uses "freedom" sarcastically here to describe the citizens' lack of restraint and their habit of mocking authority.</note>. As for me, I shall try to be <gloss>somewhat</gloss> more sensible in my dealings with you in the future. As for you, may the gods grant you the rewards for the "goodwill" and "honor" which you have shown me publicly.

->The End.<-

<summary>Emperor Julian concludes his satirical "Beard-Hater" by defending his reallocation of land to support horse breeding and mocking the elite for their ingratitude. He blames his own generosity for the friction between him and the city, ultimately promising to leave them to their own devices.</summary>

### THE BEARD-HATER <note>original title: "MISOPOGON"</note> 141

...investigating the former tax exemption of those who most deserved to be **taxpayers**, I assigned them to the administration of the heaviest city affairs. And now, those who breed horses for you every year hold three thousand tax-free acres <term>iugera: a Roman unit of land area; three thousand iugera is approximately 1,875 acres.</term>, partly through the foresight and diligence of my uncle and namesake <note>Julian refers to his maternal uncle, also named Julian, who served as Count of the East and was known for his strict administration.</note>, and partly by my own favor. Since I have punished thieves and imposters in this way, I naturally seem to you to be **turning the world upside down**. For believe me, leniency and clemency toward such people only feeds and strengthens the wickedness of men.

<margin>*Conclusion of the entire reproach.* <note>original: "Conclusio universæ vituperationis."</note></margin>
Therefore, my argument now returns to that very point I intended. For I myself am the author of all my own evils, since I have bestowed my favors upon ungrateful souls. This must be attributed, then, to my own folly, and not to your "freedom" <note>Julian uses "freedom" (libertas) sarcastically to describe the citizens' lack of restraint and their habit of mocking authority.</note>. 

Wherefore, I shall strive hereafter to be more prudent and cautious in my dealings with you; but as for you, for the "honor" and "goodwill" which you have publicly shown me, may the gods return a fitting grace to you.

->THE END.<-

<summary>Emperor Julian concludes his satirical defense by justifying his tax reforms and land reallocations, ultimately blaming his own "folly" for expecting gratitude from the citizens of Antioch. He ends the work with a biting, sarcastic prayer that the gods reward the city for the disrespect they showed him.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Julian, Antioch, land reform, tax exemption, horse breeding, satire, public service, ingratitude, Roman administration</keywords>

# THE LETTERS OF
# EMPEROR JULIAN
<note>original: "JULIANI IMPERATORIS Epistolæ"</note>

<margin>
He congratulates a certain friend on his return to his homeland from Egypt.
</margin>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring ornate scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

I thought for a long time that you had already reached the Nile. And often, recalling to memory that which I am accustomed to say, I would remark: "O fortunate Egyptians, who have long had the Nile as the provider of all things which it is usually known to bring." But I said they are now much more blessed, as they also enjoy your muses <note>Here "muses" refers to the friend's literary or rhetorical talents.</note>, which are in no way inferior to the Nile, or (as I feel) are perhaps even greater. For while that river makes them wealthy by irrigating their land, you, by stirring the minds of the youth with your tongue, direct them toward the true riches of wisdom—no less than Pythagoras once did when he traveled to them, and after him, Plato <note>Both Pythagoras and Plato were traditionally said to have traveled to Egypt to study with priests and mathematicians.</note>. Therefore, I was discussing these things silently with myself, while you meanwhile, being not far away, were hiding from us. And so, when your letters were finally brought to me, at first I was astonished, and I was suspecting a trick—

<summary>Emperor Julian begins a letter to a friend, praising his intellectual influence by comparing it to the life-giving waters of the Nile and the travels of ancient philosophers like Plato.</summary>

## THE EMPEROR

->Letters.<-

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR

...mind <note>Continuing from the previous page: "suspecting in my mind the whole matter."</note> the matter, and I could not believe my eyes. But when I happened upon the writings themselves, I was convinced that such graces <note>original: "χάριτας" (charitas); Julian refers to the elegant and refined style of his friend's prose.</note> could belong to no one else. How much joy do you think I felt? For I considered these writings to be a hope brought nearer to me that I might see you again, and I rejoiced, as was natural, that you have made your homeland an object of envy—for a short time, at least. 

But you seem to me to be accusing your homeland in jest. For I would not deny that the air over your head is just as you described it, nor that the water, when one drinks it, brings the sea to mind, and that your bread comes to you as a mixture containing barley. Perhaps these things are provided to you at a lower price because of the frugality of your homeland; but nonetheless, my good friend, you ought to be grateful to it for training your mind toward **philosophy**, so that you might someday know how to look down upon **Egyptian luxury** <note>Egypt was famously associated with agricultural wealth and physical indulgence, which Julian contrasts here with the "rugged" intellectual discipline of his friend's current home.</note>. 

Even the wise **Odysseus**, though he lived on an island that was at once small and rugged—and what else was **Ithaca**? <margin>Odyssey, 10</margin> —could not be persuaded by **Calypso**, nor by a change to a better environment, to not choose Ithaca instead of those things. Indeed, I do not even think a **Spartan**, reflecting on the way of life at home... <note>Julian likely refers to the "black broth" or the famously austere lifestyle of the Spartans, which they preferred over the luxuries of other Greek states.</note>

I was suspicious, and I hardly believed my eyes. But later, when I had read the letter through and realized that such sweetness of speech could come from no one but you, how much joy do you think I felt? For I saw that the hope of your arrival was drawing near, and I rejoiced, as was only right, because you wished to show your homeland to be a desirable place—at least for a certain time.

You seem to me, however, to be mocking your homeland in jest. For although I cannot deny that the sky is as you describe it, that the water tastes like the sea to those who drink it, and that the bread brought to you consists almost entirely of barley (all of which you, constrained by love for your country, did not exaggerate), nonetheless, my excellent friend, you should be grateful to this place, for it has cultivated your mind toward **philosophy**. Consider how you might one day look down upon the luxuries of the **Egyptians** <note>Julian frequently contrasts the intellectual "ruggedness" of Greek philosophy with the perceived soft decadence of Egypt.</note>. 

Take the wise **Odysseus** <note>original: "Vlyssem" (Ulysses)</note>: though he lived on the small and rugged island of **Ithaca**, neither the goddess **Calypso** nor the offer of a better life elsewhere could persuade him to prefer anything over Ithaca. Nor, I think, did that famous **Spartan**—when he recalled his way of life at home...

There is a proverb: "You differ in nothing from Chaerephon." It is said of those who are pale and thin, as was the poet Chaerephon, because of his excessive late-night vigils. <note>Chaerephon was a member of Socrates' circle, famously depicted in Aristophanes' comedies as gaunt and sickly from over-studying.</note>

This matter, as you say, makes you a stranger in your own land, and ensures that your body is no more elegant than that of **Chaerephon**. I believe, however, that you are being held back by some friendly nymph, and that you are finally feeling—though with difficulty—how much power love possesses. But let these things be as it pleases **Venus** <note>The goddess of love; Julian suggests Eugenius is distracted by a romantic pursuit.</note>. In the meantime, may you be well, and may we greet you soon, and indeed as the father of offspring.

## TO LIBANIUS.

<margin>He requests certain writings from Libanius.</margin>
<margin>Theocritus, Idyll 12.</margin>

Since you have forgotten your promise—for this is now the third day, and the philosopher **Priscus** <note>original: "Priscus philosophus"; Priscus of Epirus, a Neoplatonist who was one of Julian’s closest spiritual advisors and was present at his deathbed.</note> has not arrived, but has sent letters as though he will still be delayed—I am reminding you of your debt by demanding payment. It is a debt, as you know, of such a kind that it is easy for you to pay and most pleasant for me to receive. Send, therefore, that speech of yours along with the "noble consultation" <note>original: "nobili consultatione"; likely referring to a specific rhetorical exercise or a "declamation" for which Libanius was famous.</note>, but by **Mercury** <note>The god of eloquence and messenger of the gods.</note> and the **Muses**, send it quickly. But know also that I have been greatly worn down by you during these three days; for if the **Sicilian poet** <note>original: "poëta Siculus"; referring to the Greek poet Theocritus, whom Julian quotes here.</note> speaks truly when he says that those who long for another grow old in a single day, then if this is so—as it certainly is—you have already brought old age upon us, excellent man,

...the first of December <note>original: "das Decẽbres" (Kalendas Decembres); the first day of the month.</note>, that enemy of the gods, **Athanasius**, has not departed from that city, or rather from the whole of **Egypt**, the cohort <note>A military unit of roughly 500 men under the governor's command.</note> which obeys you will be fined one hundred pounds of gold. For the madness of the **Galileans** has afflicted and ruined almost all things, while through the benevolence of the gods we are all kept safe. Therefore, we ought to worship the gods and honor those men and nations who are pious.

->TO GEORGE<-

"You have come, Telemachus," as the epic says. <note>original: "Ἦλθες Τηλέμαχε" (Ēlthes Tēlemache). Julian is quoting Homer's *Odyssey* (16.23), a greeting used for a long-awaited or beloved person. Here, Julian uses it to welcome the arrival of George's letter.</note> Indeed, I have already "seen" you through your letters; I have pressed the image of your sacred soul upon my mind, just as a small seal-ring leaves the impression of a great character. <note>The Emperor uses the metaphor of a signet ring to show how a short letter can reveal a person's entire personality or "character."</note> For it is possible for much to be revealed in a small space.

Even the wise **Phidias** <note>The most famous sculptor of classical Greece (c. 480–430 BC), creator of the Statue of Zeus at Olympia.</note> was not recognized solely for his statues at **Olympia** or **Athens**; he also knew how to enclose the work of great art within a tiny carving. We speak of his grasshopper, his bee, and—if you like—his fly <gloss>if</gloss>, <note>The interlinear gloss "εἰ" (if) appears to be a grammatical correction or emphasis added by a later reader or scribe.</note> though each of these was cast in bronze by nature, it was given a soul by his art.

Yet, in those cases, perhaps the very smallness of the animals granted him the natural proportions required for his systematic art. But consider instead, if you will, his **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great.</note> hunting on horseback; the figure’s torso is quite small, no larger than the width of a finger. Yet the miracle of his art is so manifest in every part that **Alexander** strikes the beast and terrifies the spectator, entirely overwhelming them with the power of his posture. Meanwhile, the horse, standing upon the very tips of its hooves...

<summary>In this letter to George, Emperor Julian uses the aesthetics of miniature sculpture to explain how a brief correspondence can reveal the greatness of a person's soul. He cites the legendary sculptor Phidias as an example of how "much can be shown in a little."</summary>

## TO GEORGE.

<margin>George's eloquence is praised by a comparison of equals: Small works often reveal great artists; likewise, George’s letters prove him to be eloquent himself.</margin>
You know how it goes in that verse: "You have come, my Telemachus." <note>original: "Veniſti mi Telemache." This refers to Homer’s *Odyssey* (16.23), where Eumaeus greets Telemachus. Julian uses it to express the joy of receiving George's letter.</note> But I have already "seen" you in your letters; I have pressed the image of your divine mind upon my own, like the likeness of some great statue expressed in a small seal-ring. That wise **Phidias** <note>The preeminent Greek sculptor of the 5th century BC.</note> was not known only for those statues which are in **Olympia** or **Athens**; he was also renowned because he had enclosed a work of great art within a tiny sculpture. <margin>On Phidias, see Pliny, book 34, chapter 8.</margin> They say his grasshopper and his bee were of such a kind, and if you wish, even his fly: although nature fashioned each of these in bronze, his art nevertheless adorned them with life and soul.

...on its hind legs, refusing to move; while it steals away the sense of motion, yet it is moved by art. This very thing you, excellent man, did with us a little while ago. ### TO ECDICIUS, PREFECT OF EGYPT.

<margin>
In this letter, he requests the books of **George, Bishop of Alexandria**. <note>George of Cappadocia (died 361 AD) was an Arian bishop whose large library Julian, a lover of classical literature, was determined to acquire after the bishop was lynched by a mob.</note>

There were many works on rhetoric and many also concerning the teachings of the **Galileans** <note>Julian's standard derogatory term for Christians.</note>. If he tracks them down with loyalty, he may perhaps receive the reward of freedom; but if he should prove himself a scoundrel in this matter, he shall be put to the test of the rack <note>original: *βασάνων* (basanon), referring to judicial torture commonly used to extract information in the Roman legal system.</note>. For when I was in **Cappadocia** <note>A region in central Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey.</note>, he lent me some of these books to be copied, and then he took them back again.

## The Emperor Caesar Julian,
### Supreme, Augustus, to the 
### People of Alexandria.

If you have no respect for **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great, who founded Alexandria in 331 BC.</note> your founder, and even before him, if you do not revere the great and most holy god **Sarapis** <note>The patron deity of Alexandria, whose temple, the Serapeum, was one of the grandest in the ancient world.</note>, how is it that no thought of what is common, human, and decent has ever entered your minds? I shall add, furthermore, that you ought to have respect for us, whom all the gods—and especially the great Sarapis—have deemed worthy to rule over the inhabited world <term>oikoumenē: the civilized, Greco-Roman world</term>...

...much on Rhetoric, and much on the teachings of the **Galilaeans** <note>Julian’s standard derogatory term for Christians.</note>. Let George’s own **librarian** <term>librarius: a slave or freedman responsible for the care and copying of books</term> be your guide in this search. If he applies himself to this task with loyalty, let him know that his reward shall be freedom; but if he should manage the matter with malice and cunning, he shall be put to the question <note>original: *quaestionem*, referring to judicial interrogation under torture.</note>. For when I was in **Cappadocia** <note>A region in modern-day Turkey where George had been a bishop.</note>, he gave me certain volumes to be transcribed, which I later returned to him.

---

### THE EMPEROR CAESAR JULIA-
### nus, Maximus, Augustus, to the
### people of Alexandria.

<margin>
He rebukes the Alexandrians for the crime committed in the murder of Bishop George. This history is related by Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian.</note> in Book 22; the letter is indeed cited by Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian.</note> in Book 3, Chapter 2.
</margin>
If neither **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great, the city's founder.</note> your founder, nor rather that great and most holy god **Sarapis** <note>The patron god of Alexandria, whose worship Julian sought to restore.</note> moves you at all, did not a regard for your country, for humanity, and for duty have the power and the obligation to move you? I add also: a regard for us, whom all the gods—and especially the great Sarapis—[have deemed worthy to rule] the empire of the whole world...

<summary>Emperor Julian concludes his pursuit of Bishop George's library and begins a formal rebuke of the citizens of Alexandria for their lawless lynching of the bishop. He appeals to their civic pride and their reverence for the god Sarapis as reasons they should have avoided such violence.</summary>

### THE EMPEROR JULIAN

...to be remembered. But indeed, you might say George deserved to suffer such a fate. I would even say he deserved perhaps far weightier and more bitter punishments. You may claim you acted in your own name. I acknowledge his guilt, but I do not grant the right of punishment to you.

For there are laws which you all ought to have observed and honored; even if those laws were being violated in private by some, it was certainly fitting that public affairs be well-administered, that the laws be obeyed, and that the <term>institutions of our ancestors</term> <note>original: *instituta majorum*; Julian refers to the traditional Roman legal and religious frameworks that he sought to restore.</note> be held holy and sacred. 

You have been dealt with most favorably, men of Alexandria, in that you committed such an outrage during my reign. For I—partly out of reverence for the Divine, and partly out of remembrance of my grandfather and my namesake uncle <note>Julian is likely referring to his uncle, also named Julianus, who had served as the Prefect of Egypt and was known for his support of traditional Roman religion.</note> who governed both Egypt and your city—maintain a brotherly affection toward you. 

Certainly, in a good and strict empire, where authority is not held in contempt, such an insolent crime by the common people is not usually tolerated; instead, it is cured like a grave disease with a harsher medicine. However, for those reasons I just mentioned...

## Julian to the Byzantines.

I have restored all the councilors to you, as well as their associates, whether they committed themselves to the religion of the Galileans <note>original: *Γαλιλαίων*. Julian used this term for Christians to highlight what he saw as their provincial, sectarian origins, rather than acknowledging them as a universal church.</note>, or however else they managed to escape the council house <term>bouleuterion: the meeting place and administrative center of a city's governing council</term>, except for those who have performed public liturgies <note>In this context, "liturgies" refers to the expensive and compulsory public services or financial duties that wealthy citizens were required to perform for the state.</note> in the metropolis.

## To Basil.

The proverb says, "You bring no news of war." <note>A Greek idiom meaning the news is peaceful or welcome.</note> But I would add a line from the comedy, "O you who have announced a gold-mine of words!" Come then, prove this by your deeds, and hasten to us; for you shall arrive as a friend to a friend. The common and constant preoccupation with public affairs seems indeed to be...

<header>OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN.</header>

...handle [affairs] quite negligently; but those who employ diligence and industry are, in my opinion, fair and prudent, and entirely fit for all tasks. For this reason, I have prepared this opportunity for myself so that, without any neglect of the Commonwealth <note>original: *Respublica*; referring to the Roman state and public duties</note>, I may also take some space for the relaxation of my mind. Indeed, we live in such a way so far that we retain almost nothing of courtly hypocrisy (which you perhaps have experienced before, where those who praise you pursue you with greater hatred than they do their worst enemies); but with an honest and moderate freedom, when there is need, we mutually accuse and reprove one another; yet for all that, we do not love each other any less than those who are the closest of friends. 

From this it happens (if I may say so without offense) that while we are relaxing, we conduct serious business, and while doing so, we are not at all distressed and we sleep securely; for when I was awake, I kept watch not so much for myself as for everyone else, as was only right. 

Perhaps I have trifled with you in these matters out of a certain lightness of spirit and insolence: for I have praised myself, just like Astydamas <note>A Greek tragic poet who became a byword for vanity after writing an inscription praising his own talent above all others</note>. 

<margin>See Athenaeus, book 1, and the proverb: "You praise yourself" <note>original: *Teipsum laudas*</note>.</margin>

For we live together not merely with the hypocrisy of the court—which, I think, you have experienced up to this point—where people praise you while harboring a hatred as great as they would for their worst enemies; but rather with the freedom that is proper between friends. When necessary, by refuting and rebuking one another, we do not love each other any less than the most intimate companions. 

From this it is possible for us—and may envy <note>original: *phthonos*; often implying the divine ill-will that follows excessive human success</note> be far from us!—to pursue serious matters while relaxed, and in our seriousness to avoid being worn out, but to sleep without fear. For even when I am awake, I watch over everyone, as is only right, rather than just over myself. 

Perhaps in these things I have babbled to you and spoken trifles, suffering from a bit of vanity. For I have praised myself just as **Astydamas** <note>A Greek tragic poet of the 4th century BCE who became a byword for vanity after he wrote an inscription for his own statue praising himself above all his rivals</note> did. At the start of the third hour of the night <note>approximately 9:00 PM</note>, not even having a secretary at my side because everyone is busy, I have barely managed to write this to you. We live, thanks to the gods, having been liberated from suffering or committing irreparable deeds. The Sun is my witness—whom I have entreated above all others to help me. And the Emperor <note>Constantius II</note> lives, as I never prayed to kill Constantius; rather, I prayed against such a thing. Why then did I come? Because the gods explicitly commanded me, promising safety if I obeyed, but for one who remained—may no god do such a thing! <note>Julian implies that the gods warned him he would be killed if he did not march against Constantius</note>. It is clear that even after being declared an enemy, I thought only to cause fear, and that matters would come to more reasonable discussions. ->JULIAN TO LIBANIUS<-
<note>Libanius was one of the most celebrated Greek rhetoricians of the 4th century and a lifelong friend of Julian.</note>

Yesterday, I read your speech—for the most part before lunch; then, having finished my meal, I completed the rest without any interruption. How happy you are, who can speak—or rather, feel—in such a way! Oh, what a speech! Oh, what a mind! Oh, what wisdom! Oh, what structure! <note>original: *partitionem*; a technical term in rhetoric for the division of parts in an argument</note> Oh, what arguments! Oh, what arrangement! Oh, what introductions! Oh, what words! Oh, what rhythm! Oh, what composition!

->JULIAN TO MAXIMUS<-
->the philosopher.<-

<margin>Maximus the philosopher corrupted Julian here.</margin>
<note>This marginal note likely reflects a later Christian commentator's view of Maximus of Ephesus, the Neoplatonist who famously initiated Julian into the mystery religions and was blamed for his "apostasy."</note>

They say that Alexander the Macedonian used to sleep upon the poems of Homer, so that he might occupy himself night and day with his books on war; but as for us, we sleep with your letters as if they were Paeonian medicines <note>original: *Pæoniis medicamentis*; Paeon was the physician to the Greek gods. The term refers to potent, divine healing remedies.</note>, and we read them over constantly as if they were fresh and new. Therefore, if you wish to represent to us the conversation of your letters—that is, your very image—write,

### Julian to Libanius

Yesterday, I read your speech—well, almost all of it; then, having eaten <note>original: *ἀλγήσας* (having suffered/felt pain) in the transcription; however, context and historical parallels suggest this is a scribal error for *ἀριστήσας* (having eaten breakfast/lunch).</note>, I returned to the reading to finish the rest before taking any rest. You are blessed to be able to speak so—or rather, to think so. Oh, what a speech! Oh, what a mind! Oh, what wisdom! Oh, what division of parts! <note>original: *διαίρεσις*; a technical term in rhetoric for the systematic partitioning of a subject into logical components.</note> Oh, what arguments! Oh, what arrangement! Oh, what points of departure! <note>original: *ἀφορμαί*; refers to the foundational premises or "springboards" of an orator's logic.</note> Oh, what diction! Oh, what harmony! Oh, what composition!

### Julian to Maximus
->the philosopher.<-

History tells us that Alexander the Macedonian used to sleep upon the poems of Homer, so that he might commune with his instructions on war both by night and by day. We, however, sleep with your letters as if they were Paeonian medicines <note>original: *παιωνίοις τισὶ φαρμάκοις*; Paeon was the physician to the Greek gods. The term refers to potent, divine healing remedies.</note>. We never cease reading them, always treating them as if they were fresh and had just now come into our hands. If, therefore, you wish to grant us an image of your presence through the conversation found in your letters, then write, and do not stop...

...doing this continuously. Rather, come to us, with the help of the gods, bearing in mind that as long as you are away, we cannot even say we are alive—except for those moments when we are able to read your letters.

->Julian to the same <note>referring to the philosopher Maximus of Ephesus.</note><-

Myth tells us that the eagle, when it wishes to test the legitimacy of its brood, carries them unflinchingly toward the upper air and brings them close to the rays of the sun. It does this as if before the sun-god <note>original: *τῷ θεῷ*; referring to Helios, whom Julian personally worshipped as the supreme deity.</note> as a witness, proving himself truly the father of the chick and disowning any bastard offspring. 

We, however, hand over our own writings to you as if to Hermes Logios <note>original: *Ἑρμῇ λογίῳ*; Hermes in his role as the god of eloquence, reason, and literary expression.</note>. And if these writings can endure your hearing, it is then up to you to judge whether they are fit to "set sail" to others; but if they are not, throw them away as if they were alien to the Muses, or wash them away in a river as bastards. 

Certainly, the Rhine River does no wrong to the Celts <note>The "Celts" here refers to the Germanic and Gallic tribes Julian encountered during his military campaigns in Gaul.</note>, for it sends bastard infants beneath its eddies, acting as a fitting punisher of an unchaste marriage bed; but those infants it recognizes as being of pure seed, it keeps afloat above the water, returning them to the mother’s trembling <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword is *χεῖρας* (hands).</note>...

## JULIAN TO ORIBASIUS <note>Oribasius of Pergamum was a Greek physician and a close confidant of Julian; he helped Julian during his rise to power in Gaul.</note>

<margin>He tells of his dream about a twin tree, by which he signifies himself and Constantius; then he complains about Eusebius the eunuch <note>Eusebius was the powerful and influential Grand Chamberlain under Emperor Constantius II, and a rival to Julian’s interests.</note>.</margin> The divine Homer says that there are two gates of dreams <note>referring to the gates of Horn (true dreams) and Ivory (false dreams) from *The Odyssey*</note>, and that their reliability regarding future events varies; but I believe that you now, if ever before, have certainly seen the future.

For today I also saw something of the sort. I thought that a tall tree was planted in a very large and spacious dining room, which was bending toward the ground, and it had a small, tender, and very flourishing little tree growing at its roots. I was vehemently distressed by fear for that tiny tree, lest it be torn up by someone along with the large one. 

And when I had approached closer, I saw the great tree prostrate on the ground, but the small one was indeed upright, yet suspended from the earth <note>interpreted by scholars as Julian being "lifted up" or saved from the fall of the reigning Emperor</note>. When I had seen this, seething and struggling, I said, "Alas, what a great tree! And yet there is a danger that the sapling born from it may also perish." At this point, someone entirely unknown to me said, "Observe carefully—"

### Julian to Oribasius.

The divine Homer says that there are two gates of dreams <note>original: "δύο πύλας". Referring to the Gates of Horn (true dreams) and Ivory (false dreams) in the *Odyssey*.</note>, and that the reliability of their outcomes differs. But I believe that you now, if ever before, have clearly seen the future. For I myself also saw a vision of this kind today. 

I thought that a tall tree had been planted in a very large dining room <term>triclinium: a formal room used for dining and entertaining in Roman houses</term>, and it was leaning toward the ground. At its root, another small and newly-budded tree had grown up, which was very flourishing. I was extremely anxious for the little tree, fearing that someone might tear it up along with the large one. 

And so, when I came closer, I saw the large tree stretched out upon the earth, but the small one remained upright, though it was suspended above the ground. When I saw this, I said in my distress, "What a tree this was! Now there is a danger that not even its offshoot <term>paraphuas: a side-growth or sucker of a plant, here used metaphorically for a successor</term> will be saved." Then, someone entirely unknown to me said, "Look closely, and take heart."

For since the root remained in the earth, the smaller tree will remain unharmed and will be more firmly established. Such is the nature of the dreams; God knows where it leads. 

Regarding that foul androgynous man <note>original: "μιαροῦ ἀνδρογύνου". This likely refers to a court eunuch, possibly the powerful chamberlain Eusebius, whom Julian despised for his influence over Emperor Constantius II.</note>, I would gladly learn this: when were these things said about me? Was it before he met me, or after? Clarify for us whatever you are able to find out. 

As for the matters concerning him, people know that although he often wronged the provincials, I remained silent, contrary to what was fitting for my character. I did so by sometimes pretending not to hear, and at other times not admitting the truth of the reports; I distrusted the obvious facts, and sometimes I looked instead toward those who associated with him. 

But when he saw fit to involve me in such shame by sending those foul records <term>hypomnemata: official reports, petitions, or memorandum</term> which are worthy of every disgrace, what was I to do? Was I to remain silent? Or should I fight back? 

To choose the first option would have been foolish, slavish, and hated by the gods. The second was just, brave, and fitting for a free man, but it was not permitted by the circumstances currently pressing upon us. What then did I do? <meta>This page continues Emperor Julian’s explanation for his actions against the corrupt administration of his cousin, Constantius II. ### ->JULIAN THE EMPEROR<-

...assuredly, I said, that man will correct his writings; for up to now he is indeed very immoderate. But when he heard this, he was so far from returning to sanity and moderation that he did things which no moderate tyrant would have done, especially since I was so close to him. 

What was a man devoted to the study of **Plato** and **Aristotle** <note>Julian frequently styled himself as a philosopher-king, modeling his governance after the classical ideals of these Greek thinkers.</note> to do in this situation? Should I deliver wretched men into the power of bandits? Or should I rather, to the best of my ability, defend those who—as I believe—having been oppressed and weakened by the wicked schemes of such men, are already singing their **swan song** <note>original: "cycneum carmen." A traditional metaphor for a final, beautiful statement or act made before death or ruin.</note>? 

To me, it certainly seems shameful to punish military tribunes <note>A rank of commissioned officer in the Roman legion.</note> when they have deserted their post—even if they were facing certain death—and to judge them unworthy even of burial; and yet, it would be even more shameful for me to desert my post when it comes to protecting miserable people from injustice, especially when we must fight against such bandits, and with God bringing us aid, just as He has prepared us for the struggle. 

And if it should happen that I must suffer something, it is no small consolation to face it with a clear and upright conscience. May the gods grant us the presence of **Salutius**, that man of integrity <note>Referring to Saturninius Secundus Salutius, a high-ranking official and one of Julian's most trusted advisors and friends.</note>.

<summary>Julian justifies his rebellion against the imperial court by appealing to the philosophical principles of Plato and Aristotle, arguing that a leader's highest duty is to protect the oppressed from "bandits" in power.</summary>

...and certainly, I said, that specific man must correct his records; for he behaves in a most shameful manner. But when he heard this, he was so far from acting with moderation that he did things—by God!—that not even a moderate tyrant would have done, even with me being so nearby. 

In this situation, what should a man do who is a follower of the teachings of **Plato** and **Aristotle**? <note>Julian frequently styled himself as a philosopher-king, modeling his governance after the classical ideals of these Greek thinkers.</note> Should he stand by and watch wretched men delivered into the hands of thieves? Or should he defend them to the best of his ability, seeing as they are already, I believe, singing their **swan song** <note>original: "kykneion exadousi." A traditional metaphor for a final, beautiful statement or a cry of distress made before death or ruin.</note> because of the law-hating workshop of such men? 

To me, it seems shameful to condemn military tribunes <term>chiliarchs: literally "commanders of a thousand," a high-ranking officer in the Roman army</term> when they desert their post—even though they deserve to die on the spot and be denied burial—and yet desert one's own post in the defense of wretched people when it is necessary to contend with such thieves. And this we do with God as our ally, just as He has ordained. 

And if it should happen that I suffer for it, a good conscience provides no small consolation. May the gods grant me the excellent **Salutius** <note>Saturninius Secundus Salutius, a trusted Neoplatonist advisor who assisted Julian in his administrative reforms.</note>; and if...

And if it should happen because of this that a successor takes my place, perhaps it will be no cause for sorrow. For it is better to act rightly for a short time than to do wrong for a long time. The Peripatetic <note>original: "peripatetika dogmata." These are the teachings of the school founded by Aristotle, which often focused on practical ethics and observation.</note> doctrines are not, as some say, less noble than those of the Stoics <note>The Stoics emphasized a strict, uncompromising adherence to duty and the suppression of emotion. Julian is arguing that the more "practical" Aristotelian view is just as virtuous.</note>. In my judgment, they differ only in this: the former [Stoic] are always more fervent and simple, while the latter [Peripatetic] are worthy of prudence, specifically in abiding by what has been decided.

## Julian to Eugenius the philosopher.

They say that Daedalus, having fashioned wings from wax for Icarus, dared to force nature through art. I praise that man for his skill, but I do not admire his judgment; for he alone trusted the safety of his son to melting wax. 

But if it were permitted to me, according to that lyric poet of Teos <note>original: "Tēion... melopoion." He refers to Anacreon, a famous Greek poet known for lighthearted poems about love and transformation.</note>, I would not wish to change into the nature of birds—certainly not to fly toward Olympus, nor out of some erotic complaint—but I would fly toward the very foothills of your mountains, so that I might embrace you, "my heart’s desire" <note>original: "to melema toumon." A term of endearment.</note>, as **Sappho** <note>The famous lyric poet from Lesbos; Julian frequently quotes classical Greek literature to signal his high culture.</note> says. But since to me...

<meta>This page concludes Emperor Julian’s "Letter to the Athenians," in which he defends his integrity against his cousin Constantius II. And if it should happen because of this that a successor is perhaps given to us, it will be, I hope, no trouble. For a short time spent well and innocently is to be preferred to an eternity of wrongdoing. The Peripatetic <term>Peripatetic: The school of philosophy founded by Aristotle, which Julian argues is just as brave as Stoicism but more grounded in practical wisdom.</term> philosophy is not, as some believe, more cowardly than the Stoic. This, however, in my opinion, is the difference between the two: that the one [the Stoic] is more fervent and more turbulent; the other [the Peripatetic] is more prudent—specifically, in remaining firm in one’s conviction.

Nature, having enclosed us in the bond of the human body <note>original: "desmō." ### JULIAN THE EMPEROR.

...to hide from view. Thus, it is fitting for you to liken silver to the gods rather than gold. To men, indeed, silver is more valuable than gold because of its utility <note>original: "chreian." Julian argues that while gold is more "precious" in a vacuum, silver’s practical use in currency and craft makes it more "valuable" to human life.</note>, and it stays with them more; it is not like gold, hidden in the earth or fleeing their sight, but is beautiful to see and superior at a distance. This is not my own opinion, but the saying of ancient men. 

And if, in exchange for the gold coin sent by you, we give back silver of equal value, do not judge the favor to be less, nor think, as in the case of Glaucus <note>In Homer’s *Iliad*, Glaucus famously exchanged his golden armor for the bronze armor of Diomede—a trade traditionally cited as a foolish bargain.</note>, that the exchange of armor was for something inferior. For perhaps even Diomede did not give silver in exchange for gold—seeing that the former were much more useful than the latter, and knew how to make the spear-points flash out like lead <note>original: "molibdou dikēn." Julian is being rhetorically playful here; while lead is a "base" metal, its weight and appearance are used here in a complex joke about the practical effectiveness of weapons versus the mere showiness of gold.</note>. 

We are joking with you about these matters, taking the cue for such frankness <term>parrhēsia: the Greek concept of bold, uninhibited speech, especially between friends or toward those in power.</term> from what you yourself have written. But if you truly wish to send us gifts more precious than gold, then write to us, and do not cease from doing so continually. For to me, even a letter <meta>catchword: beside/alongside</meta>

### LETTERS.

...to be obscured. Therefore, let me be permitted to compare silver rather than gold with the gods in your presence. For that silver is more suitable and better for human use than gold, and that it is more present to them, and is not, like gold, hidden in the earth or eluding their eyes, but is both pleasant in appearance and more convenient for life—this is not my own saying, but that of the ancients <note>Julian likely refers to various Stoic or Cynic philosophical traditions that valued utility and natural beauty over the hoarding of rare, "useless" wealth like gold.</note>. 

And if we have wished to repay the gold coin sent by you with silver, do not think on that account that you have been treated with a lesser gift, nor imagine that what happened to Glaucus <note>A reference to the *Iliad*, where Glaucus foolishly exchanges his golden armor for the bronze armor of Diomedes.</note> in the exchange of armor has happened to you in this expression of gratitude. For perhaps even Diomedes himself did not give silver armor for gold—since those former were far more useful than the latter, and better suited for blunting edges <note>original: "retundendas... acies." Julian is making a playful, if scientifically questionable, argument that "harder" or more practical metals are better in battle than soft, showy gold.</note> just like lead. 

We have joked with you about these things, having found in what you yourself wrote an opportunity for speaking more freely to you <term>parrhēsia: the Greek tradition of frank, uninhibited speech, especially between friends or toward those in power.</term>. If you wish to bestow upon us gifts more precious than gold, then write, and do so constantly. For to me...

### JULIAN TO EUSTOCHIUS.

<margin>He invites a friend to the honor of the consulship.</margin>
The wise Hesiod <note>Julian refers to the Greek poet Hesiod, specifically *Works and Days* 342-345, where he advises inviting neighbors to a feast.</note> considers that neighbors ought to be invited to banquets so that they may rejoice together, since when some disturbance or unexpected misfortune falls, they also bear the common grief. But I, for my part, believe that friends, not neighbors, should be invited. For what reason? Because a neighbor can sometimes be an enemy; but a friend can no more be an enemy than what is white can be black, or what is hot can be cold. Truly, that you have been a friend not only at this time, but for a long time before, and have followed us thereafter with that same goodwill—if nothing else—is certainly testified by the fact that we love you so vehemently. Come, therefore, so that you may fulfill the honor of the Consulship <term>Consulatus: the highest political office in the Roman state, which by Julian's time was largely honorary but carried immense prestige.</term>. You shall make use of the public horses <note>original: "publicis equis." This refers to the *cursus publicus*, the state-run postal and transport system reserved for high officials and those with imperial warrants.</note>, specifically one and a second to accompany you. And if there is anything else to be desired, we have summoned Enodia to be your friend, and Enodius as well <note>Julian is making a scholarly pun here. *Enodia* and *Enodius* are epithets for deities of the paths or roads (like Hecate or Hermes); he is essentially wishing Eustochius "godspeed" for his journey.</note>.

# LETTERS. 191

->JULIAN TO EUSTOCHIUS<-

<margin>He invites a friend to the honor of the consulship.</margin>
The wise Hesiod <note>Julian refers to the poet Hesiod’s *Works and Days*, lines 342-345, which discusses social duties toward neighbors.</note> thinks it best to invite one’s neighbors to festivals, on the grounds that they will share in the joy, so that they might also run to help and share in the anxiety when some unexpected trouble falls. But I say that one ought to invite friends, not merely neighbors. The reason is that it is possible to have a neighbor who is an enemy; but one can no more have a friend who is an enemy than white can be black, or heat can be cold. 

Truly, that you are a friend to us—not only now, but since long ago—and have continued to be well-disposed toward us, would be clearly proven by the very way we feel and are disposed toward you, even if there were no other evidence. Come, therefore, to share in the consulship <term>Consulship: The highest elective office in the Roman Republic, which remained a position of immense social prestige and ceremonial power under the Emperors.</term> yourself. The public post <note>original: "δημόσιος δρόμος" (dēmosios dromos). This refers to the *Cursus Publicus*, the state-run courier and transportation system of the Roman Empire.</note> will suffice for you, using one carriage and a spare horse. And if it is necessary to pray for anything, we have called upon Enodia <note>original: "Ενοδία" (Enodia). An epithet for the goddess Hecate as "Lady of the Roads."</note> to be gracious to you, and we have invoked Enodios <note>original: "Ενόδιος" (Enodios). Likely an epithet for Hermes as the god of travelers. Julian is making a sophisticated pun: since he is asking his friend to travel, he prays to the "Gods of the Road."</note> as well.

LETTERS.

...that a pious and religious man is proven by time. But indeed, you will say, Penelope’s love and loyalty toward her husband was also made known by time. And who, I ask, would place a woman’s love for her husband before religion and piety without immediately being judged to have drunk much mandrake? <term>Mandragora: Mandrake; used in antiquity as an anesthetic or narcotic. Julian is suggesting that anyone who values domestic loyalty over divine devotion must be drugged or delusional.</term>

EMPEROR JULIAN.

...for even if I had seen you ten times, I would not have given as much credit to the evidence of my eyes as I now give to my ears. ### LETTERS.

...for my ears are more to me than my eyes. # JULIAN THE EMPEROR

...such he was. For him, indeed, since he has departed to the blessed dead <note>original: "μακαρίτης" (*makaritēs*). Others believe that festivals are defined by wines; but I, as a sweet token for you of a pleasant feast-day, am sending one hundred long-stemmed dried figs <term>ischades: dried figs</term> <note>original: "ἰσχάδων" (*ischadōn*). These were a staple luxury in the ancient Mediterranean, often used in religious festivals.</note> of the local variety. In the size of the gift, I provide only a small pleasure, but perhaps in its beauty, it will be sufficient. Indeed, to **Aristophanes** <note>The famous Athenian comic playwright (c. 446–386 BCE).</note>, it seems that dried figs are the sweetest of all things except honey—and he does not even allow honey to be the sweeter of the two, as he says when rendering his judgment. And to **Herodotus** the historian, it was enough, when demonstrating a state of true desolation, to say: "Among whom there are neither figs, nor any other good thing" <note>A reference to Herodotus, *Histories* 1.71, describing the poverty of the Persians before their conquests.</note>. It is as if no other good thing among fruits were superior...

### JULIAN THE EMPEROR

...[thing] be found in the fig, nor where there is a fig should any good thing be found wanting. That wise **Homer** <note>The legendary Greek epic poet.</note> praises other fruits partly for their size, and partly for their color or beauty; yet to the fig alone does he attribute the name of sweetness. He even calls honey "yellow," fearing lest he imprudently call that "sweet" which is very often accustomed to be bitter. But to the fig alone he surely and properly grants this title, just as he does to nectar, because the fig alone among all fruits is sweet. **Hippocrates** <note>The famous Greek physician (c. 460–370 BCE), considered the father of medicine.</note> says that honey is sweet to the sense but bitter in smell, and I do not disagree with him; for by universal consensus it generates bile and converts the bodily <term>humors: the four fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that ancient medicine believed controlled human health and temperament</term> into a contrary flavor. This fact demonstrates that honey is, by its very nature, actually bitter. For it would never be changed into a bitter nature unless that quality had been within it from the start, from which it merely moved into a different state. For this reason **Homer** <note>The legendary Greek epic poet, author of the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey*.</note> says that in the garden of **Alcinous** <note>The King of the Phaeacians in Homer's *Odyssey*; his mythical gardens were famous for their perpetual harvest where fruit was always ripe regardless of the season.</note>, fruits grow old one upon another <note>original: "fructus alios super aliis senescere." While this might perhaps seem like a poetic fable when applied to other trees, in the case of the fig it is certainly a statement in harmony with truth, because this fruit, more than all others, is preserved for a <term>great length of time: "diuturnitatem," referring here to the fig's exceptional shelf-life, particularly when dried for storage</term>.

And although such is the nature of the fig, I believe, yet a far more excellent variety grows among us. When compared with other types of plants, ours easily surpasses them all; and even when compared to the fig species itself, our own variety altogether excels. It surpasses all other varieties in its class, and though it may be surpassed by our specific local kind, in this comparison with both, its reputation is maintained—both because it excels others as is fitting, and because in that respect where it is surpassed, it still triumphs over the entire genus. And indeed, not without merit...

...thrown together in one place, nor are they dried in the sun in heaps or in a confused mass. And regarding their origin, sweetness, appearance, preparation, and usefulness, our letter has thus far played its part. Next, let us show that the number **one hundred** is more noble than all other numbers, and that the perfection of all numbers is contained within it. 

Indeed, I am not unaware that the ancients—and these were wise men <note>Julian refers to the Pythagoreans, for whom numbers were divine and "odd" numbers were associated with the limited, the masculine, and the good.</note>—preferred the **odd** <term>impar: An odd number.</term> number to the **even** <term>par: An even number.</term>, and considered that which cannot be divided into two parts to be the source of increase. For they argued that what is like another always remains just as its fellow is; but when there are two numbers, the third element is the **inequality** <note>original: "imparilitatem." In Pythagorean thought, the "odd" factor is what allows for progression beyond simple duality.</note>. 

But I shall speak perhaps more boldly, yet I shall speak nonetheless. All numbers are joined from a single principle: and...

### EMPEROR JULIAN’S LETTERS

-> 210 <-

...can be increased by any number at all, yet it is better to attribute the cause of increase to an **even** <term>par: An even number.</term> number rather than an **odd** <term>impar: An odd number.</term> one. For the odd number only knows the one, if it has nothing with which it can be equally compared; but the association of the **binary** <term>binarius: The number two; in Pythagorean thought, the "dyad" represents the first departure from unity and the source of multiplicity.</term> produces a double inequality, and therefore it exists more fully thereafter from two numbers. And again, when the binary is joined to itself, there is the increase of the **quaternary** <term>quaternarius: The number four; for the Pythagoreans, the "tetrad" was sacred as it completes the decad (1+2+3+4=10).</term>. Generally, the conjunction of numbers within themselves, showing the inequality arising from the one, is concluded in the number of the binary.

This being established, I say first that when the **ten** <term>denarius: The number ten; the "decad."</term> is multiplied <note>original: "circunducto." Literally "led around," a geometric metaphor for multiplication or completion of a cycle.</note> by itself, the whole **hundred** <term>centenarium: The number one hundred.</term> is produced. Thus, from the one [unit] comes ten, and from these [tens] returning into themselves, a hundred is generated. From here also, from the hundreds, the whole sum of numbers takes its strength, while the **unity** <note>original: "unitate." The Monad, or the divine "One" from which all existence flows.</note> in the meantime is by no means idle, except when the mixing of the binary creates inequality...

### LETTERS.

-> 211 <-

O ij

...might receive [the benefits] of increase continually. Indeed, it is far more just to attribute the cause of increase to the **even** <term>artios: An even number; in Pythagorean thought, even numbers were often seen as feminine and receptive, facilitating the "growth" of the series.</term> rather than the **odd** <term>perittos: An odd number; viewed as masculine, limiting, and indivisible.</term>. For the number one would not be odd, having no place where it could become odd <note>Julian is following the mathematical theory that the Monad (One) is not itself an odd number, but rather the "father" or source of all numbers, both even and odd.</note>. But the **union** <note>original: "syzygia." A pairing or yoking together, often used for cosmic or mathematical conjunctions.</note> of the **dyad** <term>dyas: The number two; the principle of duality and the first step away from the absolute unity of the One.</term> gives birth to a double oddness, and from these two numbers, the third naturally comes to increase. 

And again, in the mixing of another dyad, it takes the superiority of the **tetrad** <term>tetras: The number four; for Pythagoreans, this was a sacred number that completed the decad (1+2+3+4=10).</term>. And generally, their communion with one another, revealing the oddness arising from each, is enclosed within the number of the dyad. 

This being established, I would say, I think, that as the first **decad** <term>dekas: The number ten; the "perfect number" because it contains all the primary numerical ratios.</term> circles back into itself, the whole passes over into the number of the **hundred** <term>hekatontas: The number one hundred.</term>. Thus, for the **unit** <note>original: "heni." The number one or the Monad.</note>, the increase extends to ten; and again, the decad, rising through itself, is completed into the number one hundred. And from there, again, the whole sum of numbers reaps its **power** <term>dynamis: In mathematics, this can refer to the value or potential of a number; here, it refers to the strength of the centenary as a foundation for further counting.</term> from the hundreds; nor is the unit at rest, unless the dyad, in its mixing, always brings the oddness into a circle...

### LETTERS OF EMPEROR JULIAN.

-> 212 <-

...and being recalled back into itself, until another **hundred** <term>hekatontas: The number one hundred. In Pythagorean thought, since 10 is the perfect number of the first order, 100 (10 x 10) is the perfection of the second order.</term> again encloses the gathered sum. By attaching perfection to it, the number creeps forward again from that point to the next, always referring the whole back to the infinity of comprehension through the repeated powers of the hundreds.

It seems to me that **Homer** <note>Julian, like many Neoplatonists, viewed Homer not just as a poet but as a theologian whose verses contained hidden (allegorical) truths about the gods.</note> did not simply or idly place the **hundred-tasseled aegis** <note>original: "hekatontathysanon aigida." In the *Iliad*, Zeus's shield or cloak is described as having a hundred golden tassels, each worth a hundred oxen.</note> around **Zeus** in his epic verses. Instead, he hints at this through a superior and secret doctrine, suggesting that one should attach the most perfect of numbers to the most perfect god, for he is the only one who might be more justly adorned by it than any other. 

Or perhaps it is because the number one hundred circumscribes the entire **cosmos** <term>kosmos: The ordered universe; Julian suggests the circularity of the number 100 mirrors the circular, perfect shape of the world.</term>—which Homer has visualized in the circular shape of the aegis—thus applying the understanding of the whole **intelligible realm** <note>The "intelligible" refers to the higher, non-physical reality of divine forms that the physical world reflects.</note> to the hundred by means of its circular nature.

This same reasoning also seats the **hundred-handed** **Briareus** <note>A giant of Greek myth who remained loyal to Zeus and helped him suppress the revolt of the other Olympians; his hundred hands symbolize his immense, divine utility.</note> as an assistant to Zeus, and allows him to rival the power of the Father, as if the perfection of his strength were contained within the perfection of that number [one hundred].

Literally a sacrifice of one hundred oxen, the highest form of religious offering.</note> offered to the gods, the temples of a hundred feet, the altars with a hundred foundations, the banquet halls capable of receiving a hundred guests, the fields of a hundred **plethra** <note>original: ἑκατονταπλέθρους. A *plethron* was a Greek unit of measure, approximately 100 feet; a field of this size denoted significant wealth.</note>, and all those things, both divine and human, which have been encompassed by the title of this number. 

Indeed, this number knows how to adorn both military and peaceful order; it brightens the rank of the **centurion** <note>original: ἑκατόντανδρον λοχαγίαν. A military commander of a "century" or one hundred men.</note> leading a hundred men, and it has brought you judges to a title of the same value <note>Julian likely refers to the *Centumviri* ("The Hundred Men"), a high-ranking Roman court that handled civil cases, particularly inheritance.</note>. 

Although I have many more things than these to say, the "law of the letter" <note>A rhetorical convention stating that a letter should remain brief and not transform into a full-scale treatise or oration.</note> turns me back. I ask that you grant pardon to my discourse, for even these points have been spoken in greater number than is sufficient. And if my speech maintains a sense of moderation before you as its judge—making it a worthy endeavor—it will certainly be brought before others as well, once it has received the testimony of your favorable vote. But if it happens to require another...

## JULIAN TO THE JEWS
### To the Community, if this letter is genuine. <note>Early modern editors often added "if genuine" (*si vera est*) to this letter, as its support for the Jewish people was seen as controversial or potentially a forgery by later Christian scholars, though most modern historians accept it as authentic.</note>

Much of this I witnessed with my own eyes, and I have perceived even more from the very documents which were kept as evidence against you. Furthermore, I have prohibited the tax that was already being prepared against you once again; I have suppressed this detestable impiety <note>Julian uses "impiety" (*impietas*) here to describe the previous administration's unfair treatment and financial extortion of the Jews.</note> and ordered the records to be burned which were kept in my archives for the purpose of oppressing you, so that no one may hereafter have the opportunity to spread rumors of such impiety against you. ## JULIAN TO THE COMMUNITY OF THE JEWS,
->if genuine.<- <note>Early modern editors often added "if genuine" (*si vera est*) to this letter. Because Julian—the last pagan emperor—expressed such strong support for the Jews and promised to rebuild the Temple in Jerusalem, some later Christian scholars suspected it was a forgery, though most modern historians believe it reflects Julian's actual policy of undermining Christianity by favoring other groups.</note>

In former times, the yoke of servitude was made much more burdensome for you by the fact that you were subjected to unannounced tax demands <note>original: *diagrafais*. These were supplementary tax assessments or "descriptions" of property that could be used to extract more wealth.</note> and forced to contribute an unspeakable amount of gold to the accounts of the treasury. Much of this I witnessed with my own eyes, and I have learned even more by finding the <term>brevia</term> <note>original: *βρέβια* (*brevia*), referring to official lists or tax registers.</note> which were kept as evidence against you. Furthermore, when a new levy was about to be ordered against you again, I blocked it. I have forcibly extinguished the impiety of such ill-repute here, and I have committed to the flames the <term>brevia</term> <note>tax registers</note> stored against you in my <term>skrinia</term> <note>original: *σκρινίοις* (*skrinia*), the official document chests or filing cabinets of the Roman imperial administration.</note>, so that no one can any longer cast such a report of impiety <note>The word "impiety" here refers to the unjust and cruel financial exploitation Julian's predecessors practiced.</note> against you. For these things, my brother Constantius <note>Emperor Constantius II, Julian’s predecessor and cousin, whom he refers to here by the formal imperial title of "brother."</note>, who is worthy of memory, was not so much to blame as were those men of barbarian mind and... soul—

...to the impious, who feasted at his table <note>Referring to the officials of Julian's predecessor, Constantius II, whom Julian blamed for oppressive taxation</note>. These I seized with my own hands and threw into a pit to be destroyed, so that not even a memory of their destruction might remain among us. 

But since I wished to grant you far greater benefits, I have urged my brother **Ioulos** <note>original: *Iulum*. This refers to Hillel II, the Jewish Patriarch or Nasi. Julian uses the term "brother" as a high diplomatic honor</note>, the patriarch worthy of all respect, to put an end to the tribute <note>The *aurum coronarium* or the *apostolē*, a tax paid by Diaspora Jews to the Patriarchate in Judea</note> said to be imposed upon you, and to ensure that in the future no one is permitted to demand such **taxes** from you. My purpose is that you might enjoy the greatest security and peace during my reign, and that with even greater zeal you may offer prayers for my empire to **God, the most high and the Creator** <note>original: *Deo optimo & opifici*. Julian often used philosophical or Neoplatonic language for the "Highest God" to find common ground with Jewish monotheism</note>, who saw fit to crown me with His own pure hands. 

For it is usually the case that those burdened by any anxiety find their minds constricted, and they cannot lift their hands in prayer to God with such confidence. But those who are entirely free from care rejoice in their whole spirit, and stretch out suppliant hands for the empire to the **Greatest God**, in whose power it lies to guide our **reign** toward...

<summary>Emperor Julian explains that he has abolished the "apostle-tax" by instructing the Patriarch Hillel II, seeking to ensure the Jewish community is free from financial burden so they may pray more effectively for his empire.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Julian, Hillel II, Patriarch, taxation, Jewish history, religious tolerance, prayers for the emperor, Creator God</keywords>
