<header>OF EMPEROR JULIAN.</header>

<margin>
Ammianus Marcellinus,
Book 23.
</margin>
...might be in the best possible state, just as we desire. This is what you ought especially to care for and strive toward: so that once I have successfully concluded the **Persian war** <note>Julian’s massive expedition against the Sassanid Empire in 363 AD, during which he was ultimately killed</note> according to my heart's desire, I may dwell in the holy city of **Jerusalem**—which you have for many years desired to see inhabited—now restored through my own labors. There, together with you, I shall give thanks to the **Most High God** <note>original: *Optimo Deo*. Julian continues to use language that appeals to both Jewish monotheism and his own Neoplatonic "highest principle."</note>

## JULIAN’S LAW CONCERNING
### doctors.

Experience itself demonstrates that the medical art is beneficial to mankind. Therefore, philosophers rightly proclaim that it **descended from heaven**. For the frailty of our nature and the ailments of health that occur daily are corrected by this art. Therefore, as the principle of equity demands, we—following the authority and word of previous kings <note>Referring to earlier Roman Emperors such as Antoninus Pius or Constantine, who also granted privileges to professionals</note>—command by our benevolence that you shall henceforth live free from **senatorial duties** <note>original: *Senatoriis muneribus*. In the late Roman Empire, being a member of the local senatorial class (curiales) involved heavy financial burdens and the collection of taxes; Julian is exempting doctors from these expensive civic obligations to ensure they focus on medicine</note>.

<summary>Emperor Julian concludes his promise to rebuild Jerusalem after his Persian campaign and issues a decree granting doctors immunity from burdensome civic and financial duties.</summary>

...most excellently, just as we intend. This is what you must do, so that I also, having settled the **Persian war** <note>original: τὸν τῶν Περσῶν πόλεμον. Julian’s ill-fated campaign against the Sassanid Empire in 363 AD, during which he was killed.</note>, may rebuild through my own labors and inhabit the holy city of **Jerusalem**—which for many years you have desired to see settled—and in it give glory with you to the **Higher Power** <note>original: τῷ κρείττονι. A term Julian used to refer to a supreme, transcendent divinity, designed to appeal to his Neoplatonic beliefs while remaining compatible with Jewish monotheism.</note>.

### Julian’s law concerning
### doctors.

The clear evidence of its usefulness testifies that the **science of medicine** is life-saving for mankind. For this reason, the sons of philosophers rightly proclaim that this science has **descended from heaven** <note>A reference to the divine origin of medicine, traditionally attributed to Apollo or Asclepius in the Greek tradition.</note>. For the frailty of our nature and the various diseases that occur are set right through it. Therefore, according to the reasoning of justice and decreeing in accordance with the kings of old <note>Referring to his predecessors, such as Constantine or Antoninus Pius, who had previously granted similar immunities.</note>, we command by our own benevolence that you shall spend the rest of your time undisturbed by **civic obligations** <note>original: βουλευτικῶν λειτουργημάτων. These were the mandatory, often expensive, public services or "liturgies" required of members of the municipal senates (decurions) in the late Roman Empire.</note>.

<header>EMPEROR JULIAN</header>

## Edict to the Alexandrians.

A man who has been driven out by so many imperial decrees, issued by so many emperors, ought surely to have waited for at least one imperial command before returning to his home. He should not have acted with such audacity and madness, insulting the laws as if they did not even exist. 

Indeed, even in the current decree we granted to the **Galileans** <note>original: Γαλιλαίοις. This was Julian’s preferred, somewhat derogatory name for Christians, intended to emphasize their origins as a provincial sect rather than a universal religion.</note> who were exiled by the late **Constantius** <note>Constantius II, Julian’s predecessor and cousin, who had frequently exiled Athanasius during the Arian controversies.</note>, we did not grant them a return to their churches, but only to their own homelands. 

But I learn that **Athanasius** <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373 AD), a central figure in early Christian theology and a fierce opponent of Arianism. He was exiled five times by various emperors.</note>, that most audacious man, puffed up by his usual arrogance, has seized what they call the **episcopal throne** <note>original: ἐπισκοπῆς θρόνου. The seat of the bishop's authority in Alexandria.</note>. This is no small annoyance to the **God-loving people of Alexandria** <note>original: τῷ θεοφιλεῖ τῶν Ἀλεξανδρέων δήμῳ. Julian refers here to the pagan citizens of Alexandria who remained loyal to the traditional Greek and Egyptian gods.</note>. 

Therefore, we publicly command him to depart from the city immediately, starting from the very day he receives this letter of **our Clemency** <note>original: ἡμετέρας πρᾳότητος. A formal imperial title used even when issuing stern orders.</note>. If he remains within the city, we warn him that he will face much greater and more severe punishments.

<meta>catchword: Julian</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian issues a stern edict ordering the Christian bishop Athanasius to leave Alexandria immediately, clarifying that previous amnesties allowed exiles to return home but not to resume their ecclesiastical offices.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Athanasius of Alexandria, Alexandria, Galileans, Christian exile, imperial edicts, religious conflict</keywords>

<header>EPISTLES.</header>

### EDICT TO THE ALEX-
### ANDRIANS.

<margin>Regarding the exile of Athanasius.</margin>
It was only right that he, who had been cast out by so many royal and imperial edicts, should have waited for at least one royal decree before returning to his home; he should not have relied on such singular audacity and madness to mock the laws as if they were entirely extinct and ruined. Indeed, even now, regarding the **Galileans** <note>original: "Galilæis" — Julian’s disparaging term for Christians, intended to frame the faith as a local Galilean cult rather than a legitimate religion.</note> who were exiled by **Constantius** <note>Constantius II, Julian's predecessor, who was involved in the Arian controversy and frequently exiled Athanasius.</note>, we granted them not a return to their churches, but only a return to their own respective homelands. Yet I hear that **Athanasius** <note>Athanasius of Alexandria, the influential bishop and defender of Nicene Christianity.</note>, a most audacious man puffed up by his usual arrogance, has again usurped the **episcopal seat** <note>original: "episcopatus sedem" — the bishop's throne or position of authority.</note>, as they call it. This is, in truth, no small displeasure to the people of Alexandria. Therefore, we command him to depart from the city on the very day he receives the letter of our **clemency** <note>original: "humanitatis nostræ literas" — a standard imperial phrase where the Emperor refers to his own administrative correspondence as an act of benevolence or "humanity."</note>; but if he remains in the city, we declare far greater and more severe punishments for him.

I arrived at **Litarbos** <note>A village in the region of Chalcis, located between Antioch and Beroea (modern Aleppo).</note> (which is a town in the district of Chalcis) and by chance I came upon a certain road which still bore the remains of the **Antiochian** <note>original: "Atchensiũ" — likely a scribal contraction or OCR error for *Antiochiensium*. This refers to the winter quarters used by the people or troops of Antioch.</note> winter quarters. Moreover, as I recall, one part of it was [a valley], while the other was a mountain; the whole way was certainly rugged. There, stones were scattered about as if thrown down at random, arranged with no skill, unlike the roads in other cities which are usually paved; here, in place of dust, many stones were set together as if in a wall.

Having finished this stretch of the journey, we barely reached the first lodging (which was at almost the **ninth hour** <note>Approximately 3:00 PM in the Roman system, which divided daylight into twelve hours.</note>) when a very large part of your **Senate** <note>original: "Senatus vestri" — Julian refers to the local municipal council (*curia*) of Antioch. Julian had a notoriously strained relationship with these elites.</note> arrived. What we discussed among ourselves there, you have perhaps already heard; and you shall learn of it from us eventually, if it pleases the **gods** <note>Julian was a devotee of the traditional Roman and Greek gods, often referred to as "the Apostate" for his rejection of Christianity.</note>. From Litarbos, I then came to **Beroea** <note>Modern-day Aleppo, Syria.</note>; and there, by a most clear omen, **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jovi" — The king of the gods; Julian often looked for divine signs to justify his military campaigns.</note> announced that all things would be joyful. Having stayed there for one day, I visited the citadel and to **Royal Jupiter**... <note>The text cuts off here as Julian describes his performance of traditional sacrifices at the Beroean acropolis.</note>

# Julian to Libanius the Sophist
->and Quaestor.<- <note>original: "καὶ Κοιάρῳ" — likely a scribal or OCR error for "Quaestor," a high-ranking Roman official, which Libanius was.</note>

I reached **Litarbae**; it is a village in the district of **Chalcis**. Along the way, I came across a road that still bore the remains of the **Antiochian** <note>original: "Antiochikon"</note> ground-shelters <note>original: "chamadiōn" — low-to-the-ground huts or temporary winter quarters used by soldiers or travelers.</note>. As I recall, the terrain there was part marsh and part mountain, and the whole way was quite narrow. In the marshy part, stones had been laid down, looking as if they had been thrown there at random, arranged with no professional skill—not at all like the way builders in populous cities are accustomed to construct the highways. In those cities, they build up the road using plenty of earth instead of mortar, and they set the stones close together as if they were building a wall.

When I had finally crossed this stretch and barely arrived at the first stopping point, it was almost the **ninth hour** <note>Around 3:00 PM.</note>. There, I received the majority of our **Senate** <note>The *curia* or municipal council of Antioch, with whom Julian had a famously difficult relationship.</note> inside the courtyard. You have perhaps already heard the details of what we discussed with one another; but you might also learn of it by hearing it from us directly, if the **gods** <note>original: "theoi"</note> so will.

From Litarbae, I traveled to **Beroea** <note>Modern-day Aleppo, Syria.</note>, and **Zeus** signaled that all things were favorable, showing a clear **sign from heaven** <term>diosēmia: a divine omen or weather sign, such as thunder or lightning, interpreted as a message from Zeus.</term>. Having remained there for a day, I saw the acropolis and performed a sacrifice to Zeus in a **royal manner**... <note>The text cuts off here, to be completed on the following page.</note>

...royally a white bull. <note>The text begins mid-sentence; Julian is likely describing a sacrifice he performed upon his arrival.</note> I spoke briefly to the **Council** <note>The municipal Senate of Antioch.</note> regarding piety toward the gods. ...to very few of those who came before. Why then do I now attempt to write about it, when your own account of it is so brilliantly composed? Would that such a thing [the fire at the temple] had never happened! 

Now, as for **Batnae** <note>A town in Osrhoene, between Antioch and the Euphrates.</note>, though this name is barbarian, the place itself is Greek. This is evident, first, because through the whole of the surrounding country the fragrance of incense rose from every side, and we saw beautiful sacrifices everywhere. 

This, although it pleased me greatly, nevertheless seemed "too hot" <note>original: *thermoteron*. Julian uses this term to describe religious fervor that is overly-eager or noisy, which he felt lacked the quiet dignity required for true piety.</note> and foreign to true piety toward the gods. For these rites should be performed far from the common path and in quiet, by those who have come for this very purpose, and not while carrying out the sacred and holy duties to the gods as if they were a secondary task. This matter will perhaps receive the proper attention shortly. 

I saw that **Batnae** was a lush plain, containing groves of young **cypress trees**. Among these, nothing was withered or decaying, but all flourished equally with foliage. The royal palace there was not at all luxurious; for it was made only of mud and wood, and possessed no variety of ornament. **Laertes** <note>In the *Odyssey*, Laertes is the father of Odysseus; his garden is a symbol of simple, rustic beauty and diligent cultivation.</note>, and in it there is a very small grove, full of **cypress trees**. And along the low wall, many such trees have been planted in a row, one after another. Then, in the middle, there are garden beds, and in these are vegetables and trees bearing all sorts of fruit.

What happened there? I offered sacrifice in the evening, and again at deep dawn, which is my habit every day. Since the omens of the sacrifice were favorable, we departed from the city, and the citizens came out to meet us. There, I was welcomed by a host, whom I had only just seen for the first time, but whom I have loved for a long time. 

As for the reason for this affection—I myself am well aware of it, but it is also a pleasure for me to state it clearly. For to always hear and speak about such men is like nectar to me. **Sopater** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Iamblichus who was eventually executed under Emperor Constantine.</note>, the pupil of the most divine **Iamblichus** <note>A hugely influential Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) whom Julian deeply admired as a spiritual and intellectual guide.</note>, has been this man's kinsman by marriage for some time. For to me, not to love those men <note>referring to the circle of Iamblichus</note> wholeheartedly seems like a grave injustice; indeed, nothing seems more base than to fail in that regard. 

In addition to this, there is an even greater reason. For this host had often welcomed both my **cousin** <note>original: *anepsion*. Likely referring to Emperor Constantius II.</note> and my **half-brother** <note>original: *homopatrion adelphon*. This refers to Gallus Caesar, Julian’s older half-brother.</note>, and having been treated with affection by them, as is natural, he often refused to abandon his [loyalty/piety]...

superior, resembling that of **Laertes** <note>In the *Odyssey*, Laertes is the father of Odysseus; his garden is a symbol of simple, rustic beauty and diligent cultivation.</note>: there was a very small grove in it, filled with **cypress trees**: next to the wall, many trees were planted in a row, one after another: in the middle of the courtyard, and among these, were vegetables and trees that bore fruit of every kind. What then happened there? I offered sacrifice in the evening, and on the following day at early dawn, which I have made a habit of doing diligently every single day: and since the sacrifices were favorable and auspicious, we hurried toward the city: there, the citizens came out to meet us, and a certain friend welcomed us into his home—a man whom we had then seen for almost the first time, but had loved for a long time before. 

I suspect the reason for this is known to you, yet it is no trouble for me to explain it: for to always hear and speak about those men is like nectar to me: **Sopater** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Iamblichus who was eventually executed under Emperor Constantine.</note>, a pupil of the most divine **Iamblichus** <note>A hugely influential Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) whom Julian deeply admired as a spiritual and intellectual guide.</note>, was this man's relative while he lived. To me, it seems the greatest of all crimes not to love everything pertaining to those men. 

But there is another even greater reason. Since this man had very often <margin>Full brother</margin> welcomed my **cousin** <note>original: *patruelem*</note> and my **full brother** <note>Julian likely refers to his half-brother Gallus here; the Latin *germanum* usually implies a brother of the same blood, though Julian's family relationships were often complicated by the political purges of the era.</note> with hospitality, and was earnestly urged by them, as was fitting, that by the gods...

The transport ships are being filled with grain, or rather with **hardtack** <note>original: *pane cocto*, literally "baked bread." This refers to bread baked twice to remove moisture, ensuring it would not spoil during the long march.</note> and **vinegar** <note>original: *aceto*. Discussions have arisen as to the length of this composition; consider this yourself. As for the many letters I have signed and the books that follow them, these matters are understood by me in the same way everywhere. Why is it necessary now to take the trouble of listing them all?

### Julian to Gregory, the Governor.

For me, even a small letter from you is enough to provide an occasion for great pleasure. Thus, having been exceedingly pleased by what you wrote, I return the same favor myself, judging that the friendships of companions should be measured not so much by the length of the letters as by the greatness of the goodwill.

<page-num>240</page-num>

# EMPEROR JULIAN

I received the map <note>original: *pinakion*. Literally a small tablet or board, here used to refer to the geographical map mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> sent by you with great pleasure for this very reason: it not only features better lettering than the previous ones, but you have also graced it with elegance by adding iambic verses. These are not like the verses of the poet of Cyrene <note>original: *Kyrnaios poiētēs*. This refers to Callimachus, the famous scholar-poet from Cyrene who wrote about the satirical traditions of iambic poetry.</note> which sing of the "fight with Bupalus" <note>Bupalus was a sculptor who was famously attacked in the biting, aggressive iambic satires of the poet Hipponax; Julian is saying Alypius’s poetry is not mean-spirited or satirical.</note>, but are rather like those which the fair Sappho <note>The celebrated Greek lyric poet, known for the beauty and grace of her hymns.</note> desires to harmonize with her hymns. The gift is of such a nature that it was perhaps fitting for you to give, and most pleasant for me to receive.

Regarding the administration of affairs, I am greatly pleased that you are eager to accomplish everything with both energy and gentleness. For to blend gentleness and moderation <note>original: *sōphrosynēn*. A core Greek virtue meaning temperance, self-control, or soundness of mind.</note> with courage and strength—and to employ the former toward the most reasonable men, while using the latter relentlessly toward the wicked for their correction—is an achievement of no small nature, nor of ordinary virtue, as I am convinced. We pray that you, holding fast to these character traits, may harmonize them both toward that one goal: the beautiful <note>original: *to kalon*. In Platonic and Stoic philosophy, this refers to the ultimate "Noble" or "Good" that transcends mere physical beauty.</note>. For the most learned of the ancients believed, not without reason, that this was the goal set before all the virtues. 

Farewell, and may you continue in happiness for the longest possible time.

->LETTERS<-

...was no less pleasing to me, the map <note>original: *tabella*. A tablet or map, referring to the geographical work Alypius sent to Julian.</note> sent by you; for in it there are not only descriptions better than the previous ones, but also the iambic verses with which you have adorned it. These are not indeed sounding of the "Bupalian" war <note>original: *boupalion*. This refers to the bitter, aggressive satires of the poet Hipponax against the sculptor Bupalus; Julian is complimenting Alypius's poetry for being elegant rather than attacking.</note>—that is, of a great struggle, as the poet of Cyrene <note>original: *Cyrenæus*. Referring to Callimachus, the famous scholar-poet of the Hellenistic period who was born in Cyrene.</note> says—but are such as the beautiful Sappho <note>The famous Greek lyric poet of Lesbos, known for the grace and beauty of her compositions.</note> is accustomed to weave into her hymns. In short, your gift is of such a kind that it is perhaps worthy of you, and certainly most pleasing to me.

Regarding the administration of the Republic <note>original: *Reip. [Reipublicæ]*. This refers to Alypius's duties as a high-ranking official in the Roman administration.</note>, I am pleased that you are eager to conduct all things with diligence and humanity. Indeed, to so temper gentleness and moderation with fortitude and strength—so that you use the former toward good men and apply the latter to the severe correction of the wicked—is, I believe, a matter of no mediocre talent and virtue. I desire that these goals be set before you and that they be referred to integrity <note>original: *honestatem*. The Roman concept of moral excellence, honor, and social duty.</note> alone. For it was not without cause that the wisest of the ancients believed this to be the ultimate peak of all the virtues. Farewell, and live happily for as long as possible, brother.

### JULIAN TO AETIUS, Bishop.

<margin>
Aetius the Bishop was the leader of the Eunomian heresy <note>A radical branch of Arianism that argued Christ was of a different nature than God the Father.</note>; therefore, having been cast out by Constantius, he is recalled by Julian. Sozomen <note>An early Christian church historian.</note>, Book 5, Chapter 5.
</margin>
To all others who, having been cast out from their homeland by the deceased Constantius because of the "folly of the Galileans" <note>original: *amentiam Galilæorum*. Julian’s standard derogatory term for Christians, whom he viewed as followers of a local Palestinian cult rather than a universal religion.</note>, I have granted a pardon for their exile. But you I not only free from that punishment, but even more, being mindful of our long-standing acquaintance and friendship, I urge you to come to us. You shall use a public carriage <note>original: *jumento publico*. Access to the *cursus publicus*, the state-run postal and transport system, was a high privilege granted by the Emperor.</note> as far as our court, and one additional companion.

### JULIAN TO LUCIAN the Sophist.

I am writing, and I expect a letter in return. But if I do you an injury by such frequent letters, you may return the same injury to me in turn. <note>A playful, intellectual exchange typical of Julian’s correspondence with fellow scholars or "sophists."</note>

### JULIAN TO DOSITHEUS.

I could hardly contain my tears—and deservedly so—when I heard your name called among us. For it stirred in me the memory of that noble and in every way admirable father of ours; if you wish to imitate him, you yourself will be happy, and you will make your life lovely just as he did. But if you are lazy, then you will bring grief to me, and...

## JULIAN TO AETIUS, Bishop. <note>The Greek text reads *Aeliō*, but this is a known transcription error for Aetius, a radical Arian leader whom Julian recalled from exile to stir up internal Christian debate.</note>

I am writing to you, and I expect to receive the same in return. If I do you an injury by writing so frequently, I beg to be "wronged" in return by the same treatment.

## JULIAN TO DOSITHEUS.

I was on the verge of tears—and indeed I rejoiced—at the sound of your name being spoken among us. For I was reminded of our noble and in every way wonderful father; if you will emulate him, you shall both be happy yourself and you will provide the world, as he did, with a reason to be proud. But if you are negligent, you will grieve me, and yourself even more, because...

<header>EMPEROR JULIAN</header>

...no benefit, you will blame. <note>This fragment likely concludes the previous letter to Dositheus: "...if it is of no benefit, you will blame me."</note>

## Julian to Iamblichus, the philosopher.

It was enough for Odysseus, when he was dispelling his son’s illusion of him, to say,
<margin>Odyssey, Book 16</margin> "I am no god; why do you liken me to the immortals?" <note>original: *Outoi tis theos eimi; ti m' athanatoisin eiskeis?* — Homer, *Odyssey* 16.187. Odysseus says this to Telemachus when the young man is dazzled by his father's sudden transformation.</note>
But for my part, I would not even claim to be a human being as long as I am not in the company of Iamblichus; and I confess that I am your lover <note>original: *erastēs*. In the Platonic tradition, this term refers to a deep intellectual and spiritual devotion or "erotic" longing for wisdom and beauty found in a teacher.</note>, just as that father <note>Odysseus.</note> was of Telemachus. For even if someone should call me unworthy, even so, he will not take away my longing.

I hear that many have become lovers of beautiful statues; they do not harm the art of the workmanship, but rather, through the passions they feel, they infuse the works with a kind of living pleasure. As for those ancient and wise men with whom you wish, in your playfulness, to rank me, I would say I am as far from them as I believe I am from participating in the very essence of Number <note>original: *arithmō*. In Pythagorean and Neoplatonic philosophy, "Number" is the divine principle that structures the universe. Julian is claiming he is far removed from this divine level of reality.</note>.

Yet you mention not only **Pythagoras** <note>The 6th-century BC philosopher who famously emphasized mathematics and reincarnation.</note>, nor **Democritus** <note>The philosopher known for the atomic theory of the universe.</note>, nor **Orpheus** <note>The mythical poet and musician; Julian views him as the "most ancient" source of Greek theology.</note> of old, but even the whole of the **Greek** world together—all those remembered for reaching the heights of philosophy—just as in the varied music of a lyre...

<meta>catchword: ph... (likely continuing to "voice" or "sound")</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian writes to the philosopher Iamblichus, using humble Homeric allusions to reject being compared to the great sages of old. He expresses a deep, "Platonic" devotion to Iamblichus, whom he views as his essential intellectual companion.</summary>

> I am not of the gods; why do you equate me with the immortals? <note>Homer, *Odyssey* 16.187. Odysseus says this to his son Telemachus to temper the boy's shock at his father's sudden, divinely-assisted transformation from a beggar into a hero.</note>

### UNTITLED LETTER
->On behalf of the Argives.<-

<margin>The Corinthians had made the Argives their taxpayers <note>original: *vectigales*. In the Roman provincial system, this meant Argos was required to pay a tribute or tax to Corinth, implying a subordinate status that Julian found unjust given Argos's history.</note>; Julian desires that they be freed through this letter.</margin>

There are many things that could be said concerning the city of **Argos**, should one wish to proclaim their ancient and modern achievements. For the glory of the deeds performed at **Troy** <note>The legendary Trojan War. Agamemnon, the leader of the Greeks, was the King of Mycenae/Argos.</note> must be attributed in greatest part to them, just as it was later attributed to the **Athenians** and **Spartans** <note>original: *Lacedæmoniis*. The formal name for the people of Sparta.</note>. For even if both those feats were carried out by all of Greece in common, nevertheless, just as the burden of labor and hardship belongs to the leaders, so too should the primary share of the praise. But these are matters of antiquity. Then, truly, there is the **Return of the Heraclidae** <note>The "Return of the descendants of Hercules," a founding myth used to explain the Doric settlement of the Peloponnese.</note>, the honor taken from the eldest, the colony then sent into **Macedonia**, and the perpetual defense and preservation of their free state against their **Spartan** neighbors...

### Untitled [Letter] on Behalf of the Argives.

One could say much on behalf of the city of **Argos**, wishing to extol its ancient and recent affairs. For the glory of the deeds at **Troy** <note>The legendary Trojan War. ...was a feat of no ordinary courage. Indeed, one might also justly assume that the great deeds achieved by the **Macedonians** against the **Persians** <note>Referring to the conquests of Alexander the Great.</note> rightfully belong to this city [Argos]. For Argos was the ancestral homeland of **Philip** and **Alexander**, those most illustrious of forefathers <note>The Argead dynasty of Macedon claimed direct descent from the Temenid kings of Argos.</note>. 

Furthermore, in later times, Argos did not so much fall to the **Romans** by conquest as she submitted through a treaty of alliance. Consequently, I believe she shared, just like the rest, in liberty and in the same rights that those in power have always granted to the cities throughout **Greece**. 

But now the **Corinthians**—for it is more fitting to speak of it this way—having had Argos attached to them by the ruling city <note>original: *basileuousēs poleōs*. Likely referring to the administrative reorganization under the Roman capital, either Rome or Constantinople.</note>, have been emboldened by malice to force Argos to pay them a contribution <note><term>synteleia</term>: a joint tax or financial contribution for public or religious services.</note>. They began this innovation <note><term>kainotomia</term>: in a legal context, this refers to an unauthorized or illegal change in long-standing tradition.</note>, so they say, only seven years ago. In doing so, they have shown no respect for the precedent of the contributions given to the people of **Delphi** or **Elis**, which those cities were granted in honor of the fact that they host the sacred games in their own lands. 

For as we know, there are four of the greatest and most illustrious games held throughout Greece: the **Eleans** host the **Olympics**, the **Delphians** host the **Pythian Games**, and the **Corinthians** host those at the **Isthmus**...

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian’s argument regarding the administrative dispute between Argos and Corinth. He is specifically addressing the financial burden of hosting the Panhellenic Games and why Argos should be exempt from paying additional tributes to Corinth, given that they already fund their own sacred festivals.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN</header>
<meta>catchword: bears</meta>

The Argives <note>The citizens of Argos.</note> organize and care for the Nemean games. What fairness is there, then, in allowing the immunity granted to others long ago to remain in place, while these people—who were once immune due to similar expenses, or perhaps were never even liable from the start—are now stripped of the privilege they had obtained?

Furthermore, the people of Elis and Delphi, on account of those noble Olympic and Pythian festivals, are usually required to contribute funds only once <note>referring to the four-year cycle of the games</note>; yet the Nemean games among the Argives occur twice in that time, just as the Isthmian games do among the Corinthians. Indeed, at this present time, two additional contests have been added for the Argives, so that a total of four festivals take place within every four-year period. 

By what right, then, are the former exempted for holding a single contest, while the latter, though they host four festivals at home, are still forced to pay for others—especially since these other events are neither ancient, nor are they part of the sacred traditions of Greece? For the Corinthians do not require vast sums of money for athletic or musical competitions, but rather for beast-hunts <term>venationes</term> <note>original: *venationes*. These were Roman-style spectacles involving the staged hunting of wild animals, often held in theaters or amphitheaters, which Julian contrasts with the traditional, more "refined" Greek athletic and musical contests.</note>, which are frequently put on in certain theaters,

<summary>Emperor Julian argues that Argos should maintain its tax immunity because it hosts four festivals every four years, whereas other cities with exemptions host fewer. He criticizes Corinth for demanding funds to pay for non-traditional beast-hunts rather than sacred Greek competitions.</summary>

->**OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN**<-

The Argives organize and care for the Nemean games. What justice is there, then, that the immunity <term>immunity</term> <note>original: *immunitatem*. An exemption from civic taxes or "liturgies," which were mandatory public services funded by the wealthy.</note> granted to those people long ago should remain, while these others—who were exempt long ago on account of similar expenses, or perhaps were never even liable from the start—should now be deprived of the privilege they once held?

Moreover, the people of Elis <note>The administrators of the Olympic Games.</note> and Delphi <note>The administrators of the Pythian Games.</note>, because of those noble Olympiads, are accustomed to contributing money only once <note>Julian refers to the four-year cycle of these major festivals.</note>; but the Nemean games among the Argives are held twice <note>Both the Nemean and Isthmian games occurred every two years, whereas the Olympics and Pythian games occurred every four.</note>, just as the Isthmian games are among the Corinthians. Indeed, at this time two additional contests have been added for these Argives, so that four games in total are held every four years.

Therefore, by what right are the former released after hosting a single contest, while the latter, having produced four games at home, are still compelled to contribute to others in addition? This is especially unfair since these [Corinthian festivals] are neither ancient nor traditional in Greece. For the Corinthians do not need a great deal of money for athletic or musical games, but for beast hunts <term>beast hunts</term> <note>original: *venationes*. Roman-style spectacles involving the hunting and slaughter of wild animals, which Julian considers a foreign, non-Greek expense.</note>, which are often displayed in certain theaters,

### LETTERS.

...[Corin]thians, while the Argives organize the festival of the Nemean games. How then is it reasonable for that immunity <term>immunity</term> <note>original: *ateleia*. An exemption from the "liturgies" or mandatory public-service taxes normally required of wealthy citizens.</note> granted long ago to remain for those people, while these others—who were exempted long ago because of similar expenses, or perhaps were never even liable from the start—should now be stripped of the privilege of which they were deemed worthy?

Furthermore, the people of Elis <note>The administrators of the Olympic Games.</note> and Delphi <note>The administrators of the Pythian Games.</note> are accustomed to providing their services once during the famous four-year cycle <note>original: *pentaeteridos*. Although the Greek word literally means "five-year period," it refers to a four-year cycle because the Greeks counted both the beginning and the ending years.</note>. Moreover, during this same period, two other contests are also established among the Argives, so that there are four games in all every four years.

A "liturgy" was a mandatory financial contribution for the hosting of festivals, maintaining ships, or other civic needs.</note> once, while the latter are subjected to contributing to the costs of others as well, on top of four times as many duties at home?

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian's letter regarding the city of Argos and its financial dispute with Corinth. <page-num>256</page-num>
# JULIAN THE EMPEROR

...[reminders], they buy bears and leopards. Since they themselves naturally bear these costs because of their wealth and the vastness of the expenditures—and clearly, as is likely, many other cities contribute to them for this purpose—they are buying the satisfaction of their own pride. 

But as for the Argives, since they are more lacking in funds and are being forced to serve a foreign spectacle <note>original: "ξενικῇ θέᾳ" (xenikē thea). Julian refers to the Roman-style gladiator games and beast hunts which were not part of the traditional Greek athletic festivals.</note> performed by others, how will they not suffer things that are both unjust and illegal, and unworthy of the ancient power and glory of their city? For they themselves are neighbors to them [the Corinthians], and it was fitting that they should be loved more; for if things were as they should be, "not even an ox would be lost, if it were not for the malice of neighbors." <note>Julian is quoting the ancient poet Hesiod, *Works and Days* 348: "Not even an ox would die but for a bad neighbor."</note> 

The Argives do not seem to be making these complaints against the Corinthians over a single little ox, but over many great expenditures for which they are not justly liable. And yet, one might reasonably add this point to the Corinthians: whether it seems better to them to follow the customs of **Old Greece**, or rather those which they seem to have recently [received] from the **Ruling City**... <term>Ruling City</term> <note>original: "βασιλευούσης" (basileuousēs). A reference to the imperial capital—Rome or Constantinople—and the Roman administrative laws that governed the provinces.</note>

<meta>The catchword "ληφε" indicates the beginning of the next page's first word, likely "προσείληφεν" (received/taken).</meta>

...they buy **bears and panthers**. They themselves easily bear these costs because of the magnitude of their wealth and expenditures. they received in the city. For if they wish to abide by the **sacred laws of the ancients**, it is no more fitting for the Argives to pay tribute to the Corinthians than for the Corinthians to pay tribute to the Argives. But if they embrace **modern laws**, and because they have been reduced to a **Roman colony** <note>Corinth was destroyed by Rome in 146 BCE and refounded as a Roman colony by Julius Caesar in 44 BCE, giving it a different legal status than older Greek cities like Argos.</note>, they contend for that reason that this city should be assigned to them, we will modestly beg them not to carry themselves with more arrogance than their fathers did. They should not overturn or change the noble institutions which their ancestors left to Greece—actions that would bring ruin to their neighboring citizens—especially while relying on a **recent judgment**. 

Indeed, they should count it as a much greater gain and a fruit of their own greed that they are being freed from the burden of litigating for the city of the Argives. For if anyone had summoned this case to be heard **outside of Greece** <note>Julian suggests that if the case were heard in a higher Roman court elsewhere, the Corinthians' local influence would vanish.</note>, the Corinthians certainly would have had less power, and the equity of the law, diligently sought out by many of those excellent patrons, would have appeared more clearly. It is likely that such a sentence was delivered by those who feared both the appointed judge and the dignity of the city.

# EMPEROR JULIAN #

260

...regarding the rights of the city and its advocates, if only you would be willing to listen and allow them to speak, you will understand the justice of the case from the beginning, and the matter will be judged entirely and correctly based on what is said. 

But why it is necessary to be persuaded by those who bring this embassy through us, I must set forth a few things beforehand. Diogenes and Lampsias <note>Diogenes and Lampsias: Two otherwise obscure figures whom Julian identifies as true philosophers. Julian often promoted the idea that a "true" philosopher should be active in civic life, unlike the "Cynics" he frequently criticized for their antisocial behavior.</note> practice philosophy as much as anyone else in our time; yet, they have avoided those parts of public life that are merely legalistic and profitable. Because they are always eager to aid their **fatherland** <note>original: τῇ πατρίδι. In this context, the "fatherland" refers to the specific city-state (Argos) rather than the Roman Empire as a whole.</note> to the best of their ability, whenever the city falls into great need, they then act as orators, participate in public affairs, serve on embassies, and spend eagerly from their own resources. 

By these deeds, they answer the common reproaches against philosophy and prove it a lie that those pursuing philosophy are useless to their cities. Their fatherland utilizes them for these purposes, and they endeavor to assist the cause of justice through us, and we, in turn, through you. For this alone remains as a source of help for those who are being wronged: to find a judge who is both willing and **able**—

->R iij<-

But regarding the rights of the city <note>The city in question is Argos, which was resisting being made a tributary to Corinth.</note> and its advocates, if you are willing to listen and allow them the opportunity to speak, you will hear the case from its very beginning, and the entire matter will be correctly decided based on their speech. Diogenes and Lamprias <note>Lamprias: The Latin text reads "Lamprias," though some manuscripts of Julian's letters refer to him as "Lampsias." These men were Neo-Platonist philosophers and friends of Julian.</note> practice philosophy as much as anyone else in these times. And yet, these men disdain the rewards and profits of public life; nevertheless, if their **fatherland** <note>original: *patria*. In the Roman Empire, this usually referred to one's local city-state.</note> requires their effort, they serve it to the best of their abilities. Thus, they plead cases, manage public affairs, undertake embassies, and spend their own money generously. Finally, through their actions and deeds, they refute the accusations against philosophy, proving that the common belief—that philosophers are useless to their fatherland—is false.

# EMPEROR JULIAN #

---

is willing and able: if either of these qualities is lacking—so that the judge is either deceived or acts in bad faith <note>original: *praevaricetur*. In a legal context, this refers to a judge or advocate who colludes with the opposition or fails in their duty.</note>—it is inevitable that fairness must perish. But when the judge is of the kind we desire, yet the right to speak is denied—especially as orators were then forbidden—the petitioners first ask that the opportunity to speak be granted to them. They ask that the incompetence <note>original: *inertiam*</note> of the man who then represented the city's cause should not result in such a great loss <note>original: *fraudi*. Here used in the legal sense of a disadvantage or damage to one's case.</note> for them in the future. 

Truly, it should not seem strange for a judgment to be called back for review. For it is beneficial for a people to overlook certain comforts and opportunities if it means they can secure peace and tranquility for the future. Since a man's life is confined within narrow bounds, it is a joy to live in peace and quiet for even a short time; however, it is a dreadful and perilous thing for a legal case to fail in the very sight of the forum and be passed down to one's descendants without resolution. Thus, it seems better to secure half of what is good by any means, rather than to perish while struggling for the whole. But as for those immortal cities <note>Julian refers to the Greek city-states as "immortal" because their history and institutions outlive individual citizens.</note>, unless someone judges with total integrity, all...

->### LETTERS ###<-

...[who is able to judge] well. <note>original: *dynamenou* (δυναμένου). This completes the word from the previous page, referring to the "ability" of a judge.</note> For if either of these qualities is missing—whether because the judge was deceived or because he betrayed his trust—justice must inevitably perish. 

But since we now have <term>judges</term> <note>original: *dikastōn* (δικαστῶν). In this context, these are imperial officials or arbitrators appointed to resolve the inter-city dispute.</note> of the kind we prayed for, and yet it is not possible for the parties to speak if they were not permitted to do so at the time, the petitioners ask that this right first be restored to them. They ask that the former <term>inaction</term> <note>original: *apragmosynēn* (ἀπραγμοσύνην). While this word often means "living a quiet life," here it implies a failure to act or a lack of diligence by the city's previous legal representative.</note> of the man who then represented the city's interests—who even hurried the judgment along—should not become the cause of such great harm to the city for all the ages to come. 

Nor should it be considered strange to <term>reopen a case</term> <note>original: *anadikon* (ἀνάδικον). A legal term for a trial that is conducted again or a judgment that is set aside for review.</note> for a new trial. For while it is beneficial for private citizens to overlook a few advantages and more profitable outcomes in order to purchase security for their future—since their lives are short, and it is a joy to enjoy peace even for a little while—it is a fearful thing to be ruined while being judged in the courts and to pass down an unresolved <term>lawsuit</term> <note>original: *dikēn* (δίκην)</note> to one's descendants. Thus, for an individual, it might be <term>better</term> <note>original: *kreitton* (κρεῖττον)</note> to risk accepting half of what is right by any means, rather than to perish while struggling for the whole. 

But as for cities, which are <term>immortal</term> <note>original: *athanatous* (ἀθανάτους). Julian argues that because a city exists as a legal entity forever, it cannot afford to accept "half-justice" or temporary settlements the way a mortal man might.</note>, unless someone, by judging justly, resolves their [rivalry with one another...]

# THE EMPEROR JULIAN #

...[unless he] frees them from their contention, it is absolutely necessary that they will hold an immortal ill-will. Hatred, too, grows powerful as it is strengthened by time. "So it has been said," as the <term>rhetoricians</term> <note>original: *rhētores* (ῥήτορες). Julian refers to the common wisdom of public speakers and legal experts.</note> say—it is not my own saying—but you must judge for yourself what is required.

->Emperor Julian to Porphyrius, greetings.<-

There was a very extensive and grand library belonging to <term>George</term> <note>George of Cappadocia, the Arian Christian bishop of Alexandria who was killed by a mob in 361 AD. Julian, though he disliked George, highly valued his collection of books.</note>, containing all sorts of philosophers and many commentators. Among these were not a few—indeed, many and various—books of the <term>Galileans</term> <note>original: *Galilaiōn* (Γαλιλαίων). Julian’s standard, derogatory name for Christians, intended to emphasize their origins as a provincial sect.</note>. Therefore, search out the entire library without exception and take care to send it to Antioch. 

Know that you yourself will be liable to the greatest penalty if you do not track them down with all diligence—including those who are in any way suspected of having stolen any of the books. You must try every kind of inquiry, all sorts of oaths, and even the <term>torture</term> <note>original: *basanō* (βασάνῳ). In Roman law, the testimony of enslaved people was often legally required to be taken under torture.</note> of the household slaves; if you are otherwise unable, you must compel them to bring everything forward into the open.

Julian is quoting a standard closing formula for a Greek or Roman legal oration.</note>, "now, decide on the case as will be just."

### THE EMPEROR JULIAN
### To Porphyrius, Greetings.

<margin>There was also a letter above regarding George’s library.</margin>
The library of George was truly great and extensive, filled with all kinds of philosophers and historians; however, there were also very many and diverse commentaries, especially regarding the doctrine of the <term>Galileans</term> <note>original: *Galilæorum*. This was Julian’s preferred derogatory term for Christians, used to emphasize their origins as a provincial Galilean sect rather than a legitimate Roman or Greek religion.</note>. For this reason, search out that entire library and send it to Antioch. Be aware that you yourself will suffer the most severe penalties unless you apply every diligence in this search. As for those suspected of having embezzled any books for any reason, you must—unless you can otherwise convict them—compel them to bring the books into the open by every means: by all arguments, by multiple oaths, and even more so by the <term>questioning of slaves</term> <note>original: *servorum quæstione*. In Roman legal proceedings, the "questioning" (*quaestio*) of enslaved persons almost always involved judicial torture to ensure the "truth" of their testimony.</note>.

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR

### TO AMERIUS.

<margin>He consoles Amerius regarding his wife's death, using the example of Darius.</margin> I read your letter not without tears, in which you write of your wife’s passing and describe the bitterness of this misfortune. For the matter is mournful in itself: that a young and chaste wife, who was a joy to her husband and the mother of such excellent children, was snatched away before her time—like a torch that was lit only to be extinguished shortly thereafter. Yet, it is especially distressing to me that this grief has fallen upon you. Indeed, our dear Amerius <note>Amerius (also known as Himerius) was a prominent Sophist and friend of Julian. This letter is a classic example of a *consolatio*, a formal literary greeting intended to comfort the bereaved.</note> was the most undeserving of all people to taste such trouble and sorrow, being a man of the finest eloquence and one most closely joined to us in friendship.

But truly, if I were writing to anyone else, I would have need of a much longer discourse. In such a letter, I would teach that this misfortune is a part of the human condition and that it is necessary to endure it; I would argue that in the heaviest mourning there is no greater consolation than necessity; and finally, I would apply all the remedies that seem fit for soothing grief, as if I were speaking to someone unskilled in such things. But now, since it seems a shameful thing...

# LETTERS.

## To Amerius.

I did not read the letter you wrote regarding the death of your wife without tears, as you described the overwhelming nature of your grief. For beyond the fact that the event itself is worthy of mourning—that a woman young, virtuous, and so pleasing to her husband, and furthermore the mother of noble children <note>original: παίδων ἱερῶν (paidōn hierōn), literally "sacred children," a term of high praise for offspring of noble character</note>, should be snatched away before her time like a lit torch that is extinguished shortly after—it seems to me no less grievous that this suffering has fallen specifically upon you. 

Our noble Amerius <note>Amerius, also known as Himerius, was a prominent philosopher and rhetorician of the 4th century and a member of Julian’s intellectual circle.</note> was of all people the least deserving to experience such pain, being a man gifted in speech and reason <note>original: λόγῳ χρηστὸς (logō chrēstos); this emphasizes his status as a man of culture and education.</note> and to us the most beloved of friends. However, if I were writing to anyone else on these matters, I would certainly have needed a longer discourse to explain that what has happened is merely part of the human condition, that it must be borne as a necessity, and that nothing is gained by excessive grieving. I would have applied all the remedies that seem fit for the consolation <note>original: παραμυθίαν (paramythian), the technical term for the Greco-Roman literary genre of the "Consolation."</note> of sorrow, as if I were teaching one who was ignorant of them. But since I consider it a shameful thing...

...to a man who knows how to offer admonition in clear words to those who are ignorant of how to be wise and well-educated. ->LETTERS.<-

...to a man who teaches others, using that manner of speech with which we are accustomed to soothe and instruct the unlearned. Come now, setting aside all other matters, I shall recount to you a story—or rather, a true discourse—of a certain wise man. It is perhaps not unheard of to you, but I believe it is unknown to many. By using this alone, as if it were a medicinal drug for joy <term>pharmakon: <note>original: pharmaco; a Greek-derived term for a medicinal potion, charm, or remedy.</note></term>, you will find a remedy for your distress no less effective than that cup which the Spartan woman <note>Helen of Troy, who, in Homer's *Odyssey*, used a drug called *nepenthe* to drown the sorrows of Telemachus as he grieved for his father.</note> is believed to have given to Telemachus in a similar situation.

<margin>Lucian relates a similar story in his *Life of Demonax*.</margin>

For they say that Democritus of Abdera <note>A famous Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BCE) known for his cheerfulness and for being a founder of the theory that the world is made of atoms.</note>, when he was in no way able to console Darius <note>Darius the Great, King of the Persian Empire (r. 522–486 BCE). This anecdote is a popular philosophical legend about the universality of death.</note>—whose most beautiful wife had passed from this life just a little while before—told the King that he would bring the deceased woman back to life if he were willing to provide the things necessary for the task. When the King ordered him to take whatever was needed, without any concern for the expense, and to fulfill his promises, the philosopher hesitated for a moment. He then said that while he had plenty of everything else, one thing was lacking, and he did not know how it could be obtained. However, since Darius [was the ruler] of all of Asia...

### OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN ###

...the other things required, he lacked one thing which, if he were King, he might perhaps easily obtain.

When the King asked what was so great that it was only permitted for a king to know it, they say Democritus replied that if he could inscribe upon the tomb of his wife the names of three people who had never known mourning, she would then finally return from the underworld; for it was certain that she would feel ashamed <note>Democritus's point is that if death were not a universal experience shared by everyone, it might be something to be "ashamed" of or to be reversed; but since everyone suffers, it is the natural law.</note> of her death through that habit. Then, when Darius hesitated, unable to find a single person to whom some tragedy had not occurred, Democritus laughed in his usual way and said, “And you, most foolish of all men, are you not ashamed to mourn as if you were the only one sharing such an evil, when you cannot find a single person among all those who have ever lived who has spent their life without some private sorrow?”

Indeed, it was appropriate for Darius to hear such things—a man who was a barbarian <note>original: *barbarum*; used here in the classical sense of being non-Greek and unrefined in philosophy.</note> and uneducated, and a slave to both pleasure and pain. But for you, who are both a Greek and a student of true doctrine, it is proper to find within yourself the power to command yourself. For it would be a shameful thing for reason <term>ratio: <note>The Latin translation of the Greek *logos*, referring to the rational faculty of the mind that governs the emotions.</note></term> not to be able to achieve what the mere passage of time will eventually accomplish.

...how he might perhaps find them. And when the King asked what so great a thing could be, which was permitted to be known to the King alone, they say Democritus replied that if he were to inscribe on his wife’s tomb the names of three people who had never known grief, she would immediately return to life, being moved by the law of mortality <note>original: *tō tēs teleutēs nomō dusōpoumenēn*. Democritus’s point is that if death were not a universal experience shared by everyone, it might be something to be "put to shame" or reversed; but since everyone suffers, it is the natural law.</note>.

When Darius was at a loss, and was unable to find a single man who had not experienced some painful suffering, Democritus laughed—as was his custom <note>Democritus was known in antiquity as the "Laughing Philosopher" because he laughed at the foolishness of human anxieties.</note>—and said: "Still, O most absurd of all men, do you mourn so boundlessly, as if you were the only one entangled in this pain? Can you not find even one among all who have ever lived who is without a share of private suffering?"

Indeed, Darius needed to hear such things, for he was a barbarian <note>original: *barbaros*—Julian uses this term in the classical sense to denote those who were not Greek and lacked philosophical refinement.</note> and uneducated, a slave to pleasure and passion. But for you, a Greek and a champion of true education <term>paideia: <note>The classical Greek system of education and cultivation of the mind, which Julian believed was the only path to a virtuous and disciplined life.</note></term>, it is proper to find the power to rule yourself within your own heart. For it would clearly be a disgrace to reason <note>original: *logismos*—the rational faculty of the mind.</note> if it could not achieve through wisdom what time eventually brings to all.

# THE EMPEROR JULIAN

It is well worth your while to learn how we have suddenly rejoiced in the manifestation of the gods, and how we have escaped that vast multitude of conspirators without killing anyone or seizing anyone's property, only placing under guard those we caught in the very act <note>Julian is boasting of a "bloodless" revolution as he moved through the Balkans, claiming divine protection allowed him to neutralize enemies without the typical massacres of Roman civil wars</note>. These matters should perhaps not be written but spoken of in person; yet I think you will be very glad to hear of them. We worship the gods openly, and the great mass of the army that followed me is devout. We sacrifice bulls in public. We have offered many hecatombs <note>original: *hekatombas*—traditionally a sacrifice of a hundred cattle, but here signifying a massive ritual offering</note> to the gods as tokens of gratitude. The gods command me to practice purity in all things to the best of my ability, and I obey them with zeal. They promise that the rewards of my labors will be great, provided we do not grow slothful. Evagrius has come to us.

<header>LETTERS.</header>

It is fitting for you to hear how we have suddenly felt the present help of the gods, and how we have escaped that vast multitude of conspirators <note>Julian refers to the supporters of his rival and cousin, the Emperor Constantius II, claiming he neutralized them without the usual civil war massacres</note> without anyone being killed or plundered, but only placing under guard those who were caught in the very act of their crime. We have given thanks to the gods with many hecatombs <note>original: *hecatombis*—a massive ritual offering, traditionally consisting of a hundred oxen</note>. The gods command me to live purely and chastely, as far as I am able. I truly obey them, and indeed with a willing mind. They promise great rewards for our labors, provided we do not act sluggishly. Evagrius has come to us.

...Iamblichus’s letters. Why should I mention here how greatly I rejoiced, or in what way I was affected by reading your letters? For if you have received my letter concerning this matter (which we gave to a certain messenger from among those coming to us from there, to be delivered to you), you certainly understand from my words how much pleasure I derived from them. 

Again, however, when the tutor of my children <note>original: *educator meorum liberorum*. It is notable that Julian mentions his children here, as historical records regarding his offspring are sparse.</note> was returning home, I sent another letter to you, in which I both thanked you for your previous letters and at the same time requested mutual correspondence from you for the future. 

Afterward, the illustrious man Sopater <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and distinguished pupil of Iamblichus; he was later executed under Emperor Constantine, but his son (also Sopater) remained in Julian's circle.</note> came to us as an envoy, and as soon as I recognized the man, I immediately leapt up and, embracing him, wept for joy, because I predicted nothing but you and your letters. 

When I received them, I kissed them and held them to my eyes, and gripped them firmly <note>original: *mordicusque tenui* — literally "held them with my teeth," an idiom meaning to hold onto something tenaciously or with great passion.</note>, as if fearing that while reading, the image of your face might gradually fly away from my eyes. 

And indeed I replied immediately, not to you alone, but also to his son, the most illustrious Sopater—

->### OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN<-

<page-num>286</page-num>

...[is] wiser, in my judgment, than he who dares too much. For just as those who dare to gaze constantly at the **Sun**, unless they are endowed with some more divine nature and look upon its rays like the true offspring of eagles, <note>Aristotle and other ancient naturalists recorded a belief that "true" or "noble" eagles could look directly into the sun without blinking; those that could not were cast out of the nest as illegitimate.</note> are unable to perceive those things which it is forbidden to see, and the more they strive, the more they show that they cannot attain it: so he who dares to write to you, the more he strives to dare, the more clearly he demonstrates that he ought to feel fear. 

But for you, **most illustrious man**, who have been appointed, so to speak, for the preservation of all **Greece**, <term>Greece|Græciam; here referring not just to the geography, but to "Hellenism"—the entire cultural, religious, and philosophical tradition of the Greek-speaking world which Julian sought to restore.</term> it was fitting both to send frequent letters to us and to restrain our laziness and inactivity as much as you could. 

For just as the **Sun** (to draw my comparison to you once again from the same **God**) <note>original: "ex eodem Deo"; Julian, a devotee of the Unconquered Sun (Helios-Mithras), frequently identifies the divine Sun as the source of all intellectual and physical light.</note>—just as, I say, the **Sun**, when it shines forth entirely pure and bright with its rays, performs its duty without considering whether that which meets its light is worthy: so it was necessary for you to pour out your gifts generously, like light, upon the whole of **Greece**, and not to cease because someone, hindered by fear or modesty toward you, does not dare to respond to you in writing.

->Neque<- <note>original: "Neither" or "And not"; this catchword indicates the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>Julian continues his letter to Iamblichus, using a solar metaphor to explain his hesitation to write to such a great philosopher. He argues that Iamblichus, like the sun, should continue to share his wisdom with all of Greece regardless of whether they are worthy or able to reply.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Emperor Julian's letter to the philosopher Iamblichus. Julian is concluding a metaphor from the previous page regarding the "true-born eagles" who can gaze at the sun, applying this to his own hesitation in writing to a man of Iamblichus’s intellectual stature.</meta>

<page-num>287</page-num>

<header>LETTERS.</header>

[is more] prudent than he who is daring. For just as those who venture to gaze continuously at the **shining flashes of the sun**—unless they are endowed with some divine nature and look upon its rays like the **true-born offspring of eagles** <note>A common ancient belief, cited by Aristotle, held that only "noble" or "legitimate" eagles could look directly into the sun without blinking; those that failed were cast out of the nest.</note>—are unable to behold what is not lawful to be seen, and the more they struggle, the more they reveal their inability to succeed: so too, he who ventures to write to you; however much he may wish to be bold, he only shows more clearly that he ought to feel fear. 

But for you, **noble sir**, who have been established as the **savior**, so to speak, of the whole **Greek world** <term>Greek world|Hellenism (Hellenikou); Julian uses this term to represent the totality of classical pagan culture, religion, and philosophy which he desperately wished to restore and protect.</term>, it was fitting both to write to us without stint and to restrain our hesitation as much as possible. 

For just as the **Sun**—so that my discourse may again take its imagery from the things of the **God** <note>Julian identifies Iamblichus with the qualities of the Sun-God (Helios), the central deity in Julian’s Neoplatonic theology.</note> toward you—just as the **Sun**, I say, when it shines in its entirety, purified by its rays, does not turn away those who approach its radiance, but performs its own proper work: so it was necessary for you to pour out the **beautiful gifts** from your own store, like light, upon the **Greek world**, and not to hesitate, even if someone—out of respect or fear toward you—is shy to offer a response in return.

<meta>catchword: -shamed [from the end of "δυσωπεῖται"].</meta>

<summary>In this concluding portion of a letter, the Emperor Julian uses the metaphor of the sun to encourage the philosopher Iamblichus to share his wisdom more freely. Julian argues that just as the sun shines on all without discrimination, Iamblichus should write to his followers regardless of their perceived unworthiness or hesitation.</summary>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the Emperor Julian's letter to the philosopher Iamblichus. Julian is developing a comparison between Iamblichus’s philosophical teaching and the healing arts of the gods, specifically Asclepius, while expressing his own humility as a correspondent.</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>

->**THE EMPEROR JULIAN**<-

...[unendingly]. For **Asclepius** <note>The god of medicine and healing.</note> does not heal for the sake of a reward when he grants health to mankind; rather, his own innate **benevolence**—much like a scholarly discipline—fills all things everywhere. This is exactly what you too must do, being as you are a **physician of noble souls**, and you must preserve the god’s command in all your dealings. 

You are like a **good archer**, who, even if he does not always strike down his opponent, continually trains his hand to hit the vital spot. Our goals, however, are not quite the same: mine is to obtain the right hand of friendship from you, while yours is to encounter the humble offerings I send. 

But as for me, even if I write ten thousand times, I am merely playing like the **Homeric children** <note>original: "Ὁμηρικοῖς παισὶ" (Homerikois paisi). This refers to a famous passage in the *Iliad* (15.362) where children build sandcastles on the shore and then kick them over for sport.</note>, who, along the seashore, cast aside whatever they have molded out of mud as soon as they have finished it. Yet even a **brief letter** from you is more powerful than any fertile stream. Indeed, I would rather possess a single letter from **Iamblichus** than all the **gold of Lydia** <note>Lydia, an ancient kingdom in Asia Minor, was legendary for its wealth, particularly under King Croesus.</note>.

If you care at all for those who love you—and there are such, unless I am mistaken—do not overlook us as if we were in constant want of your words, but continue to write...

For **Asclepius** <note>The Greek god of healing and medicine.</note> does not heal people with the hope of a reward; rather, he expresses his **benevolence** <note>original: "humanitatem"</note> to all people as if it were a kind of scholarly discipline. In classical mythology, Mercury's wand could both induce sleep and wake the dead; Julian asks Iamblichus to use his "wand" (his writing) to keep Julian’s mind alert and inspired.</note> not to induce sleep, but to move and awaken us, and in this regard especially to imitate that god.

->**TO THE SAME**<-
<note>Refers to Iamblichus.</note>

<margin>He sends certain orations to Iamblichus.</margin>
It was truly necessary, according to the **Delphic oracle** <note>A reference to the famous inscription at the Temple of Apollo: "Know Thyself" (*Gnothi Seauton*).</note>, for us to know ourselves, and not to make a din in the ears of so great a man; a man whom it is difficult even to look back upon when he is watching us, let alone to wish to return an equal response when he sets the harmony of all wisdom in motion. For anyone would yield to **Pan** <note>The god of the wild and shepherds, known for his flute-playing.</note> when he sings a sweet song, even if he were **Aristaeus** <note>A minor god, son of Apollo and Cyrene, credited with inventing beekeeping and other rustic arts; here he represents a talented but lesser musician.</note>; and when **Apollo** <note>The god of music, truth, and light.</note> sings to the lyre, everyone is silent, even if he possesses the musical skill of **Orpheus** <note>The legendary musician whose music could charm animals and move stones.</note>. But whoever [competes] with divine music...

->### LETTERS OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN###<-

<margin>Libanius <note>Likely referring to the famous orator Libanius, a close friend and correspondent of Julian.</note></margin>
I have labored upon that noble junction of the strait <note>original: *freti conjunctionem*. This likely refers to a literary description or a physical journey across a body of water, such as the Hellespont, which Julian had been working on.</note>. Truly, it is a small little gift if it were compared with yours; we are altogether giving back **bronze for gold** <note>A reference to Homer’s Iliad, where Glaucus foolishly exchanges his gold armor for the bronze armor of Diomedes; here used as a trope of intellectual humility.</note>. But nevertheless, we honor our own **Mercury** <note>The god of eloquence and messengers.</note> with what gift we are able to provide. **Theseus**, certainly, did not despise the table of **Hecale** <note>A legendary poor old woman who offered hospitality to the hero Theseus; she became a symbol of humble but sincere generosity.</note>, meager though it was; rather, yielding to necessity, he was content with that small amount. Even **Pan**, that protector of the pastures <term>Nomius: a title of Pan meaning "of the pastures," relating to his role as a patron of shepherds</term>, did not blush when he pressed the pipe of a young cowherd to his own lips. 

Therefore, may you also receive our book with a friendly spirit, and let it not weigh upon you to lend a "great ear" to such a small poem. Indeed, if there is anything of merit within it, then both the book and its author are fortunate, as being one to whom **Minerva** <note>The goddess of wisdom and the arts.</note> herself has given a testimony of praise. If, however, the work still requires the finishing touch <note>original: *extremam manum*. A Latin idiom for the final polish or completion of a literary work.</note> to be perfected, let it not be a burden to you to add what is missing. 

In this way, the God once stood by an archer who called upon him, and helped to guide the arrow; and in this way, when a string failed a lyre-player singing the **Orthian strain** <term>Orthian: a high-pitched, intense style of ancient Greek music</term>, **Apollo** himself responded in the form of a **cicada** <note>Referring to the legend of the musician Eunomus; when his lyre string snapped during a contest, a cicada landed on the instrument and chirped the missing note, allowing him to win.</note>.

<summary>Emperor Julian presents a literary work to his correspondent with extreme humility, using classical examples like Hecale and Pan to ask that his "meager" contribution be accepted and perfected by the master.</summary>

<page-num>295</page-num>

-># LETTERS #<-

We recently completed the yoking of the swelling strait <note>original: *οἴδιμον τοῦ πορθμοῦ ζῶξιν*. This likely refers to a literary description Julian wrote regarding a crossing of the Hellespont or a similar maritime feat</note>; and since this seems good, let us offer up the first-fruits. These are small things indeed compared to the great things we receive from you—truly we are giving back **bronze for gold** <note>A common classical idiom from Homer's *Iliad*, referring to an unequal exchange where one party gives something of far greater value than they receive</note>. Yet, we feast our **Hermes** <note>The god of eloquence and messages; Julian likely refers here to his own writing or the messenger bearing it</note> with such hospitality as we possess. 

Surely **Theseus** did not disdain the simple meal of **Hecale** <note>Hecale was a legendary elderly woman who provided a humble meal to the hero Theseus; she became a symbol of sincere, albeit poor, hospitality</note>; rather, it is sweet to be satisfied with small offerings in times of necessity. Even **Pan**, the protector of the pastures <term>Nomius: a title of the god Pan, meaning "of the pastures," relating to his role as a patron of shepherds</term>, did not think it beneath him to press the pipe of a young cowherd to his own lips. 

Therefore, may you also grant our discourse a favorable nod, and do not hesitate to lend a "great ear" to such a small melody. If there is anything of merit within it, then the work itself is fortunate, and its author likewise, having received the testimony of **Athena’s** vote <note>Referring to the goddess of wisdom, whose "vote" was famously the deciding factor in legal and intellectual judgments</note>. But if the work requires something further to reach its full completion, do not disdain to add the missing parts yourself. 

It is said that once the god stood beside an archer who called upon him and helped guide the arrow; so too, when a **cithara-player** <note>A performer on the kithara, an ancient stringed instrument similar to a lyre</note> was singing a high-pitched song and a string failed him, the **Pythian** <note>A title for Apollo, the god of music and prophecy</note> sounded the missing note through the song of a **cicada** <note>original: *τέττιγι*. Julian refers to the legend of Eunomus, whose broken lyre string was replaced by the chirping of a cicada, allowing him to win a musical contest through divine help</note>.

<sig>T iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Paul-</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian presents his writing as a humble offering, using classical myths of Theseus, Pan, and Apollo to argue that great minds should graciously accept and perfect even modest works of literature.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Epistles, Hecale, Pan, Apollo, Athena, Literary Humility, Classical Mythology, Greek Rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues the writings of the Emperor Julian, transitioning from the polite, literary exchange of the previous page toward a more formal and philosophical declaration regarding the nature of education and the ethics of teaching. This text is part of Julian’s famous (and controversial) "Rescript on Teachers" issued in 362 AD.</meta>

<page-num>296</page-num>

-># EMPEROR JULIAN #<-

We consider right education <gloss>to be</gloss> not that which consists in words, nor an expensive rhythm of the tongue; but rather a healthy disposition of an intelligent mind, and true opinions regarding both good and evil, and the beautiful and the shameful. 

Whoever, therefore, thinks one thing but teaches another to those who come to him, seems to fall as far short of true education <term>Paideia: the classical Greek system of education and cultivation that combined grammar, rhetoric, and moral philosophy</term> as he does from <gloss>being</gloss> an honorable man. And if the difference between his mind and his tongue is regarding small matters, he is bad only to that degree. But if someone thinks one thing in the most important matters, and yet teaches the opposite of what he thinks, how is this not the behavior of hucksters <note>original: *καπήλων* (kapēlōn). Julian uses this word for small-scale traders or tavern-keepers to imply that these teachers are "peddling" or "watering down" knowledge for profit without personal conviction</note>—and certainly not of honorable men, but of the most wicked? 

Such men especially teach things they consider most worthless, deceiving and baiting their students with praises, into whom they wish, I believe, to transfer their own evils. It is necessary, then, for all who profess to teach anything at all to <gloss>be</gloss> upright in character, and not to carry opinions in their souls that conflict with those they publicly profess. But it is much...

<sig>T iii</sig>
<meta>catchword: more</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian defines "right education" (Paideia) as a moral disposition rather than mere eloquence, arguing that teachers who do not believe in the values they teach are behaving like dishonest peddlers.</summary>
<keywords>Paideia, Julian the Apostate, ethics in education, Greek philosophy, rhetoric vs. truth, hypocrisy, Rescript on Teachers</keywords>
