<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian's decree regarding the ethics of education. While the previous page began his definition of "Paideia" (classical culture and education), this page deepens the moral requirement for teachers to believe in what they teach, specifically targeting Christian scholars who taught classical Greek literature while privately mocking the gods described within those texts. The Latin text here is a translation of Julian’s original Greek "Rescript on Teachers" (Letter 36).</meta>

<page-num>297</page-num>
->### LETTERS ###<-

<margin>
He forbids Christian professors from teaching the Greek authors.
</margin>
We consider right doctrine <note>original: *doctrinam*. In this context, it refers to the whole system of education and moral philosophy.</note> to be not the magnificent and exquisite sound of words or language, but the healthy disposition of a well-ordered mind, and true and certain judgments regarding good and evil, the honorable and the shameful. Therefore, whoever thinks one thing but teaches his students another, seems to be as far from true knowledge <term>Scientia: here used for the Greek "epistēmē," implying deep, foundational understanding</term> as he is from integrity. And if the disagreement between his tongue and his mind is regarding a small matter, in this very thing he is also dishonest, even if the magnitude of the crime does not exceed all bounds. But if indeed in the greatest matters he thinks one thing, and teaches against 

<margin>
An allegory regarding hucksters, who season spoiled and bad food so that they may sell it.
</margin>
what he thinks, is this not the life of hucksters <term>Caupones: innkeepers or small-scale retailers often stereotyped in antiquity as dishonest traders who watered down wine or disguised poor-quality goods</term>—I do not say good ones, but the most wicked? For they especially teach that which they consider most evil, deceiving and baiting with praises those with whom they wish, I believe, to exchange their own evils. Therefore, all who profess to teach anything at all must be of good character, and not bring forward new opinions that are at odds with common sense;

<note>Julian is arguing that because the Great Greek authors (Homer, Hesiod, etc.) believed the gods were the source of all learning, a Christian who denies those gods cannot honestly teach those authors. He views this intellectual "dishonesty" as a form of moral fraud.</note>

**Vocabulary:** 
teaching/doctrine, knowledge, integrity, hucksters/merchants, Christian professors, Greek authors

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian's famous "Rescript on Teachers." Julian (reigned 361–363 AD), the last pagan emperor, argues here that Christian teachers are hypocrites for teaching classical Greek literature while rejecting the gods who inspired those authors. He asserts that education (Paideia) is inseparable from the religious world of the ancients.</meta>

<page-num>298</page-num>
->### OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN ###<-

but above all, those who instruct youth in the writings of the ancients ought to be of such a character, whether they be *Rhetoricians* <term>Rhetors: teachers of public speaking and legal argumentation</term>, or *Grammarians* <term>Grammatici: teachers of literature, language, and the interpretation of poets</term>, and especially *Sophists* <term>Sophistae: in late antiquity, these were highly prestigious professors of eloquence who also claimed to teach moral and political wisdom</term>, who wish to be masters not only of words, but also of morals, and who contend that the philosophy regarding the administration of Public Affairs <note>original: *Rebusp.* (Republics/State). Julian emphasizes that education is a civic duty.</note> belongs to them. Whether this claim be true or not, I set aside for the present: I praise them for seeking such excellent learning; yet I would certainly praise them more if they did not lie, nor contradict themselves by thinking one thing while handing down another to their disciples. What? Did not *Homer*, *Hesiod*, *Demosthenes*, *Herodotus*, *Thucydides*, *Isocrates*, and *Lysias* hold the gods to be the leaders and authors of their learning? Did not some of them consider themselves sacred to *Mercury* <note>original: *Mercurio*, the Roman equivalent of Hermes, god of eloquence and interpretation.</note>, and others to the *Muses*? Therefore, it is absurd for those who explain the books of these authors to disparage the gods whom those authors worshipped. And yet, because I think it is absurd, I do not for that reason command them to change their beliefs for the sake of their students; rather, I give them the choice: either not to teach what they do not consider to be good, or, if they prefer to teach, let them teach...

<summary>Emperor Julian argues that teachers of classical literature must believe in the gods of the authors they study, presenting Christian educators with a choice: renounce their faith or stop teaching the classics.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, education, rhetoric, Sophists, paganism, Christian teachers, Homer, religious conflict, classical literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Emperor Julian's famous "Rescript on Teachers" (Epistle 36/61). The Greek text provided here actually overlaps significantly with the previous page's draft translation, suggesting a continuation of the argument where Julian demands intellectual honesty from educators. Julian (reigned 361–363 AD) is arguing that Christian teachers cannot in good conscience teach the classical pagan canon while simultaneously rejecting the gods that inspired those very works.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>
# EPISTLES. #

More than all others, I believe this is necessary for those who instruct the young through literature, acting as interpreters of ancient writings—whether they be **rhetoricians** <term>Rhetors: teachers of public speaking and legal argumentation</term>, **grammarians** <term>Grammatici: teachers of language, poetry, and literary analysis</term>, and especially the **sophists** <term>Sophists: elite professors of eloquence who claimed to teach moral and civic virtue</term>. For they desire, in addition to other matters, to be teachers not only of words but also of morals; and indeed, they claim that political philosophy belongs to their domain. Whether this claim is true or not, let us set it aside for now. While I praise them for aspiring to such noble professions, I would praise them even more if they did not lie, nor expose themselves as inconsistent by thinking one thing while teaching another to those who come to them. 

What then? For *Homer*, *Hesiod*, *Demosthenes*, *Herodotus*, *Thucydides*, *Isocrates*, and *Lysias*, the gods were the leaders of all education <term>Paideia: the traditional Greek system of culture and learning</term>. Did not some of them consider themselves priests of **Hermes** <note>original: Ἑρμοῦ (Hermou), the god of eloquence and interpretation</note>, and others of the **Muses**? I think it is absurd for those who explain the works of these authors to dishonor the gods who were honored by the authors themselves. I do not merely say that I think this is absurd; rather, I say they are leading the youth astray <note>original: μεταθεμένους (metathemenous). Julian suggests the teachers are fundamentally shifting the students' worldview in a deceptive way</note>. I offer them a choice: not to teach what they do not believe to be virtuous; but if they wish to teach, let them do so in deed...

<meta>catchword: first,</meta>

<summary>Emperor Julian argues that teachers of classical literature (Homer, Hesiod, etc.) who are Christians are hypocritical and dishonest because they profit from works inspired by the gods they reject. He demands that educators either embrace the religion of the authors they teach or stop teaching those subjects entirely.</summary>

-># OF EMPEROR JULIAN#<-
<meta>catchword: Βούλο- (I wish)</meta>

...first, and to persuade their students, that neither **Homer**, nor **Hesiod**, nor any of those whom they interpret—and whom they have condemned for impiety <note>original: ἀσέβειαν (asebeian)</note>, folly, and error regarding the gods—is actually such as they describe. Since these teachers are supported by what those ancient authors wrote and receive pay for it, if they were to admit they are most shamefully greedy for gain, they would certainly endure anything for the sake of a few **drachmas** <term>Drachma: a standard silver coin of ancient Greece</term>. 

Until now, there were many reasons for not frequenting the temples; and the fear hanging over men from every side gave an excuse for concealing the truest opinions regarding the gods. <note>Julian refers here to the previous Christian administrations—likely those of Constantine and Constantius II—under which practicing traditional Roman religion was often discouraged or suppressed.</note> But since the gods have granted us freedom, it seems to me absurd to teach people things which they do not believe to be good. 

But if they think those authors of whom they are interpreters are wise, and over whose works they sit like **prophets** <note>original: προφῆται (prophētai); Julian is using the term in its Greek sense of "one who speaks for a god" or interprets divine mysteries, comparing literature teachers to religious authorities.</note>, let them first emulate their

<margin>.</margin> honored beings, let them go to the churches of the **Galileans** <term>Galileans: Julian’s preferred derogatory term for Christians, intended to emphasize their origins as a local provincial sect rather than a universal religion.</term>
<margin>.</margin> to interpret **Matthew** and **Luke**; obeying whom, you legislate that men should abstain from sacred sacrifices.

# EMPEROR JULIAN

...I [wish] both your ears and your tongue (as you yourselves would say) to be **born again** <note>Julian is sarcastically using Christian terminology to suggest that Christians should be "reborn" into the truth of the classical Greek tradition.</note> in those matters, in which I pray I may always participate, along with all those who love me. Let this common law be established for teachers and instructors. 

For as for the youths who wish to attend [these schools], they are by no means prohibited. For it would be unjust to shut out from the best path those boys who are still ignorant of which way to turn, and to drive them back to their ancestral customs through fear. And although it were right to treat those who are, as it were, weak and insane even against their will and resistance; nevertheless, let all be permitted by us to be afflicted by that **disease** <note>original: "isto morbo." Julian frequently characterized Christianity as a mental illness or a plague on the Roman Empire.</note>. For one must teach the mindless, not punish them.

<header>LETTERS. 303</header>

I wish both your ears—as you yourselves would say—and your tongue to be **born again** <note>original: ἐξαναγεννηθῆναι. Julian is mockingly using the Christian theological term for baptismal rebirth to suggest a return to classical Greek culture.</note> into those matters in which I pray I may always participate, as well as anyone who thinks and acts as my friend. For the leaders and teachers, let this common law be established: any of the youths who wish to attend [these schools] is not prohibited. 

For it would not even be reasonable to shut out from the best path those children who are still ignorant of which way they should turn, nor to drive them back to their ancestral customs through fear and against their will. And yet, it would be just to heal these people against their will, just as one would treat those suffering from **delirium** <note>original: φρενιτίζοντας. Julian compares Christians to patients suffering from "phrenitis," an acute mental inflammation or frenzy.</note>; nevertheless, let pardon be granted to all for such a **disease** <note>original: νόσου (nosou). Julian frequently referred to Christianity as a "disease" or a "contagion" affecting the health of the Roman State.</note>. For I believe one ought to teach, and not punish, the foolish.

<header>EMPEROR JULIAN</header>

...once I had been restored, I received your letters on the same day that I first visited the bath. When I read them that evening, it is impossible to say how much I was encouraged, seeing your sincere and pure goodwill. I only wish I were worthy of it, so that I might not bring shame to your friendship. I read your letters immediately, although I was not very well able to do so; however, I set aside for the next day the letters that Antonius <note>Likely a mutual acquaintance or an official in Julian's circle.</note> was sending to Alexander. I have written this to you on the seventh day since then, as my health, by **God's providence** <note>original: "Dei providentia." Farewell to me, most desired and dearest brother.

## TO ZENO I.

<margin>Zeno the Physician, having been driven out for religious reasons, is recalled from exile.</margin>

Since many other things testify that you have reached the highest rank of the **medical art** <term>Medicæ artis: The practice of medicine, which in antiquity combined scientific observation with philosophy.</term>, and have joined to it a matching moderation in your character and life, a very great testimony has now been added: that even in your absence, you have turned all of **Alexandria** <note>The great Egyptian metropolis, famous for its medical school where Zeno taught.</note> toward you. You left such a sting in that city by your departure, just as a bee—

...[good]will <note>original: "εὐνοία" (eunoia). This word concludes the sentence from the previous page.</note>, your letters reached my hands on the very day I first went to the bath. Having read them that evening, you could not easily say how much I was strengthened, perceiving your sincere and pure goodwill; may I be worthy of it, so that I do not bring shame to your friendship. I read your letters immediately, although I was not very well able to do so; however, I set aside the letters from Antonius to Alexander for the next day. I am writing this to you on the seventh day from then, as my recovery is progressing according to plan through the **providence of God** <note>original: "θεοῦ προμήθειαν" (theou promētheian). Julian, though a pagan, often refers to "the Divine" or "God" in the singular when discussing philosophical concepts of fate or care.</note>. Farewell, my most longed-for and dearest brother.

->**TO ZENO**<-

Many other things testify that you have reached the first rank of the **medical art** <term>iatrikē technē: The ancient profession of medicine, which combined practical healing with the philosophical study of nature.</term>, and that your character, fairness, and the moderation of your life are in harmony with that art. But now the crowning piece of evidence has come: even by leaving, you turn the city of the **Alexandrians** <note>Alexandria in Egypt was the world's leading center for medical education in antiquity.</note> toward yourself. You have left such a sting in it, just like a bee—

...you have left behind. And naturally so; for it seems well said even by **Homer** <note>Homer was the primary authority on wisdom and literature for the Greeks; Julian frequently quotes him to support his arguments.</note>,

> "A healer is a man worth many others." <note>original: "Εἷς ἰητρὸς ἀνὴρ πολλῶν ἀντάξιος ἄλλων" (*Heis iētros anēr pollōn antaxios allōn*). This line from the *Iliad* (Book 11, line 514) was a common proverb used to honor doctors.</note>

But you are not a healer simply, but also a teacher to those who desire the **art** <term>technē: In this context, the craft or specialized knowledge of medicine.</term>; and just as healers are to the common people, so you are to those healers. Your exile is ended by the very excuse you gave, and quite brilliantly. For if you departed **Alexandria** <note>Alexandria in Egypt was the premier center for medical study and practice in the Roman Empire.</note> for the sake of farming, you did not depart justly, and you might most justly return. Return then, held in honor and possessing your former rank; and let a mutual favor be established for both of us: restoring **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Cyprus was a famous physician and professor of medicine. He had been expelled from Alexandria, likely due to local political or religious tensions, and Julian here uses his imperial authority to bring him back.</note> to the Alexandrians, and Alexandria to you.

---

Having received from my grandmother a small estate of four fields in **Bithynia** <note>A region in north-central Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey) where Julian spent much of his youth and held fond memories.</note>, I give it as a gift to your affection. It is too small for a man to think of as a great fortune or to make him wealthy. Yet it possesses a gift that is not altogether unpleasant, if [I describe] to you the details...

...you have left behind. And indeed, not without reason. For most excellently said **Homer** <note>Julian frequently cites Homer as the ultimate authority on both literature and moral philosophy.</note>,

<margin>A Medical Man.</margin>
*A healer is a man worth many others.* <note>original: "Vir medicus par est multorum millibus unus." This is a Latin rendering of a famous line from the Iliad (11.514), "Εἷς ἰητρὸς ἀνήρ..." which Julian likely quoted in Greek in the original letter.</note>

But you are not only a healer, but truly also a teacher to all those who are led by the study of that **art** <term>technē: In antiquity, medicine was considered a "craft" or "art" requiring specialized intellectual mastery.</term>; so that almost in the same way that healers are to the common people, you yourself are to those healers. This very reason frees you from your exile, and indeed magnificently. For if you departed **Alexandria** <note>Alexandria was the preeminent center for medical education and practice in the Roman world.</note> for the sake of farming, you did not depart rightly; yet you may return most justly. Return, therefore, with your former honor and dignity; and let there be gratitude for both of us—both because we have restored **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Cyprus, a famous physician who had been expelled from Alexandria during the religious and political turmoil prior to Julian's reign.</note> to the Alexandrians, and because we have restored Alexandria to Zeno.

---

# JULIAN THE EMPEROR

and a bathhouse that is not without charm, as well as a garden and trees. And when I had become a man...

<header>OF THE EMPEROR JULIAN</header>

Already, I was longing for that old way of life. I visited the place often, and our time spent together there was never without its share of pleasant conversation. There remains there a small memorial of my own labor in farming: a tiny vineyard, which produces a wine so fragrant and sweet that it has no need to wait for time or for **Dionysus** <note>The Greek god of wine and viticulture.</note> to grant it further beauty and grace. Even as the cluster hangs upon the vine, and certainly when it is pressed in the wine-vat, it gives off a scent of roses. As for the juice already in the jars, it is a "fragment of nectar," if one is to believe **Homer** <note>original: "νέκταρός ἐστιν ἀποῤῥὼξ Ὁμήρῳ." Julian quotes the Odyssey (9.359), where wine is described as a "chip" or "stream" of nectar.</note>. 

Why, then, is there not a great quantity of it, nor many acres planted with such vines? Perhaps I was not a particularly zealous farmer. But because the mixing-bowl of Dionysus is a sober one for me—diluted heavily with **Nymphs** <note>A poetic way of saying the wine is mixed with water; the Nymphs personified the fresh water of springs and streams.</note>—it was more than enough for myself and my friends. And since the number of such men is small, I was well provided for. 

Now, **dear friend** <note>original: "φίλη κεφαλὴ," literally "dear head," a classical Greek expression of deep affection for a person.</note>, I give this to you as a gift. It is small, to be sure. Yet it is a graceful thing to go from a friend to a friend, "from home to home," as the wise poet **Pindar** <note>The celebrated lyric poet of ancient Greece.</note> says. I have written this letter hurriedly by the light of a lamp; therefore, if there are any mistakes, do not judge them harshly...

<page-num>314</page-num>
<header>THE EMPEROR JULIAN</header>

...if any mistake has been made, do not investigate it severely, nor as an orator against a fellow orator. <note>Julian, who prized Greek culture and rhetoric, often argued that intellectuals and "men of letters" should treat one another with more grace than professional lawyers or politicians might.</note>

<margin>He remits a portion of the tax debts.</margin>

To a king who looks only for profit, your request would seem difficult; nor would such a ruler ever allow the public revenues <term>vectigalia<gloss>Regular state income derived from taxes, customs, and rents.</gloss></term> to suffer for the sake of a few. However, because it is our intention not to squeeze as much as possible from our subjects, but rather to benefit them as much as possible, we therefore forgive your debts. 

Yet, we do not forgive all of them; one part shall be granted to you, and another shall be set aside for the soldiers. This latter portion is also, in a sense, for your benefit, especially since it is spent for the sake of your own peace and security. Therefore, we remit to you whatever is owed from the past up to the third tribute <term>tributum<gloss>The primary land or poll tax paid by provincial subjects to the Roman state.</gloss></term>. Henceforth, however, you shall pay as custom requires. For the amount we have given you is quite substantial, and we must not neglect the accounts of the State <term>Res publica<gloss>Literally "the public affair"; the Roman Commonwealth or government.</gloss></term>. I have written to the prefects <note>High-ranking administrative officials responsible for the implementation of imperial law and tax collection.</note> regarding this matter, so that the benefit granted to you in words may be realized in fact.

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>->LETTERS.<-</header>

...investigate it, and not as an orator would investigate a fellow orator. <note>Julian frequently urged intellectuals to show mutual leniency, believing that those devoted to the "higher" pursuit of Greek literature should not behave like aggressive legal prosecutors.</note>

To a King who looks only toward profit, your request would have seemed difficult, and he would not have thought it right to harm the public prosperity <term>euporia<gloss>The general wealth or abundance of the state treasury.</gloss></term> by granting a private favor to certain individuals. But since I have made it my aim not to collect as much as possible from my subjects, but rather to be the cause of as much good as possible for them, this shall release you from your debts. 

Yet, these debts shall not be released in their entirety; rather, the matter shall be divided—one part for you, and the other for the needs of the soldiers. From this latter portion, you yourselves surely derive no small benefit: namely, peace and security. Therefore, up until the third indiction <term>epinemesis<gloss>A 15-year tax cycle; Julian is remitting arrears from a specific point in this administrative calendar.</gloss></term>, I remit to you everything that remains owed from the past. After this time, however, you shall contribute according to custom. For while that which is remitted is a sufficient favor <term>charis<gloss>A Greek term for "grace" or "favor," often used for imperial grants.</gloss></term> to you, I must not neglect the common interests. I have also written to the prefects <note>The Praetorian Prefects, the highest administrative officials in the Empire.</note> regarding this matter, so that this favor may proceed into actual effect for you.

<page-num>316</page-num>
<header># THE EMPEROR JULIAN <note>original: "JULIANI IMP."</note></header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

On all accounts, my physical health <note>original: "τὸ σῶμα," literally "the body." Julian often commented on his physical condition, which was frequently strained by his ascetic lifestyle and military campaigns.</note> is moderately well disposed; furthermore, my state of mind is also in good condition. I believe there is no better opening <term>prooimion<gloss>A formal introduction or preamble in classical rhetoric.</gloss></term> than this for a letter sent from one friend to another. 

What, then, is the purpose of this opening? It is for a request, I suppose. And what is that request? It is for a reciprocal exchange of letters <note>Julian placed immense value on "literary friendship," where the exchange of letters was seen as a way to maintain a shared intellectual and spiritual life.</note>. If these letters should indeed agree in spirit with my own, they would bring favorable news from you to us.

->The End of the Letters<-
->of Julian.<-

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header># LETTERS <note>original: "EPISTOLÆ"</note></header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

On all accounts, my body is in a moderate state of health; but indeed, my mind is well. No better introduction <term>prooimion<gloss>A formal preamble in classical rhetoric designed to win the reader's goodwill and establish the theme.</gloss></term> can exist for a letter sent from one friend to another. What, then, is contained in this introduction? A request, I suppose. And for what, exactly? For an exchange of letters—which, I hope, may carry a sentiment in harmony with my own, and report all things joyful and prosperous concerning you.

->THE END OF THE LETTERS<-
->OF JULIAN.<-

# ON JULIAN
## THE EMPEROR.

Great King of the Romans, and a mighty
->warrior,<- <note>original Greek: "aichmētes," literally a spearman or one who fights with a lance.</note>
Excelling much in deeds and in
->words;<-
Initiated into the lovely wisdom of Christ and the sacred rites,
He perished by turning away, and persecuted those same things in
->turn.<- <note>This refers to Julian's historical rejection of his Christian upbringing in favor of a revival of traditional Roman paganism, which earned him the epithet "the Apostate."</note>
He did not escape—so they say—the eyes of Zeus, <note>The author uses "Zeus" (the classical king of the gods) here as a poetic substitute for God or Divine Providence to maintain the classical dactylic meter of the poem.</note> whoever
held a ->sinful<- <note>original: "alitron," meaning one who has sinned against the gods or moral law; a transgressor.</note>
mind, and in every way he was revealed at the end.

->G. Martinus.<- <note>Likely George Martin (c. 1542–1582), a noted classical scholar and translator known for his work on the Douay-Rheims Bible.</note>

<gloss>Key terms from the Greek text: **Julian** (Ioulianon), **Emperor** (autokratora), **Romans** (Rhōmaiōn), **Christ** (Christou), **Martin** (Martinos), **Eyes** (ommata), **Sinful** (alitron).</gloss>

It serves as a moralizing conclusion to the collection of Julian's writings, framing his life as a cautionary tale of divine judgment.</summary>
